Relative and lax volutive categories
Tim Lüders

Abstract
In this paper we introduce the notion of a relative volutive (higher) category, specializing to the notion of a lax volutive (higher) category. Our primary motivation to study these objects is the following: while any rigid symmetric monoidal category admits a volutive structure, any closed symmetric monoidal category admits a lax volutive structure. We develop some of the basic theory of relative volutive categories and provide several equivalent formulations of lax volutive categories. We then study examples of interest, including categories of complete bornological vector spaces and modules over star-rings. We will also separately discuss unbounded operators between Hilbert spaces and Morita 2-categories, the latter of which in the context of fully closed symmetric monoidal 2-categories.



1
Introduction

Duality is a powerful tool in the construction of new (higher) volutive and dagger categories. A prominent conjecture
[
$$24
]
(proven for

n\leq 2
) asserts that any symmetric monoidal
n
-category
\mathcal{C}
with all duals and adjoints admits an
\operatorname{O(n)}
-volutive structure, which reduces on the maximal
subgroupoid
\mathcal{C}^{\times}
to the canonical
\operatorname{O(n)}
-action predicted by the cobordism hypothesis. On the other hand, many symmetric monoidal categories that appear
in practice only admit weaker notions of duality, such as the categories of chain complexes or bornological vector spaces. In this paper, we provide a first glance
at the appropriate generalizations of (higher) volutive structures that account for such weaker kinds of duality. In dimension one, we prove the following result,
see
Theorem

3.2.11
in the main text.

Theorem 1
.

Any closed symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
admits a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.

We summarize several good classes of examples (not necessarily constructed via
1
) of lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive
categories in the following.

Example 1
.

The following categories admit lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structures:

(i)

the category of (unbounded) chain complexes over a commutative ring,

(ii)

the category of complete (convex) bornological vector spaces,

(iii)

the full subcategory of Banach spaces,

(iv)

the category of modules over a
*
-ring (e.g. any commutative ring), and

(v)

any cartesian closed category.

By definition,
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories are
\operatorname{O(1)}
-homotopy fixed points with respect to the canonical non-trivial
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action on the (2,1)-category
of categories, assigning each category
\mathcal{C}
to its opposite
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
. Since it is not true that lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories are lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-homotopy
fixed points with respect to the aforementioned action, a different approach to formalizing these structures is required. Our second main result gives several
equivalent characterizations of lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories, see
Theorem

3.3.12
in the main text.

Theorem 2
.

The following notions are equivalent:

(i)

Lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories in the sense of
Definition

3.2.1
,

(ii)

representable symmetric pairings in the sense of
Definition

3.3.2
,

(iii)

volutive adjunctions in
\operatorname{Cat}
in the sense of
Definition

3.3.7
, and

(iv)

(2-contravariant)
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors
\operatorname{Adj}\to\operatorname{Cat}
.

The last characterization in
2
is our primary motivation to introduce the theory of
(higher) relative volutive categories, generalizing (higher) lax volutive categories. Built on the observation that both
\operatorname{Adj}
(the walking adjunction) and

\operatorname{Cat}
(the 2-category of categories) carry (2-contravariant)
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structures, the
\operatorname{O(1)}
-case presents as follows, see

Definition

3.1.1
in the main text.

Definition 1
.

A
relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
consists of a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category
\mathcal{B}
and a
2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\operatorname{Cat}
.

For
\mathcal{B}=\star
and
\mathcal{B}=\operatorname{Adj}
,
1
reduces to the notions of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive and lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories,
respectively; it may be instructive for the reader to think of the final 2-category
\star
as equivalent to the walking adjoint equivalence, so that a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive
category may be understood as an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category with lesser degrees of invertibility, which is consistent with the explicit descriptions we provide.

As the primary instance of higher relative and lax volutive categories, we introduce relative and lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories. In our discussion we put
a special emphasis on analogies with the relative and lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive case, respectively. On one hand we will establish that any closed symmetric monoidal
2-category with adjoints admits a lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive structure, given by the Serre morphism. On the other hand, our definition of a relative
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive
2-category as a (2-contravariant)
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-functor
\mathcal{B}\to 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
generalizes the one of
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive
2-categories for
\mathcal{B}=\star
and lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories for (a suitable localization of)
\mathcal{B}=\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]
, respectively, where
\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]
refers to a categorification
of the walking ambidextrous adjunction. Thus, our two main theorems (establishing that dualities give rise to lax volutive structures, and that lax volutive structures are special relative volutive
structures) regarding relative and lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories have corresponding analogues in the
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-case.

As an application of the theory of lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories, we recontextualize the dagger structure on the category of Hilbert spaces from the perspective
of the following table, containing motivating examples of categories and the structures (we discuss here) they support.

Dagger

\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive

lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive

closed symmetric monoidal

\operatorname{Hilb}

\operatorname{Ban}^{\operatorname{refl}}

\operatorname{Ban}

c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}

From left to right, the second line refers to the categories of Hilbert spaces, (reflexive) Banach spaces, and complete (convex) bornological vector spaces (each
with bounded linear maps as morphisms).

We also discuss lax volutive structures in the context of fully closed symmetric monoidal 2-categories. In particular, we will show that each fully closed
symmetric monoidal 2-category admits a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure which upon passing to the associated dagger 2-category yields a category enriched in
lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories. This suffices to pass locally to the categories of lax hermitian fixed points and lax isometries.
We will illustrate these constructions in detail at the example of Morita 2-categories. We prove the following result, see

Theorem

6.6.4
and
Corollary

6.6.7
in the main text.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category with (co)equalizers. Then,
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
is a symmetric monoidal 1-closed 2-category with duals for objects.

As a particular instance of
3
, the 2-category
\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
of complete (convex) bornological algebras is a fully
closed symmetric monoidal 2-categories. We will discuss lax hermitian fixed points in the context of this 2-category, and compare the results to more familiar
structures such as von Neumann algebras and hermitian/Hilbert modules.

Acknowledgements.
We thank Lukas Müller, Aleksandar Ivanov, and Ödül Tetik for many helpful discussions. We acknowledge financial support from the
Studienstiftung des deutschen Volkes e.V.

Conventions.
In this article, we will generically refer to bicategories as 2-categories, to pseudofunctors as 2-functors, etc., and similarly for 3-categories.
Whenever we refer to a 1-morphism in a 2-category as “invertible” or a “1-isomorphism”, we mean that it is an equivalence in the traditional sense. Given a
2-morphism
f\colon X\to Y
between invertible 1-morphisms, we will generically not distinguish in notation between
f
and the induced 2-morphism
Y^{-1}\to X^{-1}
.
We will call a (symmetric) monoidal (higher) category with all duals and adjoints
rigid
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we review essential parts of the theory of ordinary dagger category theory; the results presented here are essentially well-known to experts and
we do not claim originality for the covered material. We start by reviewing
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive and dagger categories, then once categorify the theory,
establish the respective 2-categories of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive and dagger categories, and then discuss the adjunction between them. We highlight the microcosmic
approach that a once-categorified theory of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive and dagger categories supplies us with.

2.1
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive and dagger categories

In this section, we review the notions of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive and dagger categories. We start with the former.

Definition 2.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category. An
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure
on
\mathcal{C}
consists of a functor
d\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
and a natural isomorphism

\eta\colon\operatorname{id}\to d^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d
satisfying
d(\eta_{a})=\eta_{d(a)}^{-1}
for all
a\in\mathcal{C}
.
A category together with an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure is called an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
.

Example 2.1.2
.

The category
\operatorname{Ban}^{\operatorname{refl}}
of reflexive Banach spaces admits an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure. The functor
d
assigns each Banach space
V
to its
dual space
V^{\prime}
and the natural isomorphism
\eta
has as its component at
V
the canonical isomorphism
V\to V^{\prime\prime}
from
V
into its bidual.

A good class of examples of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories comes from the following; we will prove a more general result later in this paper.

Proposition 2.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal category with duals. Then,
\mathcal{C}
admits an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.

Remark 2.1.4
.

An
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category is the same as an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-homotopy fixed point with respect to the canonical non-trivial
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action on the (2,1)-category

\operatorname{Cat}_{(2,1)}
of categories, functors, and natural isomorphisms. Explicitly, this
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action is given by the strictly involutive 2-functor assigning
each category
\mathcal{C}
to its opposite,
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
.

Remark 2.1.5
.

Any groupoid
\mathcal{G}
admits an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure with underlying functor being inversion of morphisms. More generally, any groupoid with
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action
defines an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category by suitably combining the
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action and inversion. Conversely, any
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure
(d,\eta)
on a
category
\mathcal{C}
induces an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action on the maximal subgroupoid
\mathcal{C}^{\times}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 2.1.6
.

A
(coherent) dagger category
is an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
together with a fully faithful subgroupoid

\mathcal{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}
such that the induced functor
\mathcal{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\times}
is essentially surjective.

Variant 2.1.7
.

A
(classical) dagger category
is a category
\mathcal{C}
together with a functor
d\colon C\to\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
satisfying
d^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
and

d(a)=a
for all objects
a\in\mathcal{C}
.

Example 2.1.8
.

The category of Hilbert spaces
\operatorname{Hilb}
admits a dagger structure given by the adjoint operator construction.

Remark 2.1.9
.

Coherent and classical dagger categories encode essentially the same information, with the main advantage of the former being that it generalizes arguably
more cleanly to higher dimensions.
To see the former claim, let
(\mathcal{C},d)
be a classical dagger category. We obtain an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
(\mathcal{C},d,\operatorname{id})
and we may choose the fully faitful
subgroupoid
(\mathcal{C}^{\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}\subseteq(\mathcal{C}^{\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}
, for which the induced functor in the definition of a coherent dagger category is essentially
surjective since any object
a
in
\mathcal{C}
admits the structure of an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-homotopy fixed point with respect to the induced
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action by
d
: namely,

(a,\operatorname{id}_{a})\in(\mathcal{C}^{\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}
.
Conversely, let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta,\mathcal{C}_{0})
be a coherent dagger category. Define a category
\tilde{C}
whose objects are the objects of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
and
whose morphisms
(a,\theta_{a})\to(b,\theta_{b})
are the morphisms
a\to b
in
\mathcal{C}
. The category
\tilde{C}
then carries a dagger structure given by sending
a morphism
X\colon(a,\theta_{a})\to(b,\theta_{b})
in
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
to the morphism
(b,\theta_{b})\to(a,\theta_{a})
defined by

\textstyle{b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{b}}
\textstyle{d(b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d(X)}
\textstyle{d(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{a}^{-1}}
\textstyle{a.}

(2.1)

The second claim is part of the ongoing program of defining higher dagger categories. In this paper, we will freely choose in our usage of coherent and classical
dagger categories depending on the problem at hand.

Remark 2.1.10
.

Any
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
gives rise to a dagger category, as discussed in
[
SS24
]
. We will describe a closely
related version of this construction in
Remark

5.1.3
.

Example 2.1.11
.

Applying
Remark

2.1.10
to the
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
\operatorname{Ban}^{\operatorname{refl}}
of reflexive Banach spaces
yields the dagger category of hermitian Banach spaces, which contains the dagger category of Hilbert spaces as a full subcategory.

Definition 2.1.12
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d)
be a (classical) dagger category. A morphism
X\colon a\to b
in
\mathcal{C}
is said to be an
isometry
if
d(X)\circ X=\operatorname{id}_{a}
and
said to be
unitary
if
d(X)=X^{-1}
.

2.2
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-categories and dagger categories

In this section we give specific once-categorifed versions of the notions of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive and dagger categories. We start with the former.

Definition 2.2.1
.

A
2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure
on a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
consists of a 2-functor
d\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{2\operatorname{op}}
, an invertible 2-transformation

\eta\colon\operatorname{id}\to d^{2\operatorname{op}}\circ d
, and an invertible modification
\tau\colon\operatorname{id}_{d^{\operatorname{op}}}\to(\operatorname{id}_{d^{\operatorname{op}}}\circ\eta^{2\operatorname{op}})\bullet(\eta\circ\operatorname{id}_{d^{\operatorname{op}}})

satisying

\begin{pmatrix}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 23.08092pt\raise-11.56303pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.97777pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.06131pt\raise-50.35008pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.93855pt\raise-55.44441pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 31.16667pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{d^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d\circ d^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-62.88885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 49.1562pt\raise-62.88885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{d^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.42357pt\raise-46.19443pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta\circ\operatorname{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.42357pt\raise-36.94443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{pmatrix}

(2.2)

where in the left diagram we have used the modification
\tau
and its 2-opposite, while in the right diagram we have only used the interchange law.
A 2-category together with a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure is called a
2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category
.

Our primary example of a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category in this paper is the following.

Example 2.2.2
.

The 2-category
(\operatorname{Cat},(-)^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})
of categories, functors, and natural transformations together with the 2-functor assigning each category
\mathcal{C}
to
its opposite
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
is a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category.

Remark 2.2.3
.

The 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on
\operatorname{Cat}
described in
Example

2.2.2
can be interpreted from the
perspective of the fully closed symmetric monoidal 2-category
\operatorname{Prof}
, into which
\operatorname{Cat}
embedds; we will adress this point in
Section

6.7
.

Remark 2.2.4
.

2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-categories are
\operatorname{O(1)}
-homotopy fixed points for the
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action on
2\operatorname{Cat}_{(3,1)}
given by the 3-functor
(-)^{2\operatorname{op}}

assigning each 2-category to its 2-opposite. While this motivates our terminology, it also suggests two variants of the notion of an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive
2-category: 1-contravariant ones (based on the 3-functor
(-)^{1\operatorname{op}}
) and (1,2)-contravariant ones (based on the 3-functor
(-)^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}
). Spelled out,
these amount to essentially the same data as presented in
Definition

2.2.1
with variances replaced appropriately.
The 1-contravariant version has been presented in
[
CL25
, Definition 2.40]
.

Remark 2.2.5
.

The underlying (2,1)-category of a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category inherits an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action, analogous to the one-dimensional case discussed
in
Remark

2.1.5
. Similarly, any
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action on a (2,1)-category
\mathcal{D}
defines a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive
structure.

Definition 2.2.6
.

A
(coherent) 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category
consists of a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta,\tau)
together with
a fully faithful subgroupoid
\mathcal{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}
such that the induced functor

\mathcal{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\times}
is essentially surjective.

Definition 2.2.7
.

A
(classical) 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category
consists of an identity-on-objects 2-functor
d\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{2\operatorname{op}}
and an
identity 1-morphism component invertible 2-transformation
\eta\colon\operatorname{id}\to d^{2\operatorname{op}}\circ d
satisying
\operatorname{id}_{d^{2\operatorname{op}}}=(\operatorname{id}_{d^{2\operatorname{op}}}\circ\eta^{2\operatorname{op}})\bullet(\eta\circ\operatorname{id}_{d^{2\operatorname{op}}})
.

Remark 2.2.8
.

Similar to our discussion in
Remark

2.2.4
, there are two other variances of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-categories.

Remark 2.2.9
.

Any (classical) 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
defines a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta,\operatorname{id})
.

The following is essentially the 2-contravariant analogue (in the sense of
Remark

2.2.4
) of the discussion in

[
CL25
, Section 2.3.3]
.

Construction 2.2.10
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},d,\eta,\tau)
be a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category. We obtain a (classical) 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category whose
underlying 2-category has as its objects those of
(\mathcal{B}^{\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}
and as its 1- and 2-morphisms those of
\mathcal{B}
.

Remark 2.2.11
.

Each Hom-category of a (classical) 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
carries an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure. Moreover, the composition
functors are
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive with respect to these
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structures. In other words, a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category may naturally
be interpreted as a category enriched over
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories.

2.3
The 2-categories of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive and dagger categories

In this section we briefly review the 2-categories of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive and dagger categories as well as the adjunction between them.

Remark 2.3.1
.

Applying
2.2.10
to the 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category
(\operatorname{Cat},(-)^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})

described in
Example

2.2.2
yields a (classical) 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category whose objects are

\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories, and whose 1- and 2-morphisms are functors and natural transformations, respectively.

An explicit proof of the following is given in
[
SS24
, Lemma 3.9]
.

Lemma 2.3.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
be
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories. Then,
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
admits an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure
(\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta})
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Remark

2.3.1
and
Remark

2.2.11
.
∎

Definition 2.3.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
be
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories. Applying
Remark

2.1.10

to the
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
(\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}),\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta})
yields a dagger category whose objects we call
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors

and whose morphisms are natural transformations. We call the isometries in this dagger category
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive natural transformations
.

Remark 2.3.4
.

This definition of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors and natural transformations matches the explicit one given e.g. in
[
SS24
]
.

Remark 2.3.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
be
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories and let
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
be an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor.
Then,
(F,\alpha)
induces a functor
F^{\operatorname{O(1)}}\colon(\mathcal{C}^{\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}
.

Definition 2.3.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta,\mathcal{C}_{0})
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\prime})
be (coherent) dagger categories. A
dagger functor

(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
is an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor for which the induced functor

F^{\operatorname{O(1)}}|_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}\colon\mathcal{C}_{0}\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}

(2.3)

factors through
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\times})^{\operatorname{O(1)}}
. A
dagger natural transformation
between dagger functors is an

\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive natural transformation.

Definition 2.3.7
.

We denote by
\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{O(1)}\text{-vol}}
the 2-category of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories,
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors, and
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive natural transformations.

Definition 2.3.8
.

We denote by
\operatorname{Cat}^{\dagger}
the 2-category of (coherent) dagger categories, dagger functors, and dagger natural transformations between them.

The following is
[
SS24
, Theorem 4.9]
.

Proposition 2.3.9
.

The construction of
Remark

2.1.10
extends to a functor
S_{\operatorname{O(1)}}\colon\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{O(1)}\text{-vol}}\to\operatorname{Cat}^{\dagger}

which is right adjoint to the forgetful functor
\operatorname{Cat}^{\dagger}\to\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{O(1)}\text{-vol}}
.

3
Relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories

In this section we introduce relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories. After setting up some of their general theory, we will provide a number of
good examples of these novel structures and provide several equivalent formulations of a particular special case, namely, lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories.

3.1
Relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories

In this section we introduce the notion of a relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category. Recall the 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category
(\operatorname{Cat},(-)^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})

described in
Example

2.2.2
. In the following, we will often only write
\operatorname{Cat}\equiv(\operatorname{Cat},(-)^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})
and leave
the 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure implicit.

Definition 3.1.1
.

A
relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
consists of a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category
\mathcal{B}
and a
2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\operatorname{Cat}
.

Example 3.1.2
.

The
canonical
relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category consists of the identity 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor
\operatorname{id}\colon\operatorname{Cat}\to\operatorname{Cat}
.

Example 3.1.3
.

An
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
is the same as a relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category whose domain is the final 2-category
\star
.

Denote by
\operatorname{Grpd}
the (2,1)-category of groupoids, functors, and natural isomorphisms.

Example 3.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a (2,1)-category and let
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\operatorname{Grpd}
be a functor. By
Remark

2.2.5
,

\mathcal{B}
and
\operatorname{Grpd}
carry canonical 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structures, with respect to which
F
admits a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.
Recalling that the inclusion
\operatorname{Grpd}\to\operatorname{Cat}
admits a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure as well, we obtain a relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category

\mathcal{B}\to\operatorname{Grpd}\to\operatorname{Cat}
.

Construction 3.1.5
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, we may construct a 2-category
\mathcal{C}[1]
which has two objects
0
and
1
and only one non-trivial Hom-category, namely

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}[1]}(0,1):=\mathcal{C}
. If the category
\mathcal{C}
is equiped with an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure
(d,\eta)
, then the 2-category
\mathcal{C}[1]

receives a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure: the 2-functor
d[1]\colon\mathcal{C}[1]\to\mathcal{C}[1]^{2\operatorname{op}}
is the identity on objects
and
d\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}[1]}(0,1)=\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}[1]}(0,1)^{\operatorname{op}}
on Hom-categories. The 2-transformation
\eta[1]\colon\operatorname{id}\to d^{2\operatorname{op}}\circ d

has identity 1-morphism components and as its 2-morphism components the components of
\eta^{-1}
. The 2-modification may be taken to be trivial,
due to the coherence condition of
\eta
. Hence,
(\mathcal{C}[1],d[1],\eta[1],\operatorname{id})
defines a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category.

Remark 3.1.6
.

The terminology
\mathcal{C}[1]
is motivated by the notation
[1]:=\{0\rightarrow 1\}
for the walking arrow. In particular, we have
\star[1]\cong[1]
where

\star
denotes the terminal category with a single object. On the other hand, we note that
\emptyset[1]\cong\{0,1\}
is the discrete (2-)category on
two objects, where
\emptyset
denotes the initial category with no objects.

3.1.5
supplies us with a large class of examples of 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-categories,
with respect to which we may consider relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories.

For the convenience of the reader, we spell out some of the data of a relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category in the following.

Remark 3.1.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},F)
be a relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category. First, the functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\operatorname{Cat}

assigns to each object
a\in\mathcal{B}
a category
\mathcal{C}_{a}
, to each 1-morphism
X\colon a\to b
a functor

F(X)\colon C_{a}\to C_{b}
, and to each 2-morphism
f\colon X\to Y
a natural transformation

F(f)\colon F(X)\to F(Y)
. Second, the first piece of data of the 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive
structure is an invertible 2-transformation
\alpha\colon F^{2\operatorname{op}}\circ d\cong(-)^{1\operatorname{op}}\circ F
whose 1-morphism
component at
a\in\mathcal{B}
is given by an equivalence of categories
\alpha_{a}\colon C_{d(a)}\cong C_{a}^{\operatorname{op}}
.
Third, the second piece of data of the 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure is an invertible modification
fitting into the diagram

(3.1)

whose component at
a\in\mathcal{B}
is given by a natural isomorphism fitting into the diagram

3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.858pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 33.35007pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{d^{2}(a)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.858pt\raise-48.3561pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.24501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{F(\eta_{a}^{-1})}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.858pt\raise-55.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-62.66663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 40.50793pt\raise-62.66663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{a}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(3.2)

which itself satisfies a coherence condition.

Remark 3.1.8
.

We may think of relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories as objects in the slice 3-category
2\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{O(1)}_{2}\text{-vol}}_{/\operatorname{Cat}}
. Here the subscript
2
refers
to the fact that we consider 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-categories (a fact that we will leave implicit in the following). In the following we wish to
describe the (higher) morphisms in the 3-category

2\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{O(1)}_{2}\text{-vol}}_{/\operatorname{Cat}}
more explicitly. First, a
functor
of relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories
(\mathcal{B},F)
and
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},F^{\prime})
consists of
an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-functor
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
together with an invertible
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-transformation
\alpha\colon F\to F^{\prime}\circ G
. Second,
a
transformation
of relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors
(G_{1},\alpha_{1})
and
(G_{2},\alpha_{2})
consists of an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-transformation
\beta\colon G_{1}\to G_{2}

and an invertible
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive modification
\xi
satisfying the evident compatibility condition with
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
. Finally, a
modification
of
relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive transformations
(\beta^{a},\xi^{a})
and
(\beta^{b},\xi^{b})
consists of an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive modification
\omega\colon\beta^{a}\to\beta^{b}

satisfying a compatibility condition with
\xi^{a},\xi^{b}
.

Remark 3.1.9
.

If we wish to consider relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories with a fixed domain
\mathcal{B}
, we may also consider the 2-category

\operatorname{Hom}_{2\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{O(1)}_{2}\text{-vol}}}(\mathcal{B},\operatorname{Cat})
.

3.2
Lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories

In this section we introduce lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories and prove that every closed symmetric monoidal category admits a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.

Definition 3.2.1
.

A
lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure
on a category
\mathcal{C}
consists of a functor
d\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}

and a natural transformation
\eta\colon\operatorname{id}\to d^{2}
satisfying
d(\eta_{a})\circ\eta_{d(a)}=\operatorname{id}_{d(a)}
for all
a\in\mathcal{C}
.
A category
\mathcal{C}
together with a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure
(d,\eta)
is called a
lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
.

Remark 3.2.2
.

Lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories have appeared in the literature on hermitian algebraic K-theory before, under the name
category with duality
, see e.g.

[
Sch10
, Definition 3.1]
. However, adopting this terminology would be rather confusing in the upcoming discussion.

Remark 3.2.3
.

Lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories are
not
lax homotopy fixed points with respect to the canonical non-trivial
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action on the (2,1)-category

\operatorname{Cat}_{(2,1)}
. The latter are simply
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories, as the invertibility of the natural transformation
\eta
automatically
implies the invertiblity of
d
.

Remark 3.2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and let
(d,\eta)
be a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on
\mathcal{C}
. Let
k
be a non-negative integer. We obtain a new lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive
structure whose functor is
\overline{d}_{k}:=(d\circ d^{\operatorname{op}})^{k}\circ d
and whose natural transformation is

\overline{\eta}_{k}:=\eta^{k+1}\colon\operatorname{id}\to(d\circ d^{\operatorname{op}})^{2k+2}\cong(d^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d)^{k}\circ d^{\operatorname{op}}\circ(d\circ d^{\operatorname{op}})^{k}\circ d=\overline{d}^{\operatorname{op}}_{k}\circ\overline{d}_{k}

(3.3)

which satisfies the coherence condition of a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure since
\eta
does. If we assume that
(d,\eta)
is an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure,

(\overline{d}_{k},\overline{\eta}_{k})
is an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure as well and equivalent to
(d,\eta)
.

Example 3.2.5
.

Let
\operatorname{Ban}
denote the category of Banach spaces and continuous/bounded linear maps. We construct a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on
\operatorname{Ban}
. First, we define
a functor
(-)^{*}\colon\operatorname{Ban}\to\operatorname{Ban}^{\operatorname{op}}
by assigning each Banach space
V
to its dual
V^{*}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ban}}(V,\mathbb{C})
and each continuous linear map

f\colon V\to W
to the transpose
f^{*}\colon W^{*}\to V^{*},\phi\mapsto\phi\circ f
. Second, we define a natural transformation
\iota\colon\operatorname{id}\to(-)^{**}

whose component at
V\in\operatorname{Ban}
is given by the continuous linear map
\iota_{V}\colon V\to V^{**},\iota_{V}(v)(\phi)=\phi(v)
. To see that
\iota
is indeed
a natural transformation, it suffices to note that

((\iota_{W}\circ f)(v))(\phi)=\iota_{W}(f(v))(\phi)=\phi(f(w))=f^{*}(\phi)(v)=((f^{**}\circ\iota_{V})(v))(\phi).

(3.4)

Lastly, we check that
\iota_{V}^{*}\circ\iota_{V^{*}}=\operatorname{id}_{V^{*}}
. Indeed, we have

((\iota_{V}^{*}\circ\iota_{V^{*}})(\phi))(v)=(\iota_{V}^{*}(\iota_{V^{*}}(\phi)))(v)=\iota_{V^{*}}(\phi)(\iota_{V}(v))=\iota_{V}(v)(\phi)=\phi(v)

(3.5)

so that
\iota_{V}^{*}\circ\iota_{V^{*}}=\operatorname{id}_{V^{*}}
holds. Hence,
\operatorname{Ban}
supports a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.

Example 3.2.6
.

Any
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category is a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category.

In the converse direction, we have the following.

Construction 3.2.7
.

Any lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
gives rise to an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category which can be described as follows:
the underlying category is the full subcategory
\hat{\mathcal{C}}\subset\mathcal{C}
whose objects
a
are those for which

\eta\colon a\to d^{2}(a)
is an isomorphism, and the
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure is given by the restriction of
(d,\eta)
to
\hat{C}
.

Example 3.2.8
.

The category of reflexive Banach spaces discussed in
Example

2.1.2
inherits its
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure
from the lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
\operatorname{Ban}
discussed in
Example

3.2.5
via
3.2.7
.

Definition 3.2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a monoidal category. We say that
\mathcal{C}
is
right closed
if and only if for every pair of objects
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
there exists another object

b^{a}
and a map
\operatorname{ev}_{a}^{b}\colon b^{a}\otimes a\to b
with the following universal property: for every
c\in\mathcal{C}
, the induced map

\psi\colon\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(c,b^{a})\to\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(c\otimes a,b)
is a bijection which is natural in
b
and
c
. Put differently, the construction
b\mapsto b^{a}

determines a right adjoint to the functor
c\mapsto c\otimes a
for every
a\in\mathcal{C}
.

Similarly, we will say that a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
is
left closed
if the functor
a\otimes(-)\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

admits a right adjoint for every
a\in\mathcal{C}
. For symmetric/braided monoidal categorues being right closed is equivalent to being left closed, so that we will
simply refer to a category as being
closed
in this case.

Construction 3.2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a right closed monoidal category in the sense of
Definition

3.2.9
. In the following we will use the notation

\operatorname{ev}_{a}:=\operatorname{ev}_{a}^{1}
for
a\in\mathcal{C}
and
1
for the monoidal unit. We claim that the construction
a\mapsto 1^{a}
extends to a functor

1^{(-)}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
. Indeed, for a morphism
X\colon a\to b
, we set

1^{X}:=\psi^{-1}\left(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 15.15126pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.15126pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1^{b}\otimes a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.1349pt\raise 5.84723pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.01389pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\otimes X}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.15126pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.15126pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1^{b}\otimes b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.74773pt\raise 5.03749pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.9764pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{b}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 92.45955pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 92.45955pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right)\in\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(1^{b},1^{a})

(3.6)

and note first that
1^{\operatorname{id}_{a}}=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{a})=\operatorname{id}_{1^{a}}
. Using the naturality of
\psi
we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{ev}_{a}\circ(1^{X}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{a})&=\psi(\operatorname{id}_{1^{a}})\circ(1^{X}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{a})\\
&=\psi(\operatorname{id}_{1^{a}}\circ 1^{X})\\
&=\psi(1^{X})\\
&=\psi\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{b}\circ(\operatorname{id}_{1^{b}}\otimes X))\\
&=\operatorname{ev}_{b}\circ(\operatorname{id}_{1^{b}}\otimes X),\end{split}

(3.7)

implying the commutativity of the diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(3.8)

for
X\colon a\to b
and
Y\colon b\to c
morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
and hence functoriality.

Assume now in addition that
\mathcal{C}
is a (right) closed
braided
monoidal category. We obtain for each
a\in\mathcal{C}
a canonical map
\eta_{a}\colon a\mapsto 1^{1^{a}}
defined as

\eta_{a}:=\psi^{-1}\left(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.67284pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.67284pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{a\otimes 1^{a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.32512pt\raise 6.5097pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.3514pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta_{a,1^{a}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.67284pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.67284pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1^{a}\otimes a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.73178pt\raise 5.00694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{a}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 95.79634pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 95.79634pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right).

(3.9)

We claim that
\eta\colon\operatorname{id}\to 1^{1^{(-)}}
is a natural transformation. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}1^{1^{X}}\circ\eta_{a}&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{1^{a}}\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes 1^{X}))\circ\eta_{a}\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{1^{a}}\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes 1^{X})\circ(\eta_{a}\otimes\operatorname{id}))\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{1^{a}}\circ(\eta_{a}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes 1^{X}))\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{a}\circ\beta\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes 1^{X}))\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{a}\circ(1^{X}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\beta)\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{a}\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes X)\circ\beta)\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{a}\circ\beta\circ(X\otimes\operatorname{id}))\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{a}\circ\beta)\circ X=\eta_{b}\circ X.\end{split}

(3.10)

Assume now in addition that
\mathcal{C}
is a closed
symmetric
monoidal category.
We claim that
1^{\eta_{a}}\circ\eta_{1^{a}}=\operatorname{id}_{1^{a}}
is satisfied for every
a\in\mathcal{C}
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}1^{\eta_{a}}\circ\eta_{1^{a}}&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{1^{1^{a}}}\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes\eta_{a}))\circ\eta_{1^{a}}\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{1^{1^{a}}}\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes\eta_{a})\circ(\eta_{1^{a}}\otimes\operatorname{id}))\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{1^{1^{a}}}\circ(\eta_{1^{a}}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes\eta_{a}))\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{1^{a}}\circ\beta\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes\eta_{a}))\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{1^{a}}\circ(\eta_{a}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\beta)\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{a}\circ\beta\circ\beta)\\
&=\psi^{-1}(\operatorname{ev}_{a})=\operatorname{id}_{1^{a}}.\end{split}

(3.11)

Hence, every closed symmetric monoidal category admits a functor
1^{(-)}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
and a natural transformation
\eta\colon\operatorname{id}\to 1^{1^{(-)}}
satisfying
1^{\eta_{a}}\circ\eta_{1^{a}}=\operatorname{id}_{1^{a}}
for every
a\in\mathcal{C}
.

We record our findings in the following.

Theorem 3.2.11
.

Every closed symmetric monoidal category admits a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure in the sense of
Definition

3.2.1
.

Remark 3.2.12
.

More generally, any symmetric monoidal involution on a closed symmetric monoidal category gives rise to a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure;

Theorem

3.2.11
is then recovered as the special case of the identity involution.

Remark 3.2.13
.

We have explained in
Remark

3.2.3
that lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories are
not
lax homotopy fixed
points. Instead, the terminology is motivated by the following observation: the functor
1^{(-)}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}_{\otimes^{\operatorname{op}}}
is oplax monoidal.
Here,
\otimes^{\operatorname{op}}
refers to the opposite monoidal structure, i.e.
a\otimes^{\operatorname{op}}b:=b\otimes a
. To see the claim, we construct oplax
1
1

1

While
the data we supply here resembles lax monoidality data, we recall that the target of our functor is
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
, so that this data is reversed.
monoidality
data for our functor, which amounts to morphisms
\phi_{a,b}\colon 1^{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{op}}1^{b}\to 1^{a\otimes b}
in
\mathcal{C}
for each
a,b
and
1\to 1^{1}
. The former
are given as the preimage of the morphism

\textstyle{1^{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{op}}1^{b}\otimes(a\otimes b)=1^{b}\otimes 1^{a}\otimes(a\otimes b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{a}\otimes\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{1^{b}\otimes b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{b}}
\textstyle{1}

(3.12)

under the bijection
\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(1^{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{op}}1^{b},1^{a\otimes b})\cong\hom_{\mathcal{C}}((1^{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{op}}1^{b})\otimes(a\otimes b),1)
and the
latter is given as the preimage
\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(1,1^{1})\cong\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(1\otimes 1,1)\cong\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(1,1)
. The naturality of this data is deduced from
the naturality of the defining bijections
\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(c,b^{a})\to\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(c\otimes a,b)
.

Example 3.2.14
.

The category of (complete) bornological vector spaces is closed symmetric monoidal and hence admits a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure. It contains
the lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category of Banach spaces as a full subcategory. We will discuss this example in more detail in
Section

7.1
.

Example 3.2.15
.

Let
R
be a
*
-ring. Then, the category
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}
of right
R
-modules admits a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure. We will discuss
this example in more detail in
Section

7.2
.

Example 3.2.16
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. Then, the category
\operatorname{Ch}(R)
of unbounded chain complexes of
R
-modules is a closed symmetric monoidal (model) category,
see e.g.
[
Hov99
, Proposition 4.2.13]
, and hence admits a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure. More precisely, the monoidal product on

\operatorname{Ch}(R)
is given by
(X\otimes Y)_{n}=\bigoplus_{n}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{n-k})
, the monoidal unit
1
is
R
interpreted
as a chain complex concentrated in degree
0
, and we have a chain complex
Y^{X}
with components

(Y^{X})_{n}\equiv\operatorname{Hom}(X,Y)_{n}=\Pi_{k}\hom_{R}(X_{k},Y_{n+k})=\hom_{\operatorname{Ch}(R)}(X,Y[n])

(3.13)

and differentials induced from
X
and
Y
, respectively. In particular, we have

(1^{X})_{n}\equiv\operatorname{Hom}(X,1)_{n}=\Pi_{k}\hom_{R}(X_{k},1_{n+k})=\hom_{R}(X_{n},R)

(3.14)

and

(1^{1^{X}})_{n}=\operatorname{Hom}(1^{X},1)_{n}=\hom_{R}((1^{X})_{n},R)=\hom_{R}(\hom_{R}(X_{n},R),R)

(3.15)

with a map of chain complexes
X\to(1^{1^{X}})
given in degree
n
by

X_{n}\to\hom_{R}(\hom_{R}(X_{n},R),R),a\mapsto\operatorname{Ev}_{a}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\operatorname{Ev}_{a}(f)=f(a)

(3.16)

omitting the respective differentials. In particular, under the inclusion
R\text{-}\hskip-2.84544pt\operatorname{mod}\to\operatorname{Ch}(R)
sending each
R
-module
X
to the chain
complex complex concentrated in degree zero with value
X
, this reproduces the lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure of

Proposition

7.2.11
. Moreover, noting that
(1^{1^{X}})_{n}=(1^{1^{X_{n}}})
, applying

3.2.7
yields the
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category

\widehat{\operatorname{Ch}(R)}\subseteq\operatorname{Ch}(R)
consisting of those chain complexes which are degreewise reflexive modules.

Remark 3.2.17
.

There are notions of duality for (symmetric) monoidal categories between rigidity and closedness, two of which we will recall in the following and
place in the (lax)
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive context. A
symmetric Grothendieck-Verdier (GV) category
or
symmetric
*
-autonomous category

is a closed symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
together with a
dualizing object
, that is, an object
\mathfrak{d}
such that the canonical morphism

a\to\mathfrak{d}^{\mathfrak{d}^{a}}
is an isomorphism for all
a\in\mathcal{C}
. Repeating
3.2.10

with the monoidal unit replaced by the dualizing object
\mathfrak{d}
yields an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on
\mathcal{C}
.

In the special case where the dualizing object
\mathfrak{d}=1_{\mathcal{C}}
is the monoidal unit,
\mathcal{C}
is called an
r
-category
.

Remark 3.2.18
.

We informally summarize the constructions of (lax)
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories from symmetric monoidal categories with some type of
duality in the following commutative diagram.

3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{lax $\operatorname{O(1)}$-volutive}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 101.3474pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 249.9448pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(3.17)

Here, solid arrows preserve the underlying categories while dashed arrows generically change them.

In the remainder of this section, we establish some of the general theory of lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories.

Construction 3.2.19
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
be lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories. We construct a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on the category of functors

\mathcal{E}:=\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
. First, define a functor
\tilde{d}\colon\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}^{\operatorname{op}}
by setting

\begin{cases}\tilde{d}(F)=(d^{\prime})^{\operatorname{op}}\circ F^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d\\
\tilde{d}(\alpha\colon F\to G)=\operatorname{id}_{(d^{\prime})^{\operatorname{op}}}\circ\alpha^{\operatorname{op}}\circ\operatorname{id}_{d}\colon\tilde{d}(G)\to\tilde{d}(F).\end{cases}

(3.18)

We then define the component of the natural transformation
\tilde{\eta}
at
F\in\mathcal{E}
to be

\textstyle{F\cong\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}}\circ F\circ\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}\circ\operatorname{id}_{F}\circ\eta}
\textstyle{({d^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d^{\prime})\circ F\circ(d^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d)=(\tilde{d}^{\operatorname{op}}\circ\tilde{d})(F)}

(3.19)

which one checks to satisfy the coherence condition so that
(\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta})
defines a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on
\mathcal{E}
.

Construction 3.2.20
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
be lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories. Consider the lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
(\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}),\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta})

described in
3.2.19
. We define the category of
lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors
between

(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
to be
\operatorname{LaxHerm}(\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}),\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta})
, see
Definition

5.1.16
.
We will refer to the morphisms of this category as
lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive transformations
.

Remark 3.2.21
.

In the following we wish to describe functors and transformations of lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories more explicitly. A lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor

(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
is a pair
(F,\alpha)
consisting of a functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and a natural transformation

\alpha\colon d^{\prime}\circ F\to F^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d
satisfying
(\alpha^{\operatorname{op}}\circ\operatorname{id}_{d})(\operatorname{id}_{F}\circ\eta)=(\operatorname{id}_{d^{\operatorname{op}}}\circ\alpha)(\eta^{\prime}\circ\operatorname{id}_{F})
.
Given two such lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors
(F_{1},\alpha_{1})
and
(F_{2},\alpha_{2})
, a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive natural transformation
(F_{1},\alpha_{1})\to(F_{2},\alpha_{2})

is a natural transformation
\xi\colon F_{1}\to F_{2}
satisfying
(\xi^{\operatorname{op}}\circ\operatorname{id}_{d})\alpha_{2}(\operatorname{id}_{d^{\prime}}\xi)=\alpha_{1}
.

Definition 3.2.22
.

We denote by
\operatorname{Cat}^{\text{lax}\operatorname{O(1)}\text{-vol}}
the 2-category of lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories, lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors, and lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive
natural transformations.

Definition 3.2.23
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
be lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories. Their
(cartesian) product
is the lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category

(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)\times(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}):=(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d\times d^{\prime},\eta\times\eta^{\prime}).

(3.20)

The cartesian product endows
\operatorname{Cat}^{\text{lax}\operatorname{O(1)}\text{-vol}}
with a symmetric monoidal structure.

Remark 3.2.24
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
be lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories and let

(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
be a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor. We have described a procedure in
3.2.7

that takes as an input a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category, and produces an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category. This procedure is
not
compatibile with
lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors, i.e.
(F,\alpha)
does not descend to an (
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive) functor between the associated
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories.
The situation is better if
\alpha
is a natural isomorphism, that is,
(F,\alpha)
is really an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor between lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories.

3.3
Lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structures are relative

In this section we present various equivalent formulations of the notion of a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category; in particular, we will show that lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive
categories are special relative
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories. We start with a point of view that is essentially covered in
[
HLS16
]
.

Remark 3.3.1
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Set}
be a functor, which we may also refer to as a
pairing
on
\mathcal{C}
. The pairing
F
induces under the usual Hom-tensor
adjunction a functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\operatorname{Set})
. Equivalently, this can be understood as a functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}})
into the category
of presheaves on
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
. The latter admits the yoneda embedding
y\colon\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}})
, so that we may ask whether there exists
a lift in the diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 11.06252pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.06252pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.4978pt\raise-9.39583pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{y}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.93234pt\raise-23.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 50.41672pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-6.61111pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.76283pt\raise-26.27498pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{F}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.06252pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-14.91667pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 35.06252pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(3.21)

and if so whether this lift is an equivalence of categories or not.

Definition 3.3.2
.

In the situation of
Remark

3.3.1
, we will say that
F
is
representable
2
2

2

The terminology is motivated by

[
Lur17
, Definition 5.2.1.8]
.
if there exists a lift in the diagram.
We will say that
F
is
perfect
or
non-degenerate
if it is representable and the lift is an equivalence of categories. We will say that

F
is
symmetric
if
F\circ\beta_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}}\cong F
for
\beta
the symmetric braiding on
\operatorname{Cat}
.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories in the sense of
Definition

3.2.1
are the same as representable symmetric functors
in the sense of
Definition

3.3.2
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
be a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category. We define a functor
\Phi_{d}\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Set}
as follows:
given an object
(a,b)\in\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
, we define

\Phi_{d}(a,b)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(d(a),b),

(3.22)

and given a morphism
(X,Y)\colon(a,b)\to(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
we define

\Phi_{d}(X,Y)\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(d(a),b)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(d(a^{\prime}),b^{\prime}),(f\colon d(a)\to b)\mapsto Y\circ f\circ d(X),

(3.23)

which is clearly functorial. The functor
\Phi_{d}\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Set}
is symmetric since we have a bijection

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(d(a),b)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}}(a,d^{\operatorname{op}}(b))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(d(b),a)

(3.24)

which is natural in
(a,b)\in\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
, where we have identified
\mathcal{C}\cong(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}})^{\operatorname{op}}
. Put differently,
we have a natural isomorphism
\Phi_{d}\circ\beta\cong\Phi_{d}
where
\beta
denotes the symmetric braiding on
\operatorname{Cat}
.
The functor
\Phi_{d}
is representable by construction.

Conversely, let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Set}
be a functor that is symmetric and representable. We will construct a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on
\mathcal{C}

as follows: first, since
F
is representable we have a functor
d\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
fitting into the commutative diagram of

Equation

3.21
. By definition, this amounts to an equivalence of functors

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(d(-),-)\cong F(-,-)\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Set}

(3.25)

so that by symmetry of
F
, we have a natural bijection

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(d(a),b)\cong F(a,b)\cong F(b,a)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(d(b),a)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}}(a,d^{\operatorname{op}}(b))

(3.26)

for any
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
. In other words,
d^{\operatorname{op}}
is right adjoint to
d
. The adjunction supplies us with a natural transformation
\operatorname{id}\to d^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d
.
Explicitly, the functor
d
induces a map

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(a,b)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}}(d(a),d(b))\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(a,d^{2}(b))

(3.27)

for any
a\in\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}},b\in\mathcal{C}
where in the second step we have used the adjunction. By the yoneda lemma, this
asserts the existence of maps
\eta_{b}\colon b\to d^{2}(b)
which assemble into a natural transformation
\operatorname{id}\to d^{\operatorname{op}}\circ d
.
One checks that the pair
(d,\eta)
defines a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

We have seen in the proof of
Lemma

3.3.3
that for a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
,

d^{\operatorname{op}}
is right adjoint to
d
. The following two points of view are centered around this observation. We start by recalling the following.

Definition 3.3.4
.

We define a 2-category
\operatorname{Adj}
which has two objects
a
and
b
, which has 1-morphisms freely generated by 1-morphisms
X\colon a\to b

and
Y\colon b\to a
, and 2-morphisms freely generated by 2-morphisms
f\colon\operatorname{id}_{a}\to Y\circ X
and
g\colon X\circ Y\to\operatorname{id}_{b}

subject to the condition that
(g\circ\operatorname{id}_{X})\bullet(\operatorname{id}_{X}\circ f)=\operatorname{id}_{X}
and
(\operatorname{id}_{Y}\circ g)\bullet(f\circ\operatorname{id}_{Y})=\operatorname{id}_{Y}

holds. We call the 2-category
\operatorname{Adj}
the
walking adjunction
.

Remark 3.3.5
.

An adjunction in a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
is the same as a 2-functor
\operatorname{Adj}\to\mathcal{B}
; in this sense,
\operatorname{Adj}
classifies all possible adjunctions.

Lemma 3.3.6
.

The walking adjunction
\operatorname{Adj}
admits a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.

Proof.

The 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure is given by the strictly involutive 2-functor
F\colon\operatorname{Adj}\to\operatorname{Adj}^{2\operatorname{op}}
determined by the assignment

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &F(a)=b\\
&F(b)=a\\
&(F(X)\colon F(a)\to F(b))=Y\colon b\to a\\
&(F(Y)\colon F(b)\to F(a))=X\colon a\to b\\
&(F(f)\colon F(YX)=F(Y)\circ F(X)\to F(\operatorname{id}_{a}))=g\colon X\circ Y\to\operatorname{id}_{b}\\
&(F(g)\colon F(\operatorname{id}_{b})\to F(XY)=F(X)\circ F(Y))=f\colon\operatorname{id}_{a}\to Y\circ X.\end{aligned}

(3.28)

∎

Before stating our next comparison result, we consider the following.

is a 1-morphism, and

is a 2-morphism
for which the composition

.

We call the triple
(c,Z,h)
a

in
\mathcal{B}\equiv(\mathcal{B},d,\eta,\tau)
.

Remark 3.3.8
.

In the situation of
Definition

3.3.7
, assume the functor
d
is strictly involutive and that
\eta
and
\tau
are trivial.
In this case, we can describe a volutive adjunction as a triple
(c,Z,h)
where
c\in\mathcal{B}
is an object,
Z\colon c\to d(c)
is a 1-morphism in
\mathcal{B}
,
and
h\colon\operatorname{id}_{c}\to d(Z)\circ Z
is a 2-morphism in
\mathcal{B}
satisfying
(d(h)\circ\operatorname{id})\bullet(\operatorname{id}\circ h)=\operatorname{id}_{Z}
and

(\operatorname{id}\circ d(h))\bullet(h\circ\operatorname{id})=\operatorname{id}_{d(Z)}
. Note that the first equation already implies the second equation, by applying the
2-contravariant functor
d
. We expect that the same holds true for general 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structures
(d,\eta,\tau)
; however,
we will not need this fact in the following.

Lemma 3.3.9
.

Lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories are the same as volutive adjunctions in the 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category
(\operatorname{Cat},(-)^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})
.

Proof.

Spelling out 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category
(\operatorname{Cat},(-)^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})
according to
Remark

3.3.8
yields
precisely the same data as that of a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category.
∎

Lemma 3.3.10
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},d,\eta,\tau)
be a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category. Then, volutive adjunctions in
(\mathcal{B},d,\eta,\tau)
are the same as
2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-functors
\operatorname{Adj}\to\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Let
(c,Z,h)
be a volutive adjunction in
(\mathcal{B},d,\eta,\tau)
. We define a functor
G\colon\operatorname{Adj}\to\mathcal{B}
by setting
G(a)=c,G(b)=d(c),G(X)=Z,G(Y)=\eta_{c}^{-1}\circ d(Z),G(f)=h,G(g)=d(h)\bullet(\tau_{c}\circ\operatorname{id})\bullet(\eta_{Z}\circ\operatorname{id})
.
We need to check that the Zorro moves are satisfied. The first Zorro move is satisfied by
Equation

3.29

and the second Zorro move is satisfied by
Equation

3.30
. Next, we need to construct a 2-contravariant

\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure
(\omega,\Xi)
on
G
. We define an invertible 2-transformation
\omega\colon G^{2\operatorname{op}}\circ F\to d\circ G
as follows.
Its 1-morphism component at
a\in\operatorname{Adj}
is given by the identity 1-morphism on
GF(a)=G(b)=d(c)=d(G(a))
and its 1-morphism component at

b\in\operatorname{Adj}
is given by

\textstyle{GF(b)=G(a)=c\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{c}}
\textstyle{d^{2}(c)=dG(b)}

(3.31)

Its 2-morphism component at
X\colon a\to b
in
\operatorname{Adj}
is given by the identity 2-morphism filling the diagram

(3.35)

One checks that the triple
(G,\omega,\Xi)
defines a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor

(3.38)

One checks that the triple
(c,Z,h)
defines a volutive adjunction.
∎

Corollary 3.3.11
.

Lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories are the same as 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-functors
\operatorname{Adj}\to\operatorname{Cat}
.

Proof.

Combine
Lemma

3.3.10
and
Lemma

3.3.9
.
∎

All together, we have proved the following.

Theorem 3.3.12
.

The following four notions are equivalent:

(i)

Lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories in the sense of
Definition

3.2.1
,

(ii)

representable symmetric functors in the sense of
Definition

3.3.2
,

(iii)

volutive adjunctions in
\operatorname{Cat}
in the sense of
Definition

3.3.7
, and

(iv)

2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors
\operatorname{Adj}\to\operatorname{Cat}
.

We leave it to the reader to complete the definitions in the following result, providing various equivalent formulations of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories.

Corollary 3.3.13
.

The following four notions are equivalent:

(i)

\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories in the sense of
Definition

2.1.1
,

(ii)

non-degenerate symmetric functors in the sense of
Definition

3.3.2
,

(iii)

volutive adjoint equivalences in
\operatorname{Cat}
,

(iv)

2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors
\operatorname{AdjEqv}\to\operatorname{Cat}
.

4
Relative
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories

In this section, we introduce relative and lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories. We start by reviewing the theory of adjoints of 1- and 2-morphisms in a 3-category,
highlighting in particular that the 3-category of 2-categories with adjoints has 2-adjoints. We then define
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories and consider the special
case of rigid symmetric monoidal 2-categories, in which the
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive structure is given by the Serre isomorphism. Finally, we introduce lax and
relative
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories, and show that the latter notion generalizes the former.

4.1
Adjoints in 2- and 3-categories

Definition 4.1.1
.

A 2-category has
right adjoints
if for each 1-morphism
X
there exists another 1-morphism
X^{R}
together with 2-morphisms

\operatorname{coev}_{X}\colon\operatorname{id}\to X^{R}\circ X
and
\operatorname{ev}_{X}\colon X\circ X^{R}\to\operatorname{id}
satisfying the Zorro/snake/triangle moves.
We define left adjoints analogously. We will say that a 2-category has (all) adjoints if it has both right adjoints and left adjoints
for all 1-morphisms.

Lemma 4.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a 2-category with right adjoints. The assignment
a\mapsto a,X\mapsto X^{R}
extends to a 2-functor
(-)^{R}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}
.

Proof.

We define the functor
(-)^{R}
on 2-morphism level by mapping
f\colon X\to Y
to

\textstyle{Y^{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{coev}_{X}\circ\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{X^{R}\circ X\circ Y^{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\circ f\circ\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{X^{R}\circ Y\circ Y^{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{Y}}
\textstyle{X^{R}.}

(4.1)

One checks that this defines a 2-functor.
∎

Lemma 4.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be 2-categories with right adjoints and let
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
a functor. Then, there exists an invertible 2-transformation

\xi_{F}\colon(-)^{R}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\circ F\to F^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}\circ(-)^{R}_{\mathcal{B}}.

(4.2)

Let furthermore
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be another functor and let
\alpha\colon F\to G
be a 2-transformation.
Then, there exists a modification
\xi_{\alpha}
fitting into the diagram

(4.3)

The 2-modifications
\xi_{\alpha}
are moreover natural and compatible with respect to composition and units.

Proof.

First, we construct the 2-transformation
\xi_{F}
. Recalling that
(-)^{R}
is the identity on objects, we may take the 1-morphism components of
\xi_{F}
to be
identities. The 2-morphism component of
\xi_{F}
at
X\colon a\to b
is the (unique) isomorphism
F(X^{R})\to F(X)^{R}
expressing the fact that
F(X^{R})
is a right adjoint of
F(X)
,
together with the uniqueness of adjoints. More precisely, the 2-morphisms

exhibit

(4.5)

one checks that this defines an invertible 2-transformation
\xi_{F}
. Next, we construct the modification
\xi_{\alpha}
. Its
2-morphism component at
c\in\mathcal{B}
is given by

\textstyle{(\xi_{\alpha})_{c}:=\operatorname{ev}_{\alpha_{c}}\colon\alpha_{c}\circ\alpha_{c}^{R}\to\operatorname{id};}

(4.6)

one checks that this defines a modification and that these modifications are natural and compatible with composition and units in
\alpha
.
∎

Lemma 4.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be 2-categories with adjoints. Then
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
has adjoints.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\colon F\to G
be a 2-transformation. We consider the case of right adjoints. We may define a 2-transformation
\alpha^{R}\colon G\to F
with
1-morphism components
(\alpha^{R})_{a}=\alpha_{a}^{R}\colon G(a)\to F(a)
and 2-morphism components
(\alpha^{R})_{X}:=\alpha_{X^{L}}^{R}\colon F(X)\circ\alpha_{a}^{R}\to\alpha_{b}^{R}\circ G(X)
. The adjunction data
\operatorname{ev}_{\alpha},\operatorname{coev}_{\alpha}
is then given componentwise by
(\operatorname{ev}_{\alpha})_{a}:=\operatorname{ev}_{\alpha_{a}}\colon\alpha_{a}\circ\alpha_{a}^{R}\to\operatorname{id}
and
(\operatorname{coev}_{\alpha})_{a}:=\operatorname{coev}_{\alpha_{a}}\colon\operatorname{id}\to\alpha^{R}_{a}\circ\alpha_{a}
. The case of left/all adjoints is completely
analogous.
∎

Definition 4.1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a 3-category. A 2-morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathcal{D}
has a right/left adjoint if it has a right/left adjoint as a 1-morphism in the 2-category

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(X,Y)
. We will say that
\mathcal{D}
has left/right/(all) adjoints for 2-morphisms if each 2-morphism has left/right/(both) adjoints. We will say that

\mathcal{D}
has left/right/(all) adjoints for 1-morphisms if its homotopy 2-category
\operatorname{h}\hskip-1.42271pt_{2}\mathcal{D}
has left/right/(all) adjoints. We will say that
\mathcal{D}
has (all)
adjoints if it has left and right adjoints for both 1- and 2-morphisms.

Definition 4.1.6
.

We denote by
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
the 3-category of 2-categories with (all) adjoints, 2-functors, 2-transformations, and modifications.

Corollary 4.1.7
.

The 3-category
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
has adjoints for 2-morphisms. In particular, we have 3-functors

(-)^{1\operatorname{op}}\colon 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}\to(2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}})^{2\operatorname{op}},(-)^{2\operatorname{op}}\colon 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}\to(2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}})^{3\operatorname{op}}

(4.7)

and 3-functors

(-)^{L},(-)^{R}\colon 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}\to(2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}})^{(2,3)\operatorname{op}}.

(4.8)

Remark 4.1.8
.

We wish to understand the interaction of the functors
(-)^{R},(-)^{L}
and
(-)^{1\operatorname{op}},(-)^{2\operatorname{op}}
. We claim that
(-)^{1\operatorname{op}}\circ(-)^{R}\cong(-)^{L}\circ(-)^{1\operatorname{op}}
.
The functor
(-)^{1\operatorname{op}}\circ(-)^{R}
in particular assigns each 2-category with adjoints
\mathcal{B}
to its 1-opposite
\mathcal{B}^{1\operatorname{op}}
, each 2-functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}

to its 1-opposite
F^{\operatorname{op}}\colon\mathcal{B}^{1\operatorname{op}}\to(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})^{1\operatorname{op}}
, and each 2-transformation
\alpha\colon F\to G
to

(\alpha^{R})^{1\operatorname{op}}\colon F^{1\operatorname{op}}\to(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})^{1\operatorname{op}}
. The 1-morphism component of
(\alpha^{R})^{1\operatorname{op}}
at
a\in\mathcal{B}^{1\operatorname{op}}
is given by

\alpha^{R}_{a}\colon F(a)\to G(a)
in
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})^{1\operatorname{op}}
, that is,
\alpha_{a}^{R}\colon G(a)\to F(a)
in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. We claim that
(\alpha^{R})^{1\operatorname{op}}
is
a left adjoint of the 2-morphism
\alpha^{1\operatorname{op}}
. Indeed, adjunction data is given by

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 33.066pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-33.066pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 57.066pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 57.066pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
