118.35066pt\raise-7.76227pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 136.5312pt\raise-13.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 136.5312pt\raise-13.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\alpha^{1\operatorname{op}}\circ(\alpha^{R})^{1\operatorname{op}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(4.9)

Hence, we obtain an invertible 3-transformation

(-)^{1\operatorname{op}}\circ(-)^{R}\cong(-)^{L}\circ(-)^{1\operatorname{op}}.

(4.10)

We also note that we have invertible 3-transformations

(-)^{2\operatorname{op}}\circ(-)^{R}\cong(-)^{R}\circ(-)^{2\operatorname{op}},\qquad(-)^{2\operatorname{op}}\circ(-)^{L}\cong(-)^{L}\circ(-)^{2\operatorname{op}},

(4.11)

by a similar argument keeping tracking of the contravariances.

Lemma 4.1.9
.

There exists an invertible 3-transformation

S\colon\operatorname{id}_{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}}\to(-)^{RR}.

(4.12)

Proof.

We construct the 3-transformation
S\colon\operatorname{id}_{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}}\to(-)^{RR}
. Recalling that both functors are the identity
on objects, we may set the 1-morphism component of
S
at a 2-category with adjoints
\mathcal{B}
to be the double right adjoint functor

S_{\mathcal{B}}:=(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{B}}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
. Recalling that both functors are the identity on 1-morphism level, we may set the 2-morphism component
of
S
at a 2-functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
to be the 2-transformation

(4.16)

instead, whose pasting yields the 3-morphism component

. It is straightforward to check that this defines an invertible 3-transformations, observing
that each component is evidently invertible.
∎

Corollary 4.1.10
.

We obtain an invertible 3-transformation

S\colon\operatorname{id}_{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}_{(3,1)}}\to\operatorname{id}_{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}_{(3,1)}}

(4.17)

on the underlying (3,1)-category of
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
.

4.2
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories

We start with a general discussion of
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-actions on 3-categories.

Remark 4.2.1
.

First, recall the low-dimensional homotopy groups of
\operatorname{SO(2)}
,

\pi_{0}(\operatorname{SO(2)})=\star,\pi_{1}(\operatorname{SO(2)})=\mathbb{Z},\pi_{2}(\operatorname{SO(2)})=\pi_{3}(\operatorname{SO(2)})=\dotsc=\star.

(4.18)

In particular, giving an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-action on a 3-category
\mathcal{D}
amounts to specifiying a 4-functor

\mathrm{B}\Pi_{3}(\operatorname{SO(2)})\to 3\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat},\ \star\mapsto\mathcal{D}.

(4.19)

which equivalently may be understood as a braided monoidal 2-functor

\mathbb{Z}\to\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}})

(4.20)

where
\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}})
denotes the braided monoidal 2-category of autoequivalences of the identity 3-functor on
\mathcal{D}
.

For the convenience of the reader, we will include some details of the notion of a braided monidal 2-functor in the following.

Remark 4.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}
be braided monoidal 2-categories. In the following, we wish to spell out the data of a braided monoidal 2-functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{C}
,
omitting the coherence conditions on the highest level. First, we have a 2-functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{C}
. Second, we have a monoidal structure on
F
,
which amounts to the following data: (i) for any two objects
a,b\in\mathcal{B}
1-isomorphisms
\varphi_{a,b}\colon F(a)\otimes F(b)\to F(a\otimes b)
, and a
1-isomorphism
\epsilon\colon 1_{\mathcal{C}}\to F(1_{\mathcal{B}})
(ii) for any two 1-morphisms
X\colon a\to a^{\prime}
and
Y\colon b\to b^{\prime}
, 2-isomorphisms
\varphi_{X,Y}

fitting into the diagram

3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.29579pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 77.29579pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F(b\otimes a)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(4.24)

which are subject to further coherence conditions we again do not spell out here.

Remark 4.2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 3-category. In the following, we wish to describe the braided monoidal structure on the 2-category
\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}})
in some detail.
By definition, the objects of
\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}})
are invertible 3-transformations
\alpha\colon\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\to\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
; the 1-morphisms are
3-modifications, and the 2-morphisms are pertubations between them. The monoidal structure can be described as follows: given two
invertible 3-transformations,
\alpha,\gamma\colon\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\to\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
, we may consider their composition,
\alpha\bullet\gamma
with respect to which the
the identity 3-transformation
\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}}
acts as a monoidal unit. The braided structure is given as follows. We need to supply a 3-modification

\beta_{\alpha,\gamma}\colon\alpha\bullet\gamma\to\gamma\bullet\alpha;

(4.25)

first, given an object
a\in\mathcal{C}
, we need to supply the 2-morphism component

(\beta_{\alpha,\gamma})_{a}\colon\alpha_{a}\circ\gamma_{a}=(\alpha\bullet\gamma)_{a}\to(\gamma\bullet\alpha)_{a}=\gamma_{a}\circ\alpha_{a}.

(4.26)

Recall that
\alpha
is an invertible 3-transformation, that is, it comes in particular with invertible 1-morphism components
\alpha_{a}\colon a\to a
and
2-morphism components
\alpha_{X}\colon X\circ\alpha_{a}\to\alpha_{b}\circ X
, for
X\colon a\to b
a 1-morphism in
\mathcal{C}
. In particular, we may choose

(\beta_{\alpha,\gamma})_{a}:=\alpha_{\gamma_{a}}^{-1}\colon\alpha_{a}\circ\gamma_{a}\to\gamma_{a}\circ\alpha_{a}.

(4.27)

Second, given a 1-morphism
X\colon a\to b
in
\mathcal{C}
, we need to supply the 3-morphism component
(\beta_{\alpha,\gamma})_{X}
. For this, we recall
that
\alpha
also comes with an invertible 3-morphism component at
f\colon X\to Y
of the form

(4.30)

; more specifically,
from left to right we have
\alpha_{\gamma_{a},X},\alpha_{\gamma_{X}},
and

. We have now described the data of the 3-transformation

\beta_{\alpha,\gamma}\colon\alpha\bullet\gamma\to\gamma\bullet\alpha
(omitting the various coherence conditions).

Having established the data of braided monoidal 2-functors and the braided monoidal structure on
\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}})
for some 3-category
\mathcal{C}
, we now turn
our attention to a particular
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-action.

Construction 4.2.4
.

In the following, we wish to construct an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-action on the (3,1)-category
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}_{(3,1)}
.
By our discussion in
Remark

4.2.1
, we need to supply a braided monoidal 2-functor

\rho\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{id}_{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}_{(3,1)}^{\operatorname{adj}}}).

(4.31)

We start by defining the underlying 2-functor. It suffices to describe the image
S
of
1\in\mathbb{Z}
under this 2-functor, as monoidality will
require us to send
k\mapsto S^{k}
. Hence, we need to specify a 3-transformation

S\colon\operatorname{id}_{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}_{(3,1)}^{\operatorname{adj}}}\to\operatorname{id}_{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}_{(3,1)}^{\operatorname{adj}}}.

(4.32)

We choose the 3-transformation described in
Corollary

4.1.10
, whose components we will repeat here
for the convenience of the reader. First, its 1-morphism component at
\mathcal{B}\in 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}_{(3,1)}
is given by

S_{\mathcal{B}}:=(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{B}}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B},

(4.33)

the double right adjoint functor on
\mathcal{B}
(whose inverse is given by the double left adjoint functor on
\mathcal{B}
). Second, its 2-morphism component
at
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
in
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}_{(3,1)}
is given by the canonical 2-transformation

\textstyle{S_{F}\colon F\circ S_{\mathcal{B}}=F\circ(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\circ F=S_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\circ F}

(4.34)

expressing compatibility of any 2-functor with the (double) right adjoint functor. Third, its 3-morphism component at
\alpha\colon F\to G
in

2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}_{(3,1)}
is given by the canonical modification
S_{\alpha}
fitting into the diagram

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha\circ\operatorname{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.54059pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\circ F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.92706pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\circ\alpha}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.92706pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-23.51907pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G\circ(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.89407pt\raise-26.76227pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.51907pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.51907pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\circ G}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(4.35)

using the fact that (invertible) 2-transformations are automatically compatible with (double) adjoints in precisely this sense.
One checks that this defines a 3-transformation
S\colon\operatorname{id}_{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}_{(3,1)}^{\operatorname{adj}}}\to\operatorname{id}_{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}_{(3,1)}^{\operatorname{adj}}}
, so that
we obtain a 2-functor
\rho\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{id}_{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}_{(3,1)}^{\operatorname{adj}}})
. The data of the monoidal structure on
\rho

can essentially be taken to be trivial, since we define
\rho(k):=S^{k}
, for which e.g.
\rho(n)\circ\rho(m)=S^{n}\circ S^{m}\cong S^{n+m}=\rho(n+m)

is essentially trivial. The braided structure on the other hand is non-trivial. Making use of the hexagon axioms and noting that the braided structure
on
\mathbb{Z}
is essentially trivial, it suffices to consider the component
\beta_{-1,1}
of the braiding. More precisely, according to

Equation

4.24
we need to provide a pertubation trivializing the 3-modification obtained as the composition

\textstyle{q(S):=\operatorname{id}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{S^{-1}\circ S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{S^{-1},S}}
\textstyle{S\circ S^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\operatorname{id}.}

(4.36)

We construct an invertible pertubation
\sigma\colon q(S)\to\operatorname{id}
as follows. First, we recall that the 2-morphism component of
q(S)
at an object

\mathcal{B}\in 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}_{(3,1)}^{\operatorname{adj}}
is given by the composition

\textstyle{\operatorname{id}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(-)^{LL}_{\mathcal{B}}\circ(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{B}}\circ(-)^{LL}_{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\operatorname{id}}

(4.37)

where the middle arrow is given by the 2-transformation expressing compatibility of the double left adjoint functor with the (double) right adjoint functor.
The 3-morphism component of the invertible pertubation
\sigma
is then constructed from the Zorro 3-isomorphisms expressing the adjunctions. In particular,
we note that the composition

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(X^{L})^{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(X^{R})^{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{X,}

(4.38)

whose constituents are the various 2-isomorphisms expressing uniqueness of adjoints, is isomorphic to the identity 2-morphism on
X
. A slightly more
sophisticated version of this observation (involving the various double adjoints) allows us to construct the component of
\sigma
. In conclusion,
we obtain an invertible pertubation
\sigma\colon q(S)\to\operatorname{id}
. The higher coherences of the braided structure then follow from the coherences encoded
in the various adjunctions.

Definition 4.2.5
.

The (3,1)-category of
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories

2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{SO(2)}\text{-vol}}_{(3,1)}
is the (3,1)-category of homotopy fixed points
with respect to the
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-action
\rho
on
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}_{(3,1)}^{\operatorname{adj}}
constructed in

4.2.4
.

.

\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category. An
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category is a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
with adjoints together with an
invertible 2-transformation
S\colon\operatorname{id}\to(-)^{RR}
such that the composed modification

(4.39)

is the identity on

(4.40)

where the square in the left diagram is filled by the 2-transformation expressing compatibility of the 2-functor
F
with (double) adjoints; we refer to

[
CL25
]
for the higher morphisms in the 3-category
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}_{(3,1)}^{\operatorname{SO(2)}\text{-vol}}
.

Remark 4.2.7
.

The 3-category of
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category has been discussed in
[
CL25
, Section 2.2]

with the exception of the condition presented in
Equation

4.39
, which had only been hinted at there. We stress that the notion of an

\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive structure there is incomplete and misses this important condition.

Remark 4.2.8
.

The condition in
Equation

4.39
asserts that the
twisted quantum dimension
of
S
is trivial. The terminology is motivated by the fact
that an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-action on a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
is the same as an invertible 2-transformation
P\colon\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}\to\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}
whose quantum dimension

q(P)
is trivial. The quantum dimension
q(P)
is to be understood as the quantum dimension in the category
\operatorname{Aut}_{\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}}
whose braided monoidal
structure is described in
Remark

4.2.3
. Explicity, for any object

P\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}}
, the quantum dimension is the composition

\textstyle{\operatorname{id}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{P^{-1}\circ P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{P^{-1},P}}
\textstyle{P\circ P^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\operatorname{id}}

(4.41)

where
\beta
is the braiding in
\operatorname{Aut}_{\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}}
. In the graphical calculus notation, the quantum dimension is also called the figure eight diagram.

Remark 4.2.9
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},S)
be an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category. Then, the maximal subgroupoid
\mathcal{B}^{\times}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
inherits an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-action. Conversely, any
2-groupoid with
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-action defines an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category.

The proof of the following result will occupy our attention for the remainder of this section.

Proposition 4.2.10
.

Every rigid symmetric monoidal 2-category admits an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive structure.

Lemma 4.2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a rigid symmetric monoidal 2-category. Then, there are invertible 2-transformations

\mathcal{P}\colon\operatorname{id}\to(-)^{**}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{Q}\colon(-)^{**}\to(-)^{LL}.

(4.42)

where
(-)^{*}
denotes the functor induced by duality data (for objects) and
(-)^{L}
denotes the functor induced by left adjunction data (for 1-morphisms).

Proof.

The 2-transformation
\mathcal{P}
is constructed from the symmetric braiding similar to the 1-categorical case. In order to construct the invertible
2-transformation
\mathcal{Q}
, we will work in slightly more generality. Let
\mathcal{C}
be any monoidal 2-category with duals and adjoints. We will construct
an invertible 2-transformation
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\colon{}^{*}\!(-)\to(-)^{*}\circ(-)^{RR}
, which gives rise to a 2-transformation

{}^{**}\!(-)\to(-)^{RR}
which yields
\mathcal{Q}
by inverting both sides in the case of
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{B}
. We now construct
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
. Its 1-morphism
component at an object
a\in\mathcal{C}
is given by

\textstyle{Q_{a}^{\prime}\colon{}^{*}\!a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{a}^{R}}
\textstyle{{}^{*}\!a\circ a\circ a^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{a}\circ\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{a^{*}}

(4.43)

and its 2-morphism component
Q_{X}^{\prime}
at a 1-morphism
X\colon a\to b
in
\mathcal{C}
is given by pasting the cells in the diagram

0.0pt\raise-2.86111pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{a}\circ\operatorname{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 181.35915pt\raise-95.00012pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 181.35915pt\raise-95.00012pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{a^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 229.3508pt\raise-77.85333pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.34224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(X^{LL})^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 274.88533pt\raise-51.3604pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(4.44)

here, the upper left and lower right square commute by the interchange law. The upper right diagram is filled with the naturality data of the duality
structure, that is, the 2-isomorphism

\widetilde{\Omega}_{X}\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{b}\bullet({}^{*}\!X\circ\operatorname{id})\cong\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{a}\circ(\operatorname{id}\circ X),

(4.45)

and the lower left diagram is filled with the 2-isomorphism

\Omega_{X^{L}}^{R}\colon(X\circ\operatorname{id})\bullet\operatorname{ev}_{b}^{R}\cong(\operatorname{id}\circ(X^{LL})^{*})\bullet\operatorname{ev}_{a}^{R}.

(4.46)

where we have used the interaction between the duality and adjunction functors. One checks that this defines an invertible 2-transformation.
∎

Corollary 4.2.12
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a rigid symmetric monoidal 2-category. Then, there is an invertible 2-transformation

\mathcal{S}:=\left(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.16667pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.16667pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{id}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.34718pt\raise 6.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.12001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}^{-1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.16667pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.16667pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}^{**}\!(-)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 58.10417pt\raise 6.80057pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43947pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Q}^{-1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.30562pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 79.30562pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(-)^{RR}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right)

(4.47)

which is called the
Serre isomorphism
. Its 1-morphism component at
a\in\mathcal{B}
is given by

S_{a}\colon(\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{a})\circ(\beta_{a,a}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{ev}^{R}_{a})\colon a\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces a.

(4.48)

Proof of
Proposition

4.2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a rigid symmetric monoidal 2-category, that is,
\mathcal{B}
has all duals and adjoints. We have already constructed an invertible 2-transformation

\mathcal{S}\colon\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}\to(-)^{RR}
in
Corollary

4.2.12
, the Serre isomorphism. It remains to show that the twisted
quantum dimension of the Serre isomorphism is trivial, that is, we need to check that the diagram in
Equation

4.39
is trivial. By

Corollary

4.2.12
, this is a tedious but straightforward computation.
∎

4.3
Relative
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories

Definition 4.3.1
.

A
2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-category
consists of a 3-category
\mathcal{D}
with adjoints for 2-morphisms, an invertible 3-transformation

S\colon\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}}\to(-)^{RR}
, and an invertible pertubation
\sigma
trivializing the 3-modification

(4.49)

Remark 4.3.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{D},S,\sigma)
be a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-category. Then, the pair
(S,\sigma)
induces an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-action on the
underlying (3,1)-category
\mathcal{D}_{(3,1)}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
. Conversely, any
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-action on a (3,1)-category defines a 2-contravariant

\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-category.

Example 4.3.3
.

The 3-category
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
admits a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive structure which reduces to the

\operatorname{SO(2)}
-action on
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}_{(3,1)}^{\operatorname{adj}}
described in
4.2.4
.
This follows essentially from our explicit description of this action, together with
Lemma

4.1.9
.

Definition 4.3.4
.

A
functor
of 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-categories consists of a 3-functor
F\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and an invertible modification

(4.52)

are required to be equal; we spell out the unlabeled pertubation appearing here in more detail in

Remark

4.3.5
.

Remark 4.3.5
.

In the following, we wish to describe the unlabeled pertubation in
Equation

4.52
more explicitly.
In order to describe the pertubation, we first need to describe the vertical 3-transformations and the
respective 3-modifications. The only non-trivial vertical 3-transformations are the (invertible) 3-transformations

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (-)^{LL}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\circ(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\circ F\to F\circ(-)^{LL}_{\mathcal{D}}\circ(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{D}}\\
(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\circ(-)^{LL}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\circ F\to F\circ(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{D}}\circ(-)^{LL}_{\mathcal{D}}\end{aligned}

(4.53)

expressing the compatibility of the 3-functor
F
with adjoints. Next, we need to describe the 3-modifications ensuring that all the horizontal and
vertical 3-transformations are compatible. There are five such non-trivial (invertible) 3-modifications in total, which we will now describe.
First, we have the two invertible 3-modifications

(4.56)

which are in turn filled with the 3-modifications recording the interchange law (respective right triangles), terms built from
\eta
and its inverse
(respective upper and lower triangles), and the construction of compatibility data for iterated adjoints and a 3-functor (respective left triangle).
Finally, we have an (invertible) 3-modification in the diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 62.40036pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 62.40036pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F\circ(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{D}}\circ(-)^{LL}_{\mathcal{D}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(4.57)

which records the naturality of the compatibility data of iterated adjoints and any 3-functor. Having discussed all five non-trivial 3-modifications,
we are now in position to discuss the four individual pertubations (upper, lower, left, and right) whose pasting yields the unlabeled pertubation
of
Equation

4.52
we set out to describe. The left and right pertubations express
naturality with respect to 3-modifications (here constructed from
\eta
) of
the naturality data with respect to 3-transformations (here constructed from
S
and
S^{\prime}
) of the adjunction data (here for
(-)^{RR}
and
(-)^{LL}
).
The upper pertubation is constructed by unwinding various levels of compatibility of adjunction data with itself. The lower pertubation expresses
naturality with respect to 3-modifications (here constructed from
\eta
) of the symmetric braiding
\beta
. Together, these form the unlabeled
pertubation in
Equation

4.52
.

Remark 4.3.6
.

Weaker notions of functors between 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-categories are possible, e.g. where
\eta
is not required to be invertible.

Definition 4.3.7
.

A
relative
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category
consists of a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-category
\mathcal{D}
and a
functor of 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-categories
\mathcal{D}\to 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
.

Notation 4.3.8
.

Occasionally we will call a relative
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category
\mathcal{D}\to 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
a
relative
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category
over

\mathcal{D}
. This terminology is motivated as usual via straightening-unstraightening.

Example 4.3.9
.

An
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category is the same as a functor of 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-categories
\star\to 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
.

Remark 4.3.10
.

Let
U\colon 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}\to 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}
be the forgetful functor from the 3-category of 2-categories with adjoints
to the 3-category of 2-categories. This functor has a right adjoint
U^{R}
which maps a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
to the sub 2-category
with the same objects, those 1-morphisms which have both adjoints, and all 2-morphisms between them. It is not known to us whether
U
also has a left adjoint.

4.4
Lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories

Definition 4.4.1
.

A
lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category
is a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
with adjoints together with a 2-transformation
S\colon\operatorname{id}\to(-)^{RR}
such that the composed
2-modification

(4.58)

is the identity on
\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}}
; here, the upper square is filled with the modification
\sigma_{\mathcal{B}}
described in

4.2.4
, the lower square is filled with the modification expressing naturality
of the braiding
\beta
on
\operatorname{Aut}_{\operatorname{id}_{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}}}
, and the left and right diagrams are filled with the modifications expressing
naturality of adjunction data.

Example 4.4.2
.

Any
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category is in particular a lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category. Conversely, any lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category
(\mathcal{B},S)
gives rise
to an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category by considering the full sub 2-category on those objects
a
, for which
S_{a}
is a 1-isomorphism.

Example 4.4.3
.

Any 0-closed symmetric monoidal 2-category with adjoints admits a lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive structure. The construction is essentially the same as in the
case of rigid symmetric monoidal 2-categories, with the exception that the 1-morphism component of the Serre morphism, that is

S_{a}\colon(\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{a})\circ(\beta_{a,a}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{ev}^{R}_{a})\colon a\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces a,

(4.59)

is generically not a 1-isomorphism anymore. In other words, we really have a lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive structure, rather than an ordinary
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive one.

Definition 4.4.4
.

Consider the 2-category generated freely by two objects
a
and
b
, two 1-morphisms
f\colon a\to b
and
g\colon b\to a
, four 2-morphisms

\operatorname{ev}\colon f\circ g\to\operatorname{id},\operatorname{coev}\colon\operatorname{id}\to g\circ f,\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}\colon g\circ f\to\operatorname{id}
, and
\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}\colon\operatorname{id}\to f\circ g
,
subject to the Zorro moves for
\operatorname{ev},\operatorname{coev}
and
\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}},\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}
, respectively. We denote this 2-category by
\operatorname{AmbAdj}
, the

walking ambidextrous adjunction
.

Remark 4.4.5
.

There are two 2-functors
\operatorname{Adj}\to\operatorname{AmbAdj}
, recording the two adunctions
f\dashv g
and
g\dashv f
. In particular,
\operatorname{AmbAdj}
has all adjoints, since

g
is both left and right adjoint to
f
and vice versa. The double adjoint functor
(-)^{RR}\colon\operatorname{AmbAdj}\to\operatorname{AmbAdj}
is by construction the identity
on objects and on 1-morphisms. We wish to understand whether
(-)^{RR}
is really equivalent (or even equal) to the identity functor on
\operatorname{AmbAdj}
. In order
to solve this problem, we need a short digression.

First, we note the following: we have 2-morphisms
\operatorname{ev}^{R}\colon\operatorname{id}\cong\operatorname{id}^{R}\to(f\circ g)^{R}\cong g^{R}\circ f^{R}\cong f\circ g

and
\operatorname{coev}^{R}\colon g\circ f\cong f^{R}\circ g^{R}\cong(g\circ f)^{R}\to\operatorname{id}^{R}\cong\operatorname{id}
which satisfy the Zorro moves by virtue of the Zorro moves
for
\operatorname{ev},\operatorname{coev}
. In particular,
\operatorname{coev}^{R},\operatorname{ev}^{R}
exhibit
g
as a left adjoint to
f
, in the same way as
\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}},\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}
.

However, adjunction data is essentially unique in the following sense: let
X\colon c\to d
be a 1-morphism in any 2-category
\mathcal{B}
with adjoints
and let
(Y,\operatorname{ev},\operatorname{coev})
and
(Y^{\prime},\operatorname{ev}^{\prime},\operatorname{coev}^{\prime})
be right adjunction data for
X\dashv(-)
. Then, there exists a unique 2-isomorphism
\Omega\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}

satisfying
\operatorname{ev}=\operatorname{ev}^{\prime}\bullet(\operatorname{id}\circ\Omega)
and
(\Omega\circ\operatorname{id})\bullet\operatorname{coev}=\operatorname{coev}^{\prime}
. Explicitly,
\Omega
is given by the composition

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{coev}^{\prime}\circ\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\circ X\circ Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\circ\operatorname{ev}}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}.}

(4.60)

Next, we recall the obvious fact that
X
is a left adjoint of
X^{R}
, with adjunction data given by
\operatorname{ev}_{X},\operatorname{coev}_{X}
. In particular, we have
2-isomorphisms
\Psi_{X}\colon X\cong(X^{R})^{L}
. In fact, for
Z\colon M\to N
any 2-morphism, we have a commutative diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 8.39583pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.39583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.5937pt\raise 5.89445pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.8889pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Psi_{M}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.39583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.53947pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-25.57895pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.39583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(M^{R})^{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.95367pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.34224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(Z^{R})^{L}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.95367pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-7.56248pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 11.11455pt\raise-27.0178pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.8889pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Psi_{N}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.22917pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.22917pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(N^{R})^{L}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(4.61)

so that
(Z^{R})^{L}=\Psi_{N}\bullet Z\bullet\Psi^{-1}_{M}
. Combining our simple observations, we find

\displaystyle Z^{RR}\cong(Z^{R})^{R}

\displaystyle=[(\operatorname{id}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{N})\bullet(\operatorname{id}\circ Z\circ\operatorname{id})\bullet(\operatorname{coev}_{M}\circ\operatorname{id})]^{R}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}\circ\operatorname{coev}_{M}^{R})\bullet(\operatorname{id}\circ Z^{R}\circ\operatorname{id})\bullet(\operatorname{ev}_{N}^{R}\circ\operatorname{id})

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}\circ[\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{M}\bullet(\Omega_{M}\circ\operatorname{id})])\bullet(\operatorname{id}\circ Z^{R}\circ\operatorname{id})\bullet([(\operatorname{id}\circ\Omega_{N}^{-1})\bullet\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}_{N}]\circ\operatorname{id})

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}\circ\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{M})\bullet(\operatorname{id}\circ(\Omega_{M}\bullet Z^{R}\bullet\Omega_{N}^{-1})\circ\operatorname{id})\bullet(\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}_{N}\circ\operatorname{id})

\displaystyle=(\Omega_{M}\bullet Z^{R}\bullet\Omega_{N}^{-1})^{L}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{N}\bullet(Z^{R})^{L}\bullet\Omega_{M}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{N}\bullet\Psi_{N}\bullet Z\bullet\Psi^{-1}_{M}\bullet\Omega_{M}^{-1}\cong(\Omega_{N}\bullet\Psi_{N})\bullet Z\bullet(\Omega_{M}\bullet\Psi_{M})^{-1}.

Hence, we obtain an invertible 2-transformation
S\colon\operatorname{id}\cong(-)^{RR}
whose 1-morphism components are identites and whose 2-morphism component at a
1-morphism
M
is given by
\Omega_{M}\bullet\Psi_{M}
. Moreover, the twisted quantum dimension (see
Equation

4.39
) of
S
is trivial.

We record our findings in the following.

Lemma 4.4.6
.

\operatorname{AmbAdj}
admits an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive structure.

Construction 4.4.7
.

Given any 2-category
\mathcal{B}
, we can construct a 3-category
\mathcal{B}[1]
which has two objects we denote
0
and
1
and only one non-trivial Hom 2-category, namely

\operatorname{Hom}_{[1](\mathcal{B})}(0,1):=\mathcal{B}
. If the 2-category
\mathcal{B}
is equiped with an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive structure, then the 3-category

\mathcal{B}[1]
receives a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive structure. In particular, if
\mathcal{B}
has adjoints then
\mathcal{B}[1]
has adjoints for 2-morphisms.
The notation is motivated by the notation
[1]:=\{0\rightarrow 1\}
for the walking arrow, which we recover upon setting
\mathcal{B}=\star
, the final 2-category.

Remark 4.4.8
.

4.4.7
provides us with a large class of examples of 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-categories.
Indeed, any
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category (such as any rigid symmetric monoidal 2-category) gives rise to one.

Corollary 4.4.9
.

\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]
is a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-category.

Proof.

Combine
Lemma

4.4.6
and
4.4.7
.
∎

Remark 4.4.10
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},S)
be an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category. Then, we have an invertible 2-modification in the diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern (4.62)

expressing the fact that the compatibility data of any functor with the (double) adjoint functor is natural. In particular, the components of
this modification provide 2-isomorphisms
S_{a}\cong S_{a}^{RR}
for any object
a\in\mathcal{B}
so that
S\cong S^{RR}
in
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
.
We will tacitly use this identification (or its equivalent form,
S^{R}\cong S^{L}
) in the following.

Proposition 4.4.11
.

Any lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category defines a relative
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category over
\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{B},S)
be a lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category. We define a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-functor

F\colon\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]\to 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
as follows. We set

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &F(0)=\mathcal{B},F(1)=\mathcal{B}^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}\\
&F(a)=(-)^{R},F(b)=(-)^{L}\\
&F(f)=S,F(g)=S^{R}\cong S^{L}\\
&F(\operatorname{ev})=\operatorname{ev}_{S},F(\operatorname{coev})=\operatorname{coev}_{S},\\
&F(\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}})=\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{S},F(\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}})=\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}_{S}\end{aligned}

(4.63)

where in the last line we have tacitly identified
S^{R}
and
S^{L}
.
First, this is a 3-functor since
S^{R}
is a right (2-)adjoint to
S
in
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
, so that the Zorro moves are satisfied.
The
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive structure on the 3-functor, that is, the invertible modification
\eta
is given by
S
and the coherence condition is satisfied
because the twisted quantum dimension of
S
is trivial.
∎

Remark 4.4.12
.

2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-functor
F\colon\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]\to 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
arising from

Proposition

4.4.11
are very special. For instance, we note that on 1-morphism level, the functor

F
assigns both
a
and
b
in
\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]
to an invertible 1-morphism in
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
. In other words,
The functor
F\colon\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]\to 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
admits an extension

(4.64)

where

In a similar vein, recall that any
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category is in particular a lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category, hence also fits into this scheme. These

admits an extension

0.0pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 68.78426pt\raise-50.72157pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 111.80289pt\raise-60.80313pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 127.3381pt\raise-32.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-26.88895pt\raise-65.45503pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.16905pt\raise-65.45503pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 111.80289pt\raise-65.45503pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-40.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 111.80289pt\raise-65.45503pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(4.65)

where
(\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1])[a^{-1},b^{-1},\operatorname{ev}^{-1},\operatorname{coev}^{-1},\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}^{-1},\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}^{-1}]
is the 3-category obtained from

(\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1])[a^{-1},b^{-1}]
by also freely adding inverses to
\operatorname{ev},\operatorname{coev},\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}
and
\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}
. Note that this
implies that
S\dashv S^{R}
is an adjoint equivalence (and in particular that
S
is invertible, with
S^{-1}\cong S^{R}\cong S^{L}
).
In particular, we have
(\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1])[a^{-1},b^{-1},\operatorname{ev}^{-1},\operatorname{coev}^{-1},\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}^{-1},\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}^{-1}]\cong\star
, the final 3-cateogry.
In other words, we have repackaged the fact that an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-category is a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-category

\star\to 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
by providing a more intricate (but equivalent) model for
\star
.

Remark 4.4.13
.

Currently we do not know whether there is a variant of lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories that corresponds precisely to
2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-functors
F\colon\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]\to 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
, rather than such that descend
to a localization of the domain.

5
Lax hermitian fixed points

In this section, we discuss (lax) hermitian fixed points. We start with a discussion in the lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive case and then turn our attention the
the lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive case. After discussing some general theory, we explain how lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories and lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories
are to be understood as lax hermitian fixed points in
\operatorname{Cat}
and
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
, respectively.

5.1
The lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive case

In this section we discuss the notion of a lax hermitian fixed point in a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category.

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
be a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category. A
lax hermitian fixed point
in
\mathcal{C}
is a pair
(a,\theta_{a})
consisting
of an object
a\in\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
\theta_{a}\colon a\to d(a)
satisfying
\theta_{a}=d(\theta_{a})\circ\eta_{a}
.

Remark 5.1.2
.

Lax hermitian fixed points have appeared in the hermitian algebraic K-theory literature before under the name

symmetric form
, see e.g.
[
Sch10
, Section 3]
.

Remark 5.1.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
be a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category. Consider the category
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
whose objects are lax hermitian fixed points
and whose morphisms are those of
\mathcal{C}
. The full subcategory of those pairs
(a,\theta_{a})
for which
\theta_{a}
is an isomorphism is the dagger category
of
Remark

2.1.10
; here, the dagger structure assigns a morphism
X\colon(a,\theta_{a})\to(b,\theta_{b})
to
the morphism
\dagger(X)\colon(b,\theta_{b})\to(a,\theta_{a})
given as the composition

\textstyle{b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{b}}
\textstyle{d(b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d(X)}
\textstyle{d(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{a}^{-1}}
\textstyle{a.}

(5.1)

We call pairs
(a,\theta_{a})
for which
\theta_{a}
is an isomorphism
(honest) hermitian fixed points
. Note that, in passing to the full subcategory of hermitian fixed points it does not matter whether we consider
\mathcal{C}
or
its associated
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category described in
3.2.7
: the invertibility of
\theta_{a}
forces
the invertibility of
\eta_{a}
according to the coherence condition.

Definition 5.1.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},d,\eta,\tau)
be a 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category. A
lax hermitian fixed point
in
\mathcal{B}
is a triple consisting
of an object
a\in\mathcal{C}
, a morphism
\theta_{a}\colon a\to d(a)
, and a 2-morphism
\omega_{a}\colon\theta_{a}\to d(\theta_{a})\circ\eta_{a}
satisfying

(5.2)

where the left hand diagram is filled with
\omega_{a}
while the right hand diagram is filled with
\eta_{\theta_{a}}
,
\tau_{a}
, and
d(\omega_{a})
.

Variant 5.1.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},d,\eta,\tau)
be a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category. A
lax hermitian fixed point
in
\mathcal{B}
is a triple consisting
of an object
a\in\mathcal{C}
, a morphism
\theta_{a}\colon a\to d(a)
, and a 2-morphism
\omega_{a}\colon\eta_{a}\to d(\theta_{a})\circ\theta_{a}
satisfying
a coherence condition involving
d^{3}(a)
similar to
Equation

5.2
.

Remark 5.1.6
.

Lax hermitian fixed points in the sense of
5.1.5
are essentially
the same as volutive adjunctions in the sense of
Definition

3.3.7
.

Example 5.1.7
.

Consider the 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category
(\operatorname{Cat},(-)^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})
. A lax hermitian fixed point in
(\operatorname{Cat},(-)^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})
is
a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category.

Remark 5.1.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},d,\eta,\tau)
be a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category. Consider the 2-category
\hat{\mathcal{B}}
whose objects are lax hermitian fixed points
and whose 1- and 2-morphisms are those of
\mathcal{B}
. The full sub 2-category of those pairs
(a,\theta_{a},\omega_{a})
for which
\theta_{a}
and
\omega_{a}
are
isomorphisms is the 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category of
2.2.10
. We call triples

(a,\theta_{a},\omega_{a})
for which
\theta_{a}
and
\omega_{a}
are invertible
(honest) hermitian fixed points
.

Remark 5.1.9
.

The notion of a lax hermitian fixed point presented in
Definition

5.1.4
and

5.1.5
works more generally for lax 1-, 2- or (1,2)-contravariant

\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-categories.

Lemma 5.1.10
.

Consider the 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category
\operatorname{Adj}
described in
Lemma

3.3.6
.
We claim that
a\in\operatorname{Adj}
admits a lax hermitian fixed point structure, while no object admits an (honest) hermitian fixed point structure.

Proof.

We will construct a lax hermitian fixed point structure for
a\in\operatorname{Adj}
. First, we have the 1-morphism
\theta_{a}:=X\colon a\to F(a)=b
.
Second, we have the 2-morphism
\Pi_{a}:=f\colon\operatorname{id}_{a}\to F(X)\circ X=Y\circ X
. The Zorro moves ensure that this defines a lax hermitian fixed point.
To see that no object admits an (honest) hermitian fixed point structure, it suffices to note that
a
and
b
are not isomorphic.
∎

Remark 5.1.11
.

The lax hermitian fixed point structure on
a\in\operatorname{Adj}
is not unique: a different one is given by the 1-morphism
\theta_{a}^{\prime}:=X\circ Y\circ X\colon a\to F(a)=b

together with the 2-morphism
\Pi_{a}^{\prime}:=f\circ f\colon\operatorname{id}_{a}\to F(X\circ Y\circ X)\circ X=Y\circ X\circ Y\circ X
. In fact, there are infinitely many
such lax hermitian fixed point structures, with the one presented in the proof of
Lemma

5.1.10
being
the “minimal” one. This fact essentially implies
Remark

3.2.4
.

The object
b\in\operatorname{Adj}
does not admit a lax hermitian fixed point structure, to the best of our knowledge. This apparent defect can be lifted by passing
to the walking ambidextrous adjunction, in which
Y
is not only a right adjoint of
X
, but also a left adjoint. The coevaluation of the latter adjunction
then precisely supplies the missing 2-morphism of a lax hermitian fixed point structure on
b\in\operatorname{Adj}
, with 1-morphism given by
\theta_{b}:=Y\colon b\to F(b)=a
.

Construction 5.1.12
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},d,\eta,\tau)
and
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
be 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-categories. According to
Remark

5.1.8
we
may form the 2-categories
\hat{\mathcal{B}}
and
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
whose objects are lax hermitian fixed points, and whose 1- and 2-morphisms are those of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
,
respectively. Moreover, let
(F,\alpha,\Xi)\colon(\mathcal{B},d,\eta,\tau)\to(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
be a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor.
We wish to construct a 2-functor
\hat{\mathcal{B}}\to\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
. To an object
(a,\theta_{a},\omega_{a})\in\hat{\mathcal{B}}
we assign the triple consisting of
(i) the object
F(a)\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
(ii) the 1-morphism
\theta_{F(a)}:=\alpha_{a}\circ F(\theta_{a})\colon F(a)\to d^{\prime}F(a)
(iii) the 2-morphism
\omega_{F(a)}

is constructed from
\alpha_{\theta_{a}},\Xi_{a},
and
\omega_{a}
by appropriately filling the diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 27.67438pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-13.44157pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-27.67438pt\raise-16.16446pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.13611pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}_{F(a)}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.32892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.82887pt\raise-56.3289pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 75.40182pt\raise-26.52753pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.21251pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha_{d(a)}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 100.32127pt\raise-32.32892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 100.32127pt\raise-32.32892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{d^{\prime}Fd(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.27245pt\raise-55.1017pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0228pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d^{\prime}(\alpha_{a})}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.68898pt\raise-56.3289pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-64.96893pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 37.44157pt\raise-64.96893pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{d^{\prime 2}F(a)}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 117.37048pt\raise-64.96893pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(5.3)

One checks that
(F(a),\theta_{F(a)},\omega_{F(a)})
defines a lax hermitian fixed point. On 1- and 2-morphism level our assignment is given simply by
F
.
In summary, 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors descend to the 2-categories of lax hermitian fixed points.

Remark 5.1.13
.

It is evident from
5.1.12
that we could consider lax 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive
2-categories and lax 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-functors instead; at no point did we need the invertibility of any map in sight. For the purposes
of this paper, the given construction is sufficient.

Definition 5.1.14
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
be a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category and let
(a,\theta_{a})
and
(b,\theta_{b})
be lax hermitian fixed points in
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
. A

lax isometry

(a,\theta_{a})\to(b,\theta_{b})
is a morphism
X\colon a\to b
satisfying
d(X)\circ\theta_{b}\circ X=\theta_{a}
.

Remark 5.1.15
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
be a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category, let
(a,\theta_{a})
,
(b,\theta_{b})
, and
(c,\theta_{c})
be lax hermitian fixed points in
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
,
and let
X\colon(a,\theta_{a})\to(b,\theta_{b})
and
Y\colon(b,\theta_{b})\to(c,\theta_{c})
be lax isometries. Then,
Y\circ X\colon(a,\theta_{a})\to(c,\theta_{c})

is a lax isometry as well; indeed, we have

d(Y\circ X)\circ\theta_{c}\circ(Y\circ X)=d(X)\circ d(Y)\circ\theta_{c}\circ Y\circ X=d(X)\circ\theta_{b}\circ X=\theta_{a}.

(5.4)

We also note that
\operatorname{id}_{a}\colon(a,\theta_{a})\to(a,\theta_{a})
is a lax isometry.

We record our findings in the following.

Definition 5.1.16
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
be a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category. We denote the category of lax hermitian fixed points and lax isometries in
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
by

\operatorname{LaxHerm}(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
.

Remark 5.1.17
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)
be a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category. A lax isometry in its associated dagger category is the same as an ordinary isometry.
Indeed, recall that the dagger structure assigns each morphism
X\colon(a,\theta_{a})\to(b,\theta_{b})
to
\dagger(X)=\theta_{a}^{-1}\circ d(X)\circ\theta_{b}
, so that a lax isometry precisely satisfies
\dagger(X)\circ X=\operatorname{id}_{a}
.

Remark 5.1.18
.

Similarly, one defines lax unitaries to be lax isometries which are also invertible.

Lemma 5.1.19
.

Let
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
be a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor. Then,
(F,\alpha)
induces a functor

\operatorname{LaxHerm}(\mathcal{C},d,\eta)\to\operatorname{LaxHerm}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
.

5.2
The lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive case

In this section we discuss the notion of a lax hermitian fixed point in a lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category.

Definition 5.2.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},S)
be a lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category. A
lax hermitian fixed point
in
\mathcal{B}
is a pair
(a,\lambda_{a})
consisting of an object

a\in\mathcal{B}
and a 2-morphism
\lambda_{a}\colon\operatorname{id}_{a}\to S_{a}
.

Remark 5.2.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},S)
be a lax
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category. Consider the 2-category
\hat{\mathcal{B}}
whose objects are lax hermitian fixed points and whose 1- and 2-morphisms
are those of
\mathcal{B}
. The full sub 2-category of those objects for which
\lambda_{a}
is a 2-isomorphism is an
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-dagger 2-category, see

[
CL25
, Section 2.2.3]
; here, the
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-dagger structure has as its component at

X\colon(a,\lambda_{a})\to(b,\lambda_{b})
in
\hat{B}
the 2-isomorphism

\textstyle{X^{RR}\cong X^{RR}\circ\operatorname{id}_{a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\circ\lambda_{a}}
\textstyle{X^{RR}\circ S_{a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{X}}
\textstyle{S_{b}\circ X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{b}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{id}_{b}\circ X\cong X.}

(5.5)

We call pairs
(a,\lambda_{a})
for which
\lambda_{a}
is a 2-isomorphism
(honest)
hermitian fixed points.

Example 5.2.3
.

Recall the
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-category
\operatorname{AmbAdj}
described in
Lemma

4.4.6
. We have seen that the
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive
structure on
\operatorname{AmbAdj}
has identity 1-morphism components. In particular, every object in
\operatorname{AmbAdj}
admits both a lax and an honest hermitian fixed
point structure.

Definition 5.2.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{D},S,\sigma)
be a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-category. A
lax hermitian fixed point
in
\mathcal{D}
is a pair
(a,\lambda_{a})

consisting of an object
a\in\mathcal{B}
and a 2-morphism
\lambda_{a}\colon\operatorname{id}_{a}\to S_{a}
satisfying a compatibility condition with
\sigma
, namely, that
the diagram of 3-morphisms

59.00656pt\raise-64.11223pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern (5.6)

equals the identity 3-morphism on
\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{id}_{a}}
, where the left and right diagrams are filled with the naturality data of the adjunction/inversion data,
the upper diagram is filled with the 3-morphism component
\sigma_{a}
, and the lower diagram is filled with the naturality data of
S
with respect to
\lambda_{a}
.

Remark 5.2.5
.

As in the 2-categorical case, we call a lax hermitian fixed point an (honest) hermitian fixed point if
\lambda_{a}
is a 2-isomorphism.

Example 5.2.6
.

Recall the 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-category
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
from
Example

4.3.3
.
(Lax) hermitian fixed points in
2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
are precisely (lax)
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 2-categories. Indeed,
the object
a\in 2\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{adj}}
corresponds precisely to a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
with adjoints, the 2-morphism

\lambda_{a}\colon\operatorname{id}_{a}\to S_{a}
corresponds precisely to a 2-transformation
\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}\to(-)^{RR}_{\mathcal{B}}
, and the coherence condition of

Equation

5.6
corresponds precisely to
Equation

4.58
.

Example 5.2.7
.

Recall the 2-contravariant
\operatorname{SO(2)}
-volutive 3-category
\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]
from
Corollary

4.4.9
.
Every object in
\operatorname{AmbAdj}[1]
admits the structure of an (honest) hermitian fixed point, hence also the structure of a lax hermitian fixed point.

6
Closed 2-categories

In this section, we discuss (fully) closed symmetric monoidal 2-categories and lax volutive structures. We highlight key difference to the theory
of rigid symmetric monoidal 2-categories, such as the fact that 1-closedness leads only to an oplax 2-functor, whereas having all adjoints leads to an honest
2-functor (which in addition is invertible). A first instance of this phenomenonen we have seen earlier, noting that the functor underlying the canonical
lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure of a closed symmetric monoidal category is only oplax monoidal. We will illustrate our theory at the example of the Morita 2-category
of a sufficiently nice closed symmetric monoidal category, as well as the 2-category of Profunctors.

6.1
Lax 2-functors and 2-transformations

In this section we review the notions of lax 2-functors and 2-transformations for the convenience of the reader, and to establish conventions.

Definition 6.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be 2-categories. A
lax 2-functor

F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
assigns to each object
a\in\mathcal{B}
an object
F(a)\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, to each
1-morphism
X\colon a\to b
in
\mathcal{B}
a 1-morphism
F(X)\colon F(a)\to F(b)
in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, to each 2-morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathcal{B}
a 2-morphism

F(f)\colon F(X)\to F(Y)
in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, to each pair of 1-morphisms
X\colon a\to b
and
Y\colon b\to c
in
\mathcal{B}
a 2-morphism

F_{Y,X}\colon F(Y)\circ F(X)\to F(Y\circ X)
in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, and to each object
a\in\mathcal{B}
a 2-morphism
F_{a}\colon\operatorname{id}_{F(a)}\to F(\operatorname{id}_{a})
in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
,
subject to the coherence conditions of lax associativity and lax (left and right) unity, spelled out in
[
JY20
, Definition 4.1.2]
.

Definition 6.1.2
.

A lax 2-functor is called
normal
(or
unitary
) if each of the 2-morphisms
F_{a}\colon\operatorname{id}_{F(a)}\to F(\operatorname{id}_{a})
is a 2-isomorphism.
If moreover each
F_{Y,X}
is a 2-isomorphism, we call
F
a
pseudofunctor
or simply
2-functor
.

Definition 6.1.3
.

Let
F,G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be lax 2-functors. A
lax 2-transformation

\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow G
consists of 1-morphism components

\alpha_{c}\colon F(c)\to G(c)
for each
c\in\mathcal{C}
and 2-morphism components
\alpha_{X}\colon G(X)\circ\alpha_{c}\Rightarrow\alpha_{d}\circ F(X)
for

X\colon c\to d
a 1-morphism in
\mathcal{C}
satisfying compatibility with units and composition of 1-morphisms, and naturality with respect to 2-morphisms,
spelled out in
[
JY20
, Definition 4.2.1]
.

Definition 6.1.4
.

A lax 2-transformation is called a
pseudonatural transformation
or simply
2-transformation
if each
\alpha_{X}
is a 2-isomorphism.

Variant 6.1.5
.

Both lax 2-functors and lax 2-transformations admit oplax versions, in which the structure 2-morphisms
F_{a},F_{Y,X}
and the 2-morphisms
\alpha_{X}

are reversed, respectively. We emphasize that one may consider oplax 2-transformations between lax 2-functors, as discussed in

[
JY20
, Section 4.3]
(where oplax is called colax).

Remark 6.1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be 2-categories. Then, there is a 2-category
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
whose objects are lax 2-functors, whose 1-morphisms are lax 2-transformations,
and whose 2-morphisms are modifications, see
[
JY20
, Definition 4.4.10 & Theorem 4.4.11]
.

Remark 6.1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
be 2-categories and
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to B^{\prime}
and
G\colon\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
lax 2-functors. Then, we may compose
G
and
F
to obtain a lax
2-functor
G\circ F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, see
[
JY20
, Definition 4.1.26 & Lemma 4.1.29]
. Moreover, there is a category
whose objects are 2-categories and whose morphisms are lax 2-functors, see
[
JY20
, Theorem 4.1.30]
.

Remark 6.1.8
.

For each lax 2-functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, there is a
1-opposite
lax 2-functor
F^{1\operatorname{op}}\colon\mathcal{B}^{1\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}^{1\operatorname{op}}
defined
in
[
JY20
, Example 4.1.10]
. On the other hand, there is a
2-opposite
oplax 2-functor

F^{2\operatorname{op}}\colon\mathcal{B}^{2\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}^{2\operatorname{op}}
, and a
(1,2)-opposite
oplax 2-functor
F^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}\colon\mathcal{B}^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}
.

Remark 6.1.9
.

The 1-categories of 2-categories and lax (respectively oplax) 2-functors are equivalent via the 2-opposite construction.

Remark 6.1.10
.

Note that, by definition, an (op)lax 2-functor
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
induces honest functors on Hom-category level. The (op)laxness is reflected only
in the compability of these induced functors with units and composition.

Similarly, an (op)lax natural transformation between (op)lax 2-functors induces natural transformations between the the induced functors on
Hom-category level; the (op)laxness is again reflected in the compatibility with respect to units and composition.

Given a lax/oplax 2-functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
which induces functors
F_{a,b}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}(F(a),F(b))
, the 2-opposite
oplax/lax 2-functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}^{2\operatorname{op}}\to(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})^{2\operatorname{op}}
induces on the respective Hom-categories the functors

F_{a,b}^{\operatorname{op}}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)^{\operatorname{op}}\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}(F(a),F(b))^{\operatorname{op}}
.

6.2
1-closed 2-categories

In this section we discuss the basic theory of 1-closed 2-categories.

Definition 6.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
a 2-category. We will say that
\mathcal{B}
is
right 1-closed
if for every object
c\in\mathcal{C}
and every 1-morphism
X\colon a\to b
in
\mathcal{B}
,
the functor
(-)\circ X\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(b,c)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,c)
admits a right adjoint. Similarly, we will say that
\mathcal{B}
is
left 1-closed
if
for every object
c\in\mathcal{C}
and every 1-morphism
X\colon a\to b
in
\mathcal{B}
, the functor
X\circ(-)\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(c,a)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(c,b)

admits a right adjoint. We will say that
\mathcal{B}
is
1-closed
if it is both left and right 1-closed.

Example 6.2.2
.

Any 2-category
\mathcal{B}
with left/right adjoints is left/right 1-closed.

Variant 6.2.3
.

If the compostion functors in
Definition

6.2.1
admit left adjoints rather than right adjoints, we call
\mathcal{B}

right/left 1-coclosed
.

Lemma 6.2.4
.

The 1-opposite of a 1-closed 2-category is 1-closed again. The 2-opposite of a 1-closed 2-category is 1-coclosed.

Remark 6.2.5
.

Lemma

6.2.4
suggests that passing to the 2-opposite is not a (classical)
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetry of the (higher) category of 1-closed
2-categories. This is substentially different than in the case of 2-categories (with adjoints). In particular, while 2-categories with adjoints have
more symmetry than ordinary 2-categories, 1-closed 2-categories in contrast have less symmetry.

Remark 6.2.6
.

We wish to spell out some of the data of a right 1-closed 2-category. Let
\mathcal{B}
be a right 1-closed 2-category and
X\colon a\to b
be a 1-morphism.
Then,
(-)\circ X\colon\operatorname{Hom}(b,c)\to\operatorname{Hom}(a,c)
has a right adjoint which we denote by
(-)^{X}\colon\operatorname{Hom}(a,c)\to\operatorname{Hom}(b,c)
. By definition,
we have for any 1-morphism
Z\colon a\to c
a 2-morphism
\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{Z}\colon Z^{X}\circ X\to Z
so that for each
Y\colon b\to c
we have a
natural bijection
\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y,Z^{X})\cong\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y\circ X,Z)
.

Let us now assume that
\mathcal{B}
is a left 1-closed 2-category and
X\colon a\to b
is a 1-morphism.
Then,
X\circ(-)\colon\operatorname{Hom}(c,a)\to\operatorname{Hom}(c,b)
has a right adjoint, denoted
{}^{X}\!(-)\colon\operatorname{Hom}(c,b)\to\operatorname{Hom}(c,a)
. By definition, we have
for any 1-morphism
Z\colon c\to b
a 2-morphism
\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{X}^{Z}\colon X\circ{}^{X}\!(Z)\to Z
so that for each
Y\colon c\to a
we have
a natural bijection
\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y,{}^{X}\!Z)\cong\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(X\circ Y,Z)
.

Construction 6.2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a right 1-closed 2-category and let
a,b,c\in\mathcal{B}
be objects. First, note that the assignment
\operatorname{Hom}(a,b)^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{Hom}(a,c)\to\operatorname{Hom}(b,c)

that assigns
(X,Y)\mapsto Y^{X}
is functorial. We define an oplax 2-functor
\mathcal{J}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}
by setting

\mathcal{J}(a)=a,\mathcal{J}(X)=\operatorname{id}_{a}^{X}
, and
\mathcal{J}(f):=\operatorname{id}_{a}^{f}
for an object
a\in\mathcal{B}
, 1-morphisms
X,Y\colon a\to b
, and a
2-morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathcal{B}
.

For the sake of completeness, we also describe the coherence 2-morphisms
\operatorname{id}^{X}\circ\operatorname{id}^{Y}\to\operatorname{id}^{Y\circ X}
and
\operatorname{id}_{a}\to\operatorname{id}_{a}^{\operatorname{id}_{a}}
in
\mathcal{B}
of our
oplax
3
3

3

Note that oplax functoriality data in
\mathcal{B}^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}
resembles lax functoriality data in
\mathcal{B}
.
2-functor
\mathcal{J}
. The former is constructed
as the preimage of the 2-morphism

\textstyle{\operatorname{id}^{X}\circ\operatorname{id}^{Y}\circ Y\circ X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{Y}\circ\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{id}^{X}\circ X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{X}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{id}}

(6.1)

under the bijection
\hom(\operatorname{id}^{X}\circ\operatorname{id}^{Y},\operatorname{id}^{Y\circ X})\cong\hom(\operatorname{id}^{X}\circ\operatorname{id}^{Y}\circ Y\circ X,\operatorname{id})
and the latter is constructed
as the preimage of the 2-morphism
\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{id}_{a}}
under the bijection
\hom(\operatorname{id}_{a},\operatorname{id}_{a}^{\operatorname{id}_{a}})\cong\hom(\operatorname{id}_{a}\circ\operatorname{id}_{a},\operatorname{id}_{a})\cong\hom(\operatorname{id}_{a},\operatorname{id}_{a})

where we have used the right closedness of
\mathcal{B}
in both cases.

Example 6.2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a closed monoidal category, which we may interpret as a 1-closed 2-category with a single object.
Then, the duality functor
d\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}_{\otimes^{\operatorname{op}}}
is oplax monoidal, hence induces an oplax 2-functor upon delooping.

Remark 6.2.9
.

In the case of a 2-category with right adjoints,
6.2.7
reduces to the adjunction 2-functor

(-)^{R}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}
.

Notation 6.2.10
.

6.2.7
carries over to left 1-closed 2-category with the evident modifications. If we consider a 2-category
which is both left and right 1-closed, we will employ the notation
\mathcal{J}_{L}
and
\mathcal{J}_{R}
to distinguish the two respective oplax 2-functors.

Remark 6.2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a 1-closed 2-category. For any two 1-morphisms
Y\colon a\to b
and
X\colon b\to c
in
\mathcal{B}
, we have 2-morphisms

\textstyle{\operatorname{id}^{\operatorname{id}^{X}}\circ\operatorname{id}^{\operatorname{id}^{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{id}^{\operatorname{id}^{Y}\circ\operatorname{id}^{X}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{id}^{\operatorname{id}^{X\circ Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(6.2)

fitting into the commutative diagram of 2-morphisms

Remark 6.2.12
.

In the case of a 2-category with adjoints, we do have a 2-functor
(-)^{RR}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
.

Variant 6.2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a 1-closed 2-category. We have 2-morphisms

\textstyle{\operatorname{id}^{{}^{X}\!\operatorname{id}}\circ\operatorname{id}^{{}^{Y}\!\operatorname{id}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{id}^{{}^{Y}\!\operatorname{id}\circ^{X}\!\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{id}^{{}^{X\circ Y}\!\operatorname{id}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(6.4)

and similarly for the other possible combinations of
\mathcal{J}_{L}
and
\mathcal{J}_{R}
.

Remark 6.2.14
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a 1-closed 2-category. Let
X\colon a\to b
be a 1-morphism in
\mathcal{B}
. We have a canonical 2-morphism

X\to\operatorname{id}^{{}^{X}\!\operatorname{id}}

(6.5)

induced by the 2-morphism
\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{X}\colon X\circ^{X}\!\operatorname{id}\to\operatorname{id}
. Since the assignment
X\mapsto\operatorname{id}^{{}^{X}\!\operatorname{id}}

does not necessarily define an (op)lax 2-functor, it is not reasonable to ask whether the 2-morphisms we described here assemble into an (op)lax 2-transformation.
Completely analogously, there is a 2-morphism
X\to^{\operatorname{id}^{X}}\!\operatorname{id}
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a 2-morphism. We claim that the diagram of 2-morphisms

3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern
