31.75348pt\raise-35.1745pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.75348pt\raise-35.1745pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{id}^{{}^{Y}\!\operatorname{id}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(6.6)

is commutative. Indeed, the two 2-morphisms
X\to\operatorname{id}^{{}^{Y}\!\operatorname{id}}
induce two 2-morphisms
X\to^{Y}\!\operatorname{id}
which coincide by the commutativity of the
diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.94064pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.94064pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\circ^{Y}\!\operatorname{id}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.53955pt\raise 6.24501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.24501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\circ^{f}\!\operatorname{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.94064pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-14.75pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f\circ\operatorname{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.94064pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\circ^{X}\!\operatorname{id}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.22087pt\raise-15.4839pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.22087pt\raise-25.46782pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-17.41983pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y\circ^{Y}\!\operatorname{id}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.65883pt\raise-27.90254pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{Y}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.6653pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 51.6653pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{id}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(6.7)

In other words, the 2-morphisms
X\to\operatorname{id}^{{}^{X}\!\operatorname{id}}
are natural in
X
.

Remark 6.2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a right 1-closed 2-category together with an equivalence of 2-categories
d\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{2\operatorname{op}}
.
Then,
\mathcal{B}
is left 1-closed with

{}^{X}\!\operatorname{id}=d^{-1}(\operatorname{id}^{d(X)})

(6.8)

for any 1-morphism
X\colon a\to b
in
\mathcal{B}
.

6.3
0-closed 2-categories

In this section we consider the basic theory of 0-closed monoidal categories.

Definition 6.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a monoidal 2-category. We will say that
\mathcal{B}
is
right 0-closed
if the 2-functor
(-)\otimes a\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
admits a right adjoint
for every
a\in\mathcal{B}
. Similarly, we will say that
\mathcal{B}
is
left 0-closed
if the 2-functor
a\otimes(-)\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
admits a right adjoint
for every
a\in\mathcal{B}
. We will say that
\mathcal{B}
is
0-closed
if it is both left and right 0-closed.

We will say that a symmetric monoidal 2-category
\mathcal{B}
is
0-closed
if its underlying monoidal 2-category is right (equivalently: left) 0-closed.

Remark 6.3.2
.

There are weaker versions of 0-closedness for monoidal 2-categories, as there are weaker versions of adjunctions between 2-functors. These will be of
no concern in the following.

Definition 6.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a 2-category. A
lax 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure
on
\mathcal{B}
consists of a 2-functor
d\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{1\operatorname{op}}
, a
2-transformation
\eta\colon\operatorname{id}\to d^{1\operatorname{op}}\circ d
, and an invertible modification
\tau\colon(\operatorname{id}\circ\eta^{1\operatorname{op}})\bullet(\eta\circ\operatorname{id})\to\operatorname{id}

satisfying the evident coherence condition over the fourfold product of
d
.

Remark 6.3.4
.

Despite the choice of terminology, a lax 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure consists entirely of pseudofunctors, transformations, etc.
As in the 1-categorical case, the terminology is motivated as a separation from honest
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structures and to emphasize the (op)lax monoidality of
the underlying functor.

The proof of the following assertion is similar to its 1-categorical analogue and its rigid 2-categorical counterpart; we will be brief.

Proposition 6.3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a 0-closed symmetric monoidal 2-category. Then,
\mathcal{B}
admits a lax 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.

Proof.

First, we note that we have a 2-functor
\mathcal{B}^{1\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B},(a,b)\mapsto b^{a}
. To a 1-morphism
(X,Y)\colon(a,b)\to(\hat{a},\hat{b})
, it assigns
the 1-morphism
Y^{X}\colon b^{a}\to\hat{b}^{\hat{a}}
corresponding under the equivalence of categories
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(b^{a},\hat{b}^{\hat{a}})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(b^{a}\otimes\hat{a},\hat{b})
to the 1-morphism

\textstyle{b^{a}\otimes\hat{a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\otimes X}
\textstyle{b^{a}\otimes a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{a}^{b}}
\textstyle{b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Y}
\textstyle{\hat{b}.}

(6.9)

To a 2-morphism
(f,g)\colon(X,Y)\to(\overline{X},\overline{Y})
, it assigns the 2-morphism
g^{f}\colon Y^{X}\to\overline{Y}^{\overline{X}}

corresponding to the 2-morphism

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.26605pt\raise-37.97778pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.42221pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\otimes\overline{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.23138pt\raise-31.72223pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 44.23138pt\raise-31.72223pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b^{a}\otimes a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.582pt\raise-38.46724pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.74501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{a}^{b}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 104.64659pt\raise-31.72223pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 104.64659pt\raise-31.72223pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 124.43826pt\raise-37.56111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{Y}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 144.9383pt\raise-31.72223pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 144.9383pt\raise-31.72223pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hat{b}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(6.10)

One checks that this defines a 2-functor.
We now define a 2-functor
d\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{1\operatorname{op}}
by assigning
a\mapsto 1^{a},X\mapsto 1^{X},f\mapsto 1^{f}
, that is, we consider the composition

\mathcal{B}\cong\mathcal{B}\times\{\star\}\to\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}^{1\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}^{1\operatorname{op}}
where the last 2-functor is the 1-opposite of the one constructed above and
the 2-functor
\{\star\}\to\mathcal{B}^{1\operatorname{op}}
is determined by its image,
1
.

Next, we wish to describe a 2-transformation
\eta\colon\operatorname{id}\to d^{1\operatorname{op}}\circ d
. Its 1-morphism component at
a\in\mathcal{B}
is given by
the 1-morphism
\eta_{a}\colon a\to 1^{1^{a}}
corresponding to the 1-morphism
a\otimes 1^{a}\cong 1^{a}\otimes a\to 1
where we have used the braiding of
\mathcal{B}
.

Next, we wish to describe a modification
\tau\colon(\operatorname{id}\circ\eta^{1\operatorname{op}})\bullet(\eta\circ\operatorname{id})\to\operatorname{id}
. Its 2-morphism component at
a\in\mathcal{B}

is a 2-isomorphism
1^{\eta}_{a}\circ\eta_{1^{a}}\to\operatorname{id}_{1^{a}}
which is constructed completely analogously to the 1-categorical case (in which
this is a coherence condition). One checks that this satisfies the required coherence condition.
∎

6.4
Fully closed 2-categories

In this section we study the basic theory of (fully) closed monoidal 2-categories.

Definition 6.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a (symmetric) monoidal 2-category. We will say that
\mathcal{B}
is
(fully) closed
if
\mathcal{B}
is both 0- and 1-closed.

In the following we wish to understand the interaction between the 0- and 1-closedness (oplax) 2-functors
d
and
\mathcal{J}
on a fully closed monoidal
2-category. We start by working in slightly more generality.

Construction 6.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be right 1-closed 2-categories and let
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be a 2-functor. Then, we obtain an oplax 2-transformation

\xi^{F}\colon\mathcal{J}_{R}\circ F\to F^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}\circ\mathcal{J}_{R}
as follows: the 1-morphism components are trivial,
and the 2-morphism component at
X\colon a\to b
is given by the image of the 2-morphism
\operatorname{ev}_{X}\colon\operatorname{id}^{X}\circ X\to\operatorname{id}
under the map

\textstyle{\hom(\operatorname{id}^{X}\circ X,\operatorname{id})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\hom(F(\operatorname{id}^{X})\circ F(X),\operatorname{id})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{-1}}
\textstyle{\hom(F(\operatorname{id}^{X}),\operatorname{id}^{F(X)})}

(6.11)

induced by
F
and the adjunction defining the right 1-closed structure. In other words, the component is given by

\xi^{F}_{X}:=\psi^{-1}(F(\operatorname{ev}_{X}))\colon F(\operatorname{id}^{X})\to\operatorname{id}^{F(X)}
(where we have left the structure 2-morphism of the 2-functor
F
implicit).
Since the 1-morphism components of this 2-transformation are trivial, it can equivalently be understood as a lax 2-transformation

F^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}\circ\mathcal{J}_{R}\to\mathcal{J}_{R}\circ F
which we denote by the same symbol.

Remark 6.4.3
.

The construction of the 2-morphisms
\xi^{F}_{X}
in
6.4.2
is completely
analogous under the weaker assumption that
F
is a lax 2-functor. In this case however,
F^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}
is an oplax 2-functor so that
the composition of
F
with
\mathcal{J}_{R}
is not (globally) well-defined. This is similar to the phenomenona observed in

Section

6.2
.

Corollary 6.4.4
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a (fully) closed monoidal 2-category. We have an oplax 2-transformation
(\mathcal{J}_{R})^{1\operatorname{op}}\circ d\to d^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}\circ\mathcal{J}_{R}
.

6.5
A local perspective on closedness

In this section we develop a local perspective on closedness. We start with a motivation.

Remark 6.5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a 1-closed symmetric monoidal 2-category. We have observed earlier that the oplax 2-functor
\mathcal{J}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}

induces for each pair of objects
a,b\in\mathcal{B}
a functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(b,a)^{\operatorname{op}}
. While the 2-opposite

\mathcal{J}^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}\colon\mathcal{B}^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}
is a lax 2-functor, so that it cannot immediately be composed with
\mathcal{J}
, it induces
a functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(b,a)^{\operatorname{op}}\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)
so that we may compose these two (op)lax 2-functors at least locally. The primary intuition
behind the constructions in this section is that this local perspective is sufficient for some purposes.

Construction 6.5.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},d,\eta,\tau)
be a lax 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category. Then, the full sub 2-category on those objects
a\in\mathcal{B}
for which
\eta_{a}
is
a 1-isomorphism inherits a 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure. This 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive 2-category we can then turn into a
1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category
\hat{B}
by applying the construction of
[
CL25
, Section 2.3.3]
.
To be more explicit, the objects of the 2-category
\hat{B}
are triples
(a,\theta_{a},\omega_{a})
consisting of an object
a\in\hat{B}
, a 1-isomorphism

\theta_{a}\colon a\to d(a)
, and a 2-isomorphism
\omega_{a}\colon d(\theta_{a})\circ\eta_{a}\to\theta_{a}
satisfying a coherence condition over
d^{3}(a)
, for
which we refer to
[
CL25
, Section 2.3.3]
. The 1- and 2-morphisms of
\hat{B}
are the same as those of
\mathcal{B}
.
The functor underlying the
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger structure assigns to a 1-morphism
X\colon(a,\theta_{a},\omega_{a})\to(b,\theta_{b},\omega_{b})
the 1-morphism
given by

\textstyle{b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{b}}
\textstyle{d(b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d(X)}
\textstyle{d(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{a}^{-1}}
\textstyle{a.}

(6.12)

Lemma 6.5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a fully closed symmetric monoidal 2-category. The 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category constructed by combining

Proposition

6.3.5
and
6.5.2

is 1-closed.

Proof.

This follows from the observation that the Hom-categories and compositions of
\hat{B}
described in
6.5.2

are the same as those of
\mathcal{B}
.
∎

Construction 6.5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a 1-closed 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category. In the following we will make extensive use of
Remark

6.1.10
.
By composition we obtain an oplax 2-functor
d\circ\mathcal{J}_{R}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{2\operatorname{op}}

which induces a functor
\overline{d}_{a,b}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)^{\operatorname{op}}
for each pair of objects
a,b\in\mathcal{B}
. On the other hand, the
2-opposite
(d\circ\mathcal{J}_{R})^{2\operatorname{op}}\colon\mathcal{B}^{2\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}
(which is a lax 2-functor) induces a functor

\overline{d}_{a,b}^{\operatorname{op}}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)^{\operatorname{op}}\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)
so that we may consider the composition

\overline{d}_{a,b}^{\operatorname{op}}\circ\overline{d}_{a,b}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b).

(6.13)

Now, by
Remark

6.2.15
, we compute

(\overline{d}_{a,b}^{\operatorname{op}}\circ\overline{d}_{a,b})(X)=^{\operatorname{id}^{X}}\!\operatorname{id}

(6.14)

and similar for 2-morphisms. In particular, we have a natural transformation

\overline{\eta}_{a,b}\colon\operatorname{id}\to\overline{d}_{a,b}^{\operatorname{op}}\circ\overline{d}_{a,b}
whose component at
X\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)
is given by
the canonical morphism
X\to^{\operatorname{id}^{X}}\!\operatorname{id}
described in
Remark

6.2.14
.

Lemma 6.5.5
.

In the situation of
6.5.4
,
the natural transformation
\overline{\eta}_{a,b}\colon\operatorname{id}\to\overline{d}_{a,b}^{\operatorname{op}}\circ\overline{d}_{a,b}
satisfies

\overline{d}_{a,b}((\overline{\eta}_{a,b})_{X})\circ(\overline{\eta}_{a,b})_{\overline{d}_{a,b}(X)}=\operatorname{id}_{\overline{d}_{a,b}(X)}
for all

X\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)
. In particular,
(\overline{d}_{a,b},\overline{\eta}_{a,b})
defines a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b)
.

Proof.

Essentially the same as in the non-lax case.
∎

Construction 6.5.6
.

In the situation of
Lemma

6.5.5
, let
a,b,c
be objects in
\mathcal{B}
and consider the composition functor

\operatorname{Hom}(b,c)\times\operatorname{Hom}(a,b)\to\operatorname{Hom}(a,c)
in
\mathcal{B}
. Both sides carry lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structures, the former of which is given as the product (cf.

Definition

3.2.23
) of the ones on
\operatorname{Hom}(a,b)
and
\operatorname{Hom}(b,c)
. We wish to construct a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure
on the composition functor. First, recall that the functors underlying the lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structures assign
(Y,X)\in\operatorname{Hom}(b,c)\times\operatorname{Hom}(a,b)

to
(d(\operatorname{id}^{Y}),d(\operatorname{id}^{X}))
and
Y\circ X\in\operatorname{Hom}(a,c)
to
d(\operatorname{id}^{Y\circ X})
, respectively. By definition, we need to construct a natural transformation
fitting into the diagram

(6.15)

The component of
\alpha
at

we define to be the canonical 2-morphism

\textstyle{d(\operatorname{id}^{Y})\circ d(\operatorname{id}^{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{d(\operatorname{id}^{X}\circ\operatorname{id}^{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d(\operatorname{id}^{Y\circ X}).}

(6.16)

expressing the (oplax) functoriality of
d
and
\mathcal{J}
. One checks that this defines a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on the composition functor.

Let us also construct for each object
a\in\mathcal{B}
a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor
\star\to\operatorname{Hom}(a,a)
. The underlying functor we may take to be the constant
functor with value
\operatorname{id}_{a}\in\operatorname{Hom}(a,a)
. The lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure is given by the natural transformation fitting in the diagram

18.95142pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.95142pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.95142pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}(a,a)^{\operatorname{op}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(6.17)

assigning the unique object of
\star
to the canonical 2-morphism

\operatorname{id}_{a}\to d(\operatorname{id}^{\operatorname{id}_{a}})

(6.18)

expressing the (oplax) functoriality of
d
and
\mathcal{J}
.

We deduce the following result.

Proposition 6.5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a 1-closed 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category. Then,
\mathcal{B}
is enriched in lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories.

Remark 6.5.8
.

While categories enriched in
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories are the same as 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-categories
(see
Remark

2.2.11
), we are not aware of a result
like this identifying categories enriched in lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories with some flavour of “classical” dagger structure. We stress again that
simply asking only for lax ingredients in the definition of a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive/dagger structure leads to immediate problems, due to the
fact that the 2-opposite changes the variance.

As a consequence of
Proposition

6.5.7
, we may perform the following.

Construction 6.5.9
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a 1-closed 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category. Since
\mathcal{B}
is enriched in lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories, we may locally pass to
the categories of lax hermitian fixed points and lax isometries. This procedure is compatible with compsitions and units by the functoriality of
passing to
\operatorname{LaxHerm}
as well as
Proposition

6.5.7
. Explicitly, we may associate to the lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive category

(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b),\overline{d}_{a,b},\overline{\eta}_{a,b})
its category of lax hermitian fixed points

\operatorname{LaxHerm}((\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b),\overline{d}_{a,b},\overline{\eta}_{a,b}))
. Here, an object in this category consists
of a 1-morphism
X\colon a\to b
together with a 2-morphism
\theta_{X}\colon X\to\overline{d}_{a,b}(X)
satisfying
\overline{d}_{a,b}(\theta_{X})\circ(\overline{\eta}_{a,b})_{X}=\theta_{X}
, and morphisms are lax isometries. To see the compatibility with compositions and units explicitly, let

(X,\theta_{X})
be a lax hermitian fixed point in
(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b),\overline{d}_{a,b},\overline{\eta}_{a,b})
and
let
(Y,\theta_{Y})
be a lax hermitian fixed point in
(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(b,c),\overline{d}_{b,c},\overline{\eta}_{b,c})
. We claim that
Y\circ X
admits the
structure of a lax hermitian fixed point in
(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,c),\overline{d}_{a,c},\overline{\eta}_{a,c})
. Indeed, we have a 2-morphism

\textstyle{Y\circ X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{Y}\circ\theta_{X}}
\textstyle{d(\operatorname{id}^{Y})\circ d(\operatorname{id}^{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{d(\operatorname{id}^{X}\circ\operatorname{id}^{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d(\operatorname{id}^{Y\circ X}).}

(6.19)

Similarly, for any object
a
the identity 1-morphism
\operatorname{id}_{a}
admits the structure of a lax hermitian fixed point given by
the canonical 2-morphism
\operatorname{id}_{a}\to d(\operatorname{id}^{\operatorname{id}_{a}})
.

Remark 6.5.10
.

It is evident from the construction that the composition of two (honest) hermitian fixed points does not need to be an (honest) hermitian fixed point,
as the last morphism in the construction is generically non-invertible (as part of the oplax functoriality data of
\mathcal{J}
). This is illustrated further
in
Section

7.4
. On a structural level, this is a reflection of the fact that lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functors between lax

\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories do not induce (
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive/dagger) functors between the associated
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive/dagger categories.

Remark 6.5.11
.

Summarizing the results of this section, from each fully closed symmetric monoidal 2-category we may extract a 1-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category
enriched in lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories, whose lax hermitian fixed points we may study locally.

6.6
Example: Morita 2-categories

In this section, we discuss duality/closedness in Morita 2-categories. We first establish some notation, loosely following
[
DLRS25
]
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a monoidal category. Let
A,B,C
be monoids in
\mathcal{C}
and let
M
be an
A
-
B
-bimodule and
N
be a
B
-
C
-bimodule.
The
balanced tensor product
of
M
and
N
over
B
is the coequalizer (if it exists)

M\otimes_{B}N:=\operatorname{coeq}\left(M\otimes B\otimes N\rightrightarrows M\otimes N\right)

(6.20)

where the two maps are induced by the left and right
B
-actions on
M
and
N
, respectively, supressing associators. We will say that a monoidal category

\mathcal{C}
admits (a calculus of) balanced tensor products if all balanced tensor products exist and the respective coequalizers are preserved by the monoidal
structure, see
[
DLRS25
, Definition 2.7]
. We then obtain a 2-category
4
4

4

Recall that we use the term 2-category synonymously for bicategory.
In
[
DLRS25
]
an
(\infty,2)
-category of monoids modeled by a 2-complicial set is constructed.

\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
whose objects are monoids, whose 1-morphisms
are bimodules, whose 2-morphisms are intertwiners, and whose composition of 1-morphisms is given by the balanced tensor product (with the induced bimodule actions).

Assuming now in addition that
\mathcal{C}
is a symmetric monoidal category, the 2-category
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
inherits a symmetric monoidal structure with respect to
which every object
A
has a dual, given by the opposite algebra
A^{\operatorname{op}}
whose multiplication is that of
A
precomposed with the symmetric braiding.

Construction 6.6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a right closed monoidal category. Let
A
and
B
be monoids in
\mathcal{C}
, let
M
be a left
A
-module and
let
N
be a left
B
-module in
\mathcal{C}
. We will abuse notation in the following and denote underlying objects of
M
and
N
in
\mathcal{C}
by the same symbol.
By assumption of right closedness, there is an object
M^{N}
in
\mathcal{C}
together with a morphism
\operatorname{ev}_{N}^{M}\colon M^{N}\otimes N\to M
such that for every other object

P\in\mathcal{C}
the induced map
\psi\colon\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(P,M^{N})\to\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(P\otimes N,M)
is a natural bijection. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(A\otimes M^{N},M^{N})

\displaystyle\cong\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(A\otimes M^{N}\otimes N,M)

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(M^{N}\otimes B,M^{N})

\displaystyle\cong\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(M^{N}\otimes B\otimes N,M)

B
-bimodule
(M^{N},\lambda_{M^{N}},\rho_{M^{N}})
.

Construction 6.6.2
.

In the situation of
6.6.1
, asumme in addition that we are given a monoid
C
in
\mathcal{C}
and

M
and
N
carry right
C
-actions so that
M
is an
A
-
C
-bimodule and
N
is a
B
-
C
-bimodule. We wish to construct two morphisms of the form

M^{N}\rightrightarrows M^{N\otimes C}

(6.24)

Invoking the right closedness, we may use the bijection
\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(M^{N},M^{N\otimes C})\cong\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(M^{N}\otimes N\otimes C,M)
so that it suffices
to construct two elements in the latter set. The two maps we choose are

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 31.79297pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-31.79297pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 28.37277pt\raise 6.72002pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 59.10548pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M\otimes C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 100.83086pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho_{M}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 123.29428pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 123.29428pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M^{N}\otimes N\otimes C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.04135pt\raise-7.80116pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\otimes\rho_{N}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 55.79297pt\raise-13.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 55.79297pt\raise-13.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M^{N}\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 100.68312pt\raise-7.19225pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.71446pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{N}^{M}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 123.29428pt\raise-13.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 123.29428pt\raise-13.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(6.25)

Given these two maps, we may ask whether the respective equalizer

M^{N}_{C}:=\operatorname{eq}(M^{N}\rightrightarrows M^{N\otimes C})

(6.26)

exists in the category of
A
-
B
-bimodules in
\mathcal{C}
.

Construction 6.6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a right closed monoidal category,
A,B
and
C
monoids in
\mathcal{C}
,
M
an
A
-
C
-bimodule, and
N
a
B
-
C
-bimodule.
Assume the balanced tensor product
M^{N}\otimes_{B}N
and the object
M^{N}_{C}
exists.
For every
A
-
B
-bimodule
P
, we wish to construct a (natural) bijection

\textstyle{\hom_{A,B}(P,M^{N}_{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hom_{A,C}(P\otimes_{B}N,M),}

(6.27)

recalling the (natural) bijection
\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(P,M^{N})\cong\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(P\otimes N,M)
following from the closedness assumption on
\mathcal{C}
.
First, we note that we have a map
\hom_{A,B}(P,M^{N}_{C})\to\hom_{A,B}(P,M^{N})
induced by the equalizer map. Second, we have a map

\textstyle{\hom_{A,B}(P,M^{N})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(P,M^{N})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(P\otimes N,M)}

(6.28)

whose image we wish to understand. First, it is evident that each element in the image will be an intertwiner of left
A
-modules. Second, we
note for every intertwiner of left
B
-modules
f\colon P\to M^{N}
, we have a commutative diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.57806pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 45.75066pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P\otimes B\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.2527pt\raise-9.62807pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\otimes\lambda_{N}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.77208pt\raise-24.78947pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.4832pt\raise-14.77193pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f\otimes\operatorname{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.4832pt\raise-23.21053pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 102.97612pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho_{P}\otimes\operatorname{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 133.56093pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 133.56093pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P\otimes 151.13899pt\raise-14.77193pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f\otimes\operatorname{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 151.13899pt\raise-23.21053pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-17.57806pt\raise-32.12279pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\otimes N}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.75279pt\raise-53.40054pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f\otimes\operatorname{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.59598pt\raise-56.12279pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 41.57806pt\raise-32.12279pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M^{N}\otimes B\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.4832pt\raise-47.28944pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\otimes\lambda_{N}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.4832pt\raise-56.12279pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 98.90405pt\raise-25.46724pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.20555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho_{M^{N}}\otimes\operatorname{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 129.38834pt\raise-32.12279pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 129.38834pt\raise-32.12279pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M^{N}\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 111.70035pt\raise-52.71999pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 94.51056pt\raise-56.12279pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-65.03505pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 51.73254pt\raise-65.03505pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M^{N}\otimes N}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 148.13899pt\raise-65.03505pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(6.29)

where the left diagram commutes by the interchange law, the right upper square commutes by assumption of
f
being an intertwiner of left
B
-modules,
and the lower triangle commutes by definition of the right
B
-action on
M^{N}
. In particular, the two outer composite maps in the diagram

P\otimes B\otimes N\rightrightarrows M

(6.30)

are equal. By the universal property of the balanced tensor product,
f
hence induces a map
P\otimes_{B}N\to M
. Hence, we obtain a bijection

\hom_{A,B}(P,M^{N})\cong\hom_{A}(P\otimes_{B}N,M).

(6.31)

Finally, we wish to understand the image of the composition

\textstyle{\hom_{A,B}(P,M^{N}_{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hom_{A,B}(P,M^{N})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\hom_{A}(P\otimes_{B}N,M)}

(6.32)

By the universal property of
M^{N}_{C}
, the image of the fitst map consists precisely of those intertwiners
P\to N^{M}
for which postcomposition
with the two maps
M^{N}\rightrightarrows M^{N\otimes C}
coincides. By construction of these maps, we may characterize the image of the composite
map
\hom_{A,B}(P,M^{N}_{C})\to\hom_{A}(P\otimes_{B}N,M)
as the interwiners of right
C
-modules. All together, we have constructed a bijection

\textstyle{\hom_{A,B}(P,M^{N}_{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hom_{A,C}(P\otimes_{B}N,M),}

(6.33)

which is natural by the closedness of
\mathcal{C}
(implying naturality of
\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(P,M^{N})\cong\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(P\otimes N,M)
), the universal property
of the balanced tensor product, and the universal property of
6.6.2
.

We deduce the following result from our discussion so far.

Theorem 6.6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a right closed monoidal category which admits equalizers and (a calculus of) balanced tensor products. Then, the
2-category
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
is right 1-closed. If
\mathcal{C}
is moreover closed symmetric monoidal, then
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
is 1-closed and has duals for objects.

Remark 6.6.5
.

Theorem

6.6.4
holds under slightly weaker assumptions on the existence of (co)limits: it suffices that
\mathcal{C}
admits
(a calculus of) balanced tensor products and intertwiner mapping objects in the sense of
6.6.2
.

Lemma 6.6.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a left and right closed monoidal category which admits coequalizers. Then
\mathcal{C}
admits (a calculus of) balanced tensor products.

Proof.

By assumption,
\mathcal{C}
admits coequalizers hence in particular balanced tensor products. It remains to show that the functors

a\otimes(-),(-)\otimes a\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
preserve balanced tensor products for any
a\in\mathcal{C}
.
By assumption
\mathcal{C}
is left and right closed, so that both these functors have right adjoints, hence preserve (small) colimits and in particular coequalizers.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 6.6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category with (co)equalizers. Then,
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
is a symmetric monoidal 1-closed 2-category with duals.

Proof.

Combine
Theorem

6.6.4
and
Lemma

6.6.6
.
∎

Example 6.6.8
.

The 2-category
\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
of complete Bornological algebras is a symmetric monoidal 1-closed 2-category with duals.

Example 6.6.9
.

The 2-category
\operatorname{Mor}(\operatorname{Ch}(R))
of dg-algebras over a commutative ring
R
is a symmetric monoidal 1-closed 2-category with duals.

Lemma 6.6.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be closed symmetric monoidal categorues with (co)equalizers and let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric monoidal functor which preserves
(co)equalizers. Then,
F
induces a symmetric monoidal 2-functor
\operatorname{Mor}(F)\colon\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{D})
.

Corollary 6.6.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category with (co)equalizers and let
K\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal involution. Then,

K
induces a symmetric monoidal involution
\operatorname{Mor}(K)\colon\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

By assumption
K
admits an inverse, hence admits both a left and a right adjoint, hence preserves small limits and colimits. The claim then follows
from
Lemma

6.6.10
.
∎

Example 6.6.12
.

The cocomplete closed symmetric monoidal category
c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
admits a symmetric monoidal involution
\overline{(-)}\colon c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}\to c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
which
assigns each complete Bornological vector space
V
to its complex conjugate
\overline{V}
. It induces a symmetric monoidal involution

\operatorname{Mor}(\overline{(-)})\colon\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})\to\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
which assigns each complete Bornological algebra
A
to its complex conjugate
\overline{A}
.

6.7
Example: Profunctors

In this section we study the theory of profunctors from the point of view of fully closed symmetric monoidal 2-categories.

Definition 6.7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be categories. A
profunctor

\mathcal{C}\nrightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a functor
F\colon\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Set}
.

Remark 6.7.2
.

Profunctors are also called
distributors
or
correspondences
.

Remark 6.7.3
.

Profunctors
F\colon\mathcal{C}\nrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
G\colon\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\nrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
can be composed via a (co)end, i.e. we define the
composition
G\circ F\colon\mathcal{C}\nrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
to be

(G\circ F)(c^{\prime\prime},c):=\int^{c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}G(c^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime})\times F(c^{\prime},c)

(6.34)

For more details and background on profunctors, we refer to
[
Lor21
, Section 5]
.
Categories, profunctors, and natural transformations form a double category whose horizontal 2-category we denote by
\operatorname{Prof}
. The identity
1-morphism of an object
\mathcal{C}
is the profunctor
\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathcal{C}\nrightarrow\mathcal{C}
given by the functor
\mathcal{C}(-,-)\colon\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Set}
.
Every functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
induces two profunctors, namely,
\mathcal{D}(1,F)\colon\mathcal{C}\nrightarrow\mathcal{D},\;\mathcal{D}(1,F)(d,c)=\mathcal{D}(d,F(c))

and
\mathcal{D}(F,1)\colon\mathcal{D}\nrightarrow\mathcal{C},\;\mathcal{D}(F,1)(c,d)=\mathcal{D}(F(c),d)
; moreover,
\mathcal{D}(F,1)\dashv\mathcal{D}(1,F)
. In other words,
these two constructions of
representable
profunctors are the companions and conjoints in the double category.
The two embeddings of
\operatorname{Cat}
into
\operatorname{Prof}
discussed above are called the
co-
and
contravariant embeddings
.

Remark 6.7.4
.

The cartesian product (together with the evident braiding) equips
\operatorname{Prof}
with the structure of a symmetric monoidal 2-category.

Lemma 6.7.5
.

Every object
\mathcal{C}
in
\operatorname{Prof}
is dualizible, with the (left) dual given by the opposite category
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
. In particular
\operatorname{Prof}
admits a 1-contravariant

\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.

Proof.

We construct evaluation and coevaluation profunctors witnessing the opposite category
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
as a dual of
\mathcal{C}
.
Consider the profunctor
\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\nrightarrow 1
which is given by the functor

\textstyle{1^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\cong\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}(-,-)}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Set}}

(6.35)

i.e. the identity profunctor suitably reinterpreted. Similarly, we consider the profunctor

\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}\colon 1\nrightarrow\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
given by the functor

\textstyle{(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}})^{\operatorname{op}}\times 1\cong\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}(-,-)}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Set}}

(6.36)

which is also the identity profunctor suitably reinterpreted. To see that the Zorro moves are satisfied, we simply note that

\int^{c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}(c^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime})\times\mathcal{C}(c^{\prime},c)\cong\mathcal{C}(c^{\prime\prime},c)

(6.37)

according to the ninja Yoneda lemma, see
[
Lor21
, Proposition 2.2.1]
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.7.6
.

We wish to spell out the action of the dualization 2-functor
(-)^{\operatorname{op}}\colon\operatorname{Prof}\to\operatorname{Prof}^{1\operatorname{op}}
on 1- and 2-morphisms.
Let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\nrightarrow\mathcal{D}
be a profunctor. Then,

F^{\operatorname{op}}\colon\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{op}}\nrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
is given by the functor

\textstyle{F^{\operatorname{op}}\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Set}}

(6.38)

where in the first step we have used the symmetric braiding on
\operatorname{Prof}
. Explicitly,
F^{\operatorname{op}}(c,d)=F(d,c)
. The action of the dualization
functor on 2-morphisms is then given by sending a natural transformation
\alpha\colon F\to G
to the natural transformation

\alpha^{\operatorname{op}}\colon F^{\operatorname{op}}\to G^{\operatorname{op}}
given by

(6.39)

explicitly, its component at
(c,d)\in\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{op}}
is given by
\alpha^{\operatorname{op}}_{(c,d)}:=\alpha_{(d,c)}
. We note that,
since
(-)^{\operatorname{op}}
squares to the identity strictly, the higher coherence data of our
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure is trivial.

Remark 6.7.7
.

In this remark wish to compare the dualization 2-functor
(-)^{\operatorname{op}}\colon\operatorname{Prof}\to\operatorname{Prof}^{1\operatorname{op}}
to the 2-functor
(-)^{\operatorname{op}}\colon\operatorname{Cat}\to\operatorname{Cat}^{2\operatorname{op}}
.
There is a commutative diagram of 2-functors

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Cat}^{2\operatorname{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.50005pt\raise-32.04222pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.50005pt\raise-32.04222pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.50005pt\raise-32.04222pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Prof}^{1\operatorname{op}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(6.40)

where the horizontal arrows are the co- and contravariant embeddings of
\operatorname{Cat}
into
\operatorname{Prof}
. This follows immediately from our explicit descriptions.

The following is a useful characterization of the Cauchy/idempotent completion, presented e.g. in
[
BD86
]
.

Lemma 6.7.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (small) category. Then, the following are equivalent

(i)

\mathcal{C}
is Cauchy complete, and

(ii)

for every (small) category
\mathcal{D}
a profunctor
\mathcal{D}\nrightarrow\mathcal{C}
has a left adjoint if and only if it is representable (that is,
induced by a functor
F\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
via
Remark

6.7.3
).

Remark 6.7.9
.

The 2-category
\operatorname{Prof}
of categories, profunctors, and natural transformations is right 1-closed, see
[
Bén73
, Proposition 4.3]
.
Given 1-morphisms
X\colon\mathcal{C}\nrightarrow\mathcal{D}
and
Y\colon\mathcal{C}\nrightarrow\mathcal{E}
in
\operatorname{Prof}
, that is, functors

X\colon\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Set}
and
Y\colon\mathcal{E}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Set}
, the profunctor
Y^{X}\colon\mathcal{E}^{\operatorname{op}}\times D\to\operatorname{Set}

is defined on objects as the equalizer of the diagram

\Pi_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\hom(X(d,c),Y(e,c))\rightrightarrows\Pi_{c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\hom(X(d,c)\times\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(c,c^{\prime}),Y(e,c^{\prime}))

(6.41)

where the first map assigns to an object
\Pi_{c\in\mathcal{C}}f_{c}\in\Pi_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\hom(X(d,c),Y(e,c))
the object which on the pair
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
is defined to be

\textstyle{X(d,c)\times\hom(c,c^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{c}\times\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{Y(e,c)\times\hom(c,c^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{c}^{Y}}
\textstyle{Y(e,c^{\prime})}

(6.42)

where in the last step we have used the map
\operatorname{ev}_{c}^{Y}
corresponding under the standard hom-tensor adjunction in
\operatorname{Set}
to the map

\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(c,c^{\prime})\to\hom(Y(e,c)\to Y(e,c^{\prime})),f\mapsto Y(\operatorname{id}_{e},f)
. Similarly, the second map is defined to be the composition

\textstyle{X(d,c)\times\hom(c,c^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{c}^{X}}
\textstyle{X(d,c^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{c^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{Y(e,c^{\prime}).}

(6.43)

We will not give all details of this construction and refer to
[
Bén73
]
instead.

Remark 6.7.10
.

We have an equivalence of closed monoidal categories

(\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Prof}}(\star,\star),\circ)\cong(\operatorname{Set},\times)

(6.44)

where
\operatorname{Set}
is the category of sets equiped with the cartesian product. Indeed, by definition a profunctor

\star\to\star
is a functor
\star^{\operatorname{op}}\times\star\cong\star\to\operatorname{Set}
, hence can be understood as a specific set;
similarly, a natural transformation between such profunctors amounts to a map between sets. It is also clear that the definition
of the horizontal composition in
\operatorname{Prof}
reduces to the cartesian product of sets.

Remark 6.7.11
.

Since
\operatorname{Prof}
is symmetric monoidal, right 1-closed, and has duals, it is also left closed. Let
X\colon\mathcal{D}\nrightarrow\mathcal{C}
and
Y\colon\mathcal{E}\nrightarrow\mathcal{C}

be 1-morphisms in
\operatorname{Prof}
. We wish to compute the 1-morphism
{}^{X}\!Y:=d^{-1}(d(Y)^{d(X)})
in the following, where
d=(-)^{\operatorname{op}}
denotes the dualization
functor on
\operatorname{Prof}
. By definition,
d(X)\colon\mathcal{C}^{1\operatorname{op}}\nrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{1\operatorname{op}}
is given by
d(X)(d,c)=X(c,d)
and similarly for
d(Y)
. Next, we compute

d(Y)^{d(X)}\colon\mathcal{D}^{1\operatorname{op}}\nrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{1\operatorname{op}}
on objects to be the equalizer of

\Pi_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\hom(X(c,d),Y(c,e))\rightrightarrows\Pi_{c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\hom(X(c,d)\times\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(c,c^{\prime}),Y(c^{\prime},e)).

(6.45)

Finally,
d^{-1}(d(Y)^{d(X)})\colon\mathcal{E}\nrightarrow\mathcal{D}
is given on
(d,e)\in\mathcal{D}^{1\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{E}
again as this equalizer.
In this generality, this is all that we can say here.

We now consider a more special situation, in which we want to compare the left and right closed assignments more explicitly: given a 1-morphism

Z\colon\mathcal{C}\nrightarrow\mathcal{D}
in
\operatorname{Prof}
, we may consider the 1-morphism

\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}^{Z}\colon\mathcal{D}\nrightarrow\mathcal{C}
which is given on an object
(\hat{c},d)\in\mathcal{C}^{1\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{D}
as the equalizer of

\Pi_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\hom(Z(d,c),\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\hat{c},c))\rightrightarrows\Pi_{c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\hom(Z(d,c)\times\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(c,c^{\prime}),\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\hat{c},c^{\prime})).

(6.46)

On the other hand, we evaluate
{}^{Z}\!\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathcal{D}\nrightarrow\mathcal{C}
on an object
(c,\hat{d})\in\mathcal{C}^{1\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{D}
to be the equalizer of

\Pi_{d\in\mathcal{D}}\hom(Z(d,c),\hom_{\mathcal{D}}(d,\hat{d}))\rightrightarrows\Pi_{d,d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}}\hom(Z(d,c)\times\hom_{\mathcal{D}}(d,d^{\prime}),\hom_{\mathcal{D}}(d^{\prime},\hat{d})).

(6.47)

We note that by the closedness of the monoidal category
\operatorname{Set}
, we have

\displaystyle\hom(Z(d,c)\times\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(c,c^{\prime}),\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\hat{c},c^{\prime}))\cong

\displaystyle\hom(Z(d,c),\hom_{\operatorname{Set}}(\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(c,c^{\prime}),\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\hat{c},c^{\prime}))).

and similar for the left handed version.

Remark 6.7.12
.

In this remark, we wish to study (lax) hermitian fixed points in the context of the fully closed symmetric monoidal 2-category
\operatorname{Prof}
.
First, since
\operatorname{Prof}
is symmetric monoidal with duals, it carries an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure, so that we may consider the associated

\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category
\mathcal{B}:=S_{\operatorname{O(1)}}\operatorname{Prof}
without steps in between. The objects of this
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category are triples

(\mathcal{C},\theta_{\mathcal{C}},\omega_{\mathcal{C}})
consisting of a category
\mathcal{C}
, an invertible profunctor
\theta_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
, and an invertible
transformation
\omega_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\to\theta_{\mathcal{C}}^{\operatorname{op}}\circ\theta_{\mathcal{C}}
satisfying a coherence condition. Note that
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive
categories are special objects in
\mathcal{B}
: they are precisely those objects, for which
\theta_{\mathcal{C}}
is an honest functor
5
5

5

Under certain additional
assumptions, invertible profunctors and invertible functors are the same, such as if
\mathcal{C}
is Cauchy complete.
.

According to our general theory,
\mathcal{B}
is enriched in lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories, so that we may consider the categories of lax hermitian fixed
points and isometries locally, i.e. consider
\operatorname{LaxHerm}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(a,b))
. The objects of this category are lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive profunctors, and the
morphisms are lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive transformations between such.

Remark 6.7.13
.

Via the embedding
\operatorname{Cat}\to\operatorname{Prof}
and its compatibility with passing to opposites explained in
Remark

6.7.7
,
we could develop the theory of lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive categories and their (higher) morphisms purely from the perspective of the fully closed symmetric
monoidal 2-category
\operatorname{Prof}
. From this duality based point of view,
\operatorname{Prof}
hence carries an advantage over
\operatorname{Cat}
, the latter of which neither
being 1-closed nor having duals for objects.

7
Examples and Applications

In this appendix, we wish to give a more detailed account on specific examples of interest, for instance, (complete) bornological vector spaces and modules over
*
-rings.
We will review some of the general theory and discuss their respective (lax)
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structures. We will also consider the 2-category of
Hilbert spaces, relations, and inclusions, discuss how unbounded operators are subsumed by this theory, and whether it carries a dagger structure.
Finally, we illustrate some of the developed theory on fully closed symmetric monoidal 2-categories in the context of complete bornological algebras.

7.1
Bornological vector spaces

In this section we wish to give a more detailed account of (complete) bornological vector spaces, which we will consider again in
Section

7.4
.
Most of the material covered in this section is well-known and we do no claim originality, see e.g.
[
Mey07
]
.

Definition 7.1.1
.

A
bornology
on a set
X
is a collection
\mathfrak{B}\subset\mathcal{P}(X)
of subsets of
X
such that
(i)
\bigcup_{B\in\mathfrak{B}}B=X
(ii) if
B\in\mathfrak{B}
and
A\subseteq B
then
A\in\mathfrak{B}

(iii) if
B_{1},...,B_{n}\in\mathfrak{B}
, then
\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq n}B_{i}\in\mathfrak{B}
. A
bornological space

is a set
X
together with a bornology
\mathfrak{B}
. If
(X,\mathfrak{B}_{X}),(Y,\mathfrak{B}_{Y})
are bornological sets, a
map
f\colon X\to Y
is called
bounded
if
f(B)\in\mathfrak{B}_{Y}
for all
B\in\mathfrak{B}_{X}
.

Example 7.1.2
.

Let
X
be a semi-normed space. Then, we define a bornology on
X
by declaring a set
B\subseteq X
to be bounded if
\sup_{b\in B}\|b\|<\infty
.
We call this bornology the
von Neumann bornology
on
X
. In particular, we may think of any normed or Banach space as a bornological space.

Definition 7.1.3
.

A
(complex) bornological vector space
is a (complex) vector space internal to the category of bornological spaces.

Remark 7.1.4
.

Explicitly, a bornological vector space consists of a vector space together with a bornology so that addition and scalar multiplication are
bounded, where the underlying field (here
\mathbb{C}
) is equiped with the standard bornology. We could also consider
\mathbb{R}
as the underlying field.

Definition 7.1.5
.

A bornological vector space is called

(i)

convex
if every bounded set is contained in a bounded disk,

(ii)

separated
if every bounded set is contained in a bounded norming disk,

(iii)

complete
if every bounded set is contained in a bounded completant disk.

where we infer the definitions above from
[
AB25
, Definition 2.1.12]
and the preceding text in loc. cit.

Remark 7.1.6
.

The von Neumann bornology on a semi-normed space is convex. The von Neumann bornology on a semi-normed space is separated if and only if the space is normed.
The von Neumann bornology on a normed space is complete if and only if the space is Banach.

Definition 7.1.7
.

We denote by
\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
the category of convex bornological vector spaces and bounded linear maps, by
s\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
the full subcategory of separated convex
bornological vector spaces, and by
c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
the full subcategory of complete convex bornological vector spaces.

Remark 7.1.8
.

Every bornological vector spaces in this paper will be convex, so that we may leave this adjective implicit throughout.

Remark 7.1.9
.

The category of complete (convex) bornological vector spaces is closely related to the ind-completion of the category of Banach spaces and bounded
linear maps: it is the category of
injective/monomorphic
ind-objects in
\operatorname{Ban}
. We refer to
[
PS00
, Proposition 5.15]

for a precise account and proof of this statement.

We recall the following results from
[
AB25
, Section 2.3]
and
[
Mey07
]
.

Lemma 7.1.10
.

The inclusion functors
I\colon s\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}\to\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
and
J\colon c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}\to s\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
admit left adjoints

\textstyle{c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J}
\textstyle{s\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Comp}}
\scriptstyle{I}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Sep}}

(7.1)

Moreover,
\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn},s\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
, and
c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
have all (small) limits and colimits, see
[
Mey07
, Proposition 1.126]
.
We summarize
Remark

7.1.6
by noting the diagram of fully faithful functors, see
[
Mey07
, Section 1.1.5]
,

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.10423pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.23022pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.1952pt\raise 5.27972pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\subset}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.23022pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.23022pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern (7.2)

where
\operatorname{Ban},\operatorname{Nor}
, and
\operatorname{sNor}
denote the categories of Banach spaces, normed spaces, and semi-normed spaces, respectively,
all with bounded linear maps as morphisms.

We also infer the following results from
[
Mey07
, Proposition 1.111]
or
[
AB25
, Sections 2.4-2.6]
.

Lemma 7.1.11
.

c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
is a closed symmetric monoidal category with respect to the completed tensor product of complete convex bornological vector spaces.

Remark 7.1.12
.

The tensor product of convex bornological vector spaces is defined by the following universal property. Let
V
and
W
be convex bornological
vector spaces. Their tensor product, if it exists, is a convex bornological vector space
V\otimes^{b}W
together with a bounded bilinear map

\tau\colon V\times W\to V\otimes^{b}W
such that for all
U\in\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
and all bounded bilinear maps
\varphi\colon V\times W\to U

there is a unique linear map
f\colon V\otimes^{b}W\to U
such that the diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 18.55551pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-18.55551pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{V\times W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.71158pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tau}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.55551pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.25975pt\raise-15.58084pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 23.8344pt\raise-23.55428pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\exists!f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.95901pt\raise-28.55304pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
