0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{U}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(7.3)

is commutative. Moreover, the tensor product of bornological spaces exists; it is given by the algebraic tensor product of the underlying
vector spaces together with the bornology described in
[
AB25
, Proposition 2.4.2]
.

The tensor product of complete bornological vector spaces is given by the completion of the tensor product of convex bornological vector spaces,

V\hat{\otimes}W:=\operatorname{Comp}(V\otimes^{b}W)
.

Remark 7.1.13
.

The tensor product of complete convex bornological vector spaces recovers the projective tensor product
\otimes_{\pi}
of Banach spaces (and more generally
Frechet spaces, see
[
Mey07
, Definition 1.15 & Theorem 1.87]
) in the sense that
\operatorname{vN}(V\otimes_{\pi}W)\cong\operatorname{vN}(V)\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{vN}(W)
holds
for all Banach spaces
V
and
W
.

Corollary 7.1.14
.

The category
c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
of complete convex bornological vector spaces and bounded linear maps admits a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.

Proof.

Combine
Lemma

7.1.11
and
Theorem

3.2.11
.
∎

Remark 7.1.15
.

We wish to describe the lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure constructed in
Corollary

7.1.14
explicitly.
First, let
V
and
W
be complete convex bornological vector spaces. Them, the exponential object
W^{V}
is the vector space
\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}(V,W)

together with the complete convex bornology described in
[
AB25
, Section 2.6 & Proposition 2.5.1]
. In particular,
the functor of our lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure assigns each complete convex bornological vector space
V
to its dual space
V^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}(V,\mathbb{C})
.
The natural transformation of our lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure has as its component at
V
the canonical map

\iota_{V}\colon V\to\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}(\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}(V,\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{C})
.

Definition 7.1.16
.

A complete convex bornological vector space
V
is called
reflexive
if
\iota_{V}
is an isomorphism.

Corollary 7.1.17
.

The category of reflexive complete convex bornological vector spaces
c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}^{\operatorname{refl}}
admits an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.

Proof.

Combine
Corollary

7.1.14
and
3.2.7
.
∎

Lemma 7.1.18
.

The functor
\operatorname{vN}\colon\operatorname{Ban}\to c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
is lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive. Moreover, it descends to an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive functor

\operatorname{vN}\colon\operatorname{Ban}^{\operatorname{refl}}\to c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}^{\operatorname{refl}}
.

Proof.

Let
V
be a Banach space. We claim that
\operatorname{vN}(V^{*})\cong\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}(\operatorname{vN}(V),\mathbb{C})
as bornological vector spaces. By definition, both sides
have the same underlying vector spaces, so that it suffices to show that their bounded sets coincide. First, recall that
V^{*}
is a Banach space
equiped with the operator norm. In particular, a set
A\subset V^{*}
is bounded if and only if

\sup_{f\in A}\|f\|=\sup_{f\in A}\sup_{\|x\|\leq 1}|f(x)|<\infty
. In other words,
A
is bounded if and only if the image of the unit
ball
A(B_{V})
is bounded in
\mathbb{C}
. In particular, any bounded set in
\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}(V,\mathbb{C})
is bounded in
V^{*}
. Conversely, any bounded set
A

in
V^{*}
is bounded in
\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}(V,\mathbb{C})
since
|f(x)|\leq\|f\|\cdot\|x\|\leq(\sup_{g\in A}\|g\|)\cdot\|x\|<\infty
for all

f\in A,x\in V
. This proves our claim. Similarly, the respective double dual spaces coincide, and the canonical morphisms into the double dual
spaces coincide. Noting that the lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on
\operatorname{vN}
is already a natural isomorphism, the second claim follows immediately.
∎

7.2
Modules over star-rings

In this section we wish to give a more detailed account of modules over
*
-rings. We start with a discussion of dualities for modules over ordinary (non-commutative) rings.

Construction 7.2.1
.

Let
R
be a (possibly non-commutative) ring and let
M
and
N
be right
R
-modules. Then the set
\hom_{R}(M,N)
of intertwiners generically does not
inherit a right
R
-module structure. Assume now in addition that
S
is a second (possibly non-commutative) ring and
N
is an
S
-
R
-bimodule.
Then
\hom_{R}(M,N)
carries the structure of a left
S
-module: we take the pointwise addition together with the
S
-action

(s\triangleright\phi)(m)=s\phi(m).

(7.4)

In particular, since every ring
R
is canonically an
R
-
R
-bimodule over itself,
\hom_{R}(M,R)
carries the structure of a left
R
-module.
We will refer to
M^{\vee_{r}}:=\hom_{R}(M,R)
as the
dual
left
R
-module to
M
.

Variant 7.2.2
.

Similarly, we may start with a left
R
-module
M
in which case the dual
M^{\vee_{\ell}}
will be a right
R
-module.

Construction 7.2.3
.

Let
R
and
S
be (possibly non-commutative) rings, let
M_{1},M_{2}
be right
R
-modules, and let
N_{1},N_{2}
be
S
-
R
-bimodules. Furthermore, let

f\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
be an intertwiner of right
R
-modules and let
g\colon N_{2}\to N_{1}
an intertwiner of
S
-
R
-bimodules. Then, we obtain
an intertwiner of left
S
-modules
\hom_{R}(M_{2},N_{2})\to\hom_{R}(M_{1},N_{1}),\phi\mapsto g\circ\phi\circ f
. This construction is clearly compatible
with respect to compositions of intertwiners
f,f^{\prime}
and
g,g^{\prime}
.

In particular, for any intertwiner of right
R
-modules
f\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
we obtain an intertwiner of left
R
-modules
M_{2}^{\vee_{r}}\to M_{1}^{\vee_{r}}
.
The assignment
M\mapsto M^{\vee}_{r}
hence defines a functor
(-)^{\vee_{r}}\colon\operatorname{Mod}_{R}\to{}_{R}\!\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{op}}
.

Variant 7.2.4
.

Similarly, we obtain a functor
(-)^{\vee_{\ell}}\colon{}_{R}\!\operatorname{Mod}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\operatorname{op}}
.

Construction 7.2.5
.

Let
R
be a (possibly non-commutative) ring. For each right
R
-module
M
, we obtain an intertwiner of right
R
-modules
\iota_{M}\colon M\to M^{\vee_{r}\vee_{\ell}},m\mapsto\operatorname{ev}_{m}
where
\operatorname{ev}_{m}(\phi)=\phi(m)
is the evaluation at
m
. The assignment
M\mapsto\iota_{M}
defines a natural transformation

\iota^{r}\colon\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{Mod}_{R}}\to(-)^{\vee_{r}\vee_{\ell}}
.

The natural transformation
\iota^{r}
satisfies a coherence condition over the triple dual, namely
\iota_{M}^{\vee}\circ\iota_{M^{\vee}}=\operatorname{id}_{M^{\vee}}
.
The proof that
\iota^{r}
defines a natural transformation and is coherent in the sense of the previous sentence is completely analogous to the one
given in
Example

3.2.5
.

Variant 7.2.6
.

Similarly, we obtain a natural transformation
\iota^{\ell}\colon\operatorname{id}_{{}_{R}\!\operatorname{Mod}}\to(-)^{\vee_{\ell}\vee_{r}}
which satisfies the evident coherence condition.

Lemma 7.2.7
.

The assignment
a\mapsto\operatorname{Mod}_{R},b\mapsto{}_{R}\!\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{op}},X\mapsto(-)^{\vee_{r}},Y\mapsto((-)^{\vee_{\ell}})^{\operatorname{op}},f\mapsto\iota^{r},g\mapsto(\iota^{\ell})^{\operatorname{op}}

defines a 2-functor
\operatorname{Adj}\to\operatorname{Cat}
.

Without further assumption,
Lemma

7.2.7
is the best statement we can make.
We now consider more favorable conditions, starting with an observation/definition.

Remark 7.2.8
.

The category of rings
\operatorname{Ring}
carries an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-action which assigns each ring
R
to its opposite
R^{\operatorname{op}}
. The category of
\star
-rings
is precisely the category of
\operatorname{O(1)}
-homotopy fixed points with respect to this action. Spelled out, a
\star
-structure on a ring is an isomorphism

\star\colon R\to R^{\operatorname{op}}
satisfying
\star^{2}=\operatorname{id}_{R}
.

Example 7.2.9
.

Any commutative ring
R
is a
*
-ring with respect to the identity
\operatorname{id}\colon R\to R
.

Example 7.2.10
.

The quaternions
\mathbb{H}
together with the quaternionic conjugation form a
\star
-ring.

Proposition 7.2.11
.

Let
R
be a (possibly non-commutative)
*
-ring. Then,
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}
admits a lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.

Proof.

We have already constructed in
Lemma

7.2.7
a 2-functor
\operatorname{Adj}\to\operatorname{Cat}

whose value at
a\in\operatorname{Adj}
is
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}
. It remains to describe a 2-contravariant
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on this functor. The construction of such
a structure from the
*
-structure is straightforward.
∎

Corollary 7.2.12
.

Let
R
be a (possibly non-comuutative)
*
-ring. Then, the category of reflexive right
R
-modules
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\operatorname{refl}}
admits an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure.

Proof.

Combine
Proposition

7.2.11
and
3.2.7
.
∎

Remark 7.2.13
.

A more sophisticated version of the discussion here (involving Banach
*
-modules) allows us to reconstruct the dagger structure on the category
of quaternionic hermitian(/Hilbert) spaces from the lax
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure on the category of Banach modules over the
*
-ring
\mathbb{H}
.
The discussion here is sufficient only for the finite-dimensional case.

7.3
Unbounded operators

In this section we discuss unbounded operators and their higher category. We will start by reviewing some standard material in the theory of
unbounded operators, which may be found e.g. in
[
Sch12
]
.

Definition 7.3.1
.

Let
B
and
B^{\prime}
be Banach spaces. An
unbounded operator

B\to B^{\prime}
is a linear map
f\colon D(f)\to B^{\prime}
where
D(f)
is a linear subspace of
B
,
called the
domain
of
f
.

Example 7.3.2
.

Let
B
and
B^{\prime}
be Banach spaces. Any bounded operator
f\colon B\to B^{\prime}
is in particular an unbounded operator with domain
D(f)=B
.

A good class of examples of unbounded operators is given by differential operators. The following is one such example.

Example 7.3.3
.

Consider the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
of square integrable functions and the differentiation operator
\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}
with domain
the (dense) subspace of Schwartz functions
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\subseteq L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Remark 7.3.4
.

Unbounded operators can be (unitaly and associatively) added and composed, with domains
D(f+g):=D(f)\cap D(g)
for
f,g\colon B\to B^{\prime}
and

D(f\circ g):=\{x\in D(g)|g(x)\in D(f)\}
for
g\colon B\to B^{\prime}
and
f\colon B^{\prime}\to B^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. However, in general we have

f(g+h)\neq fg+fh

(7.5)

for
g,h\colon B\to B^{\prime}
and
f\colon B^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
. A counterexample
6
6

6

which can be found in lecture notes
[
Rem21
]
.

is given by the unbounded operators
f=d/dx,g=d/dx,h=-d/dx
on the Banach space
C^{0}([0,1])
; indeed, we have
D(f(g+h))=C^{1}([0,1])
while

D(fg+fh)=C^{2}([0,1])
.

Definition 7.3.5
.

Let
f\colon B\to B^{\prime}
be an unbounded operator.

•

The
graph
of
f
is the linear subspace
\Gamma(f):=\{(x,f(x))|x\in\mathcal{D}(f)\}\subseteq B\oplus B^{\prime}
.

•

An unbounded operator
\hat{f}\colon B\to B^{\prime}
is called an
extension
of
f
if
\Gamma(f)\subseteq\Gamma(\hat{f})
; equivalently,

D(f)\subseteq D(\hat{f})
and
\hat{f}x=fx
for all
x\in D(f)
.

•

f
is said to be
closed
if
\Gamma(f)\subseteq B\oplus B^{\prime}
is a closed subset.

•

f
is said to be
closeable
if it admits a closed extension.

•

f
is said to be
densely defined
if
D(f)
is dense in
\mathcal{B}
.

Remark 7.3.6
.

Any bounded densely defined operator
f\colon D(f)\subseteq B\to B^{\prime}
extends to all of
B
, by continuity.

Remark 7.3.7
.

By the closed graph theorem, if an unbounded operator
f\colon B\to B^{\prime}
is closed and has
D(f)=B
, then
f
is bounded.

Notation 7.3.8
.

We denote by
\overline{T}
the (unique)
closure
of a closable unbounded operator
T
, that is, the smallest closed
extension of
T
.

We now turn our attention to Hilbert spaces, following
[
Sch12
, Section 1.2]
.

Construction 7.3.9
.

Let
H
and
H^{\prime}
be Hilbert spaces. Let
T\colon H\to H^{\prime}
be an unbounded operator. We define

D(T^{\dagger}):=\{y\in H^{\prime}\ |\ \exists z\in H:\langle Tx,y\rangle=\langle x,z\rangle\forall x\in D(T)\}

(7.6)

which is clearly a linear subspace of
H^{\prime}
. In order for the element
z
to be unique, we assume in addition that
T
is densely defined;
in this case, we have
\langle x,z-z^{\prime}\rangle=0
for all
x\in D(T)
in a dense subset of
H
, implying that
z=z^{\prime}
. We may then define

T^{\dagger}y=z
, obtaining an unbounded operator
T^{\dagger}\colon H^{\prime}\to H
.

Notation 7.3.10
.

Let
T\colon H\to H^{\prime}
be a densely defined unbounded operator. The unbounded operator
T^{\dagger}\colon H^{\prime}\to H
described in

7.3.9
is called the
adjoint
of
T
. By construction, we have

\langle Tx,y\rangle=\langle x,T^{\dagger}y\rangle\ \forall x\in D(T),\ y\in D(T^{\dagger}).

(7.7)

Warning 7.3.11
.

Many of the properties that the adjoint of a bounded operator satisfies do not carry over to this more general setting.
We will collect desired results under favorable additional assumptions in the following.

Proposition 7.3.12

(
[
Sch12
, Propositions 1.6 & 1.7 and Theorem 1.8]
)
.

Let
T\colon H\to H^{\prime}
be a densely defined unbounded operator. Then:

(i)

T^{\dagger}\colon H^{\prime}\to H
is a closed unbounded operator,

(ii)

T
is closeable if and only if
D(T^{\dagger})
is dense in
H^{\prime}
,

(iii)

if
T
is closable, then
\overline{T}^{\dagger}=T^{\dagger}
and we have
\overline{T}=(T^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=:T^{\dagger\dagger}
, and

(iv)

T
is closed if and only if
T=T^{\dagger\dagger}
.

Furthermore, let
S\colon H^{\prime}\to H^{\prime\prime}
be another densely defined unbounded operator so that
ST
is densely defined. Then

(v)

\Gamma(T^{\dagger}S^{\dagger})\subset\Gamma((ST)^{\dagger})
, and

(vi)

if
S
is bounded (and hence extends to a bounded operator
\hat{S}
defined on all of
H^{\prime}
),
then
T^{\dagger}\hat{S}^{\dagger}=(\hat{S}T)^{\dagger}
.

Remark 7.3.13
.

Proposition

7.3.12
suggests that the differences of the theory of ordinary dagger categories and unbounded operators are
too great to be ignored. If one were to attempt to formalize the latter in the setting of the former, one would first of all have to consider
closed and densely defined operators. However, the composition of closed and densely defined operators is not necessarily closed and densely defined,
so that it is not clear which category to consider; second, while the supposed dagger structure would be identity-on-object and strictly involutive,
it need not be a functor without further assumptions. However, we do note that there is a category of Hilbert spaces and unbounded operators.

In the following, we pursue a relational approach towards organizing unbounded operators and their adjoints, partially following lecture notes

[
Rem21
]
(which treat the case of unbounded operators on a single Hilbert space). The theory is motivated by the following
observation.

Remark 7.3.14
.

Let
T\colon H\to H^{\prime}
be an unbounded operator. Then,
T
and its domain can be covered from its graph
\Gamma(T)
. Indeed, we obtain the domain as

D(T):=\{x\in H\ |\ (x,y)\in\Gamma(T)\ \text{for some}\ y\in H^{\prime}\}

(7.8)

and the operator
T
by setting
Tx=y
for the unique
y\in H^{\prime}
with
(x,y)\in\Gamma(T)
.

Definition 7.3.15
.

Let
H
and
H^{\prime}
be Hilbert spaces. A
relation
from
H
to
H^{\prime}
is a linear subspace of
H\oplus H^{\prime}
.
If
H^{\prime}=H
, we will call a relation from
H
to
H^{\prime}
a
relation on
H
.

Example 7.3.16
.

Let
T\colon H\to H^{\prime}
be an unbounded operator. Then
\Gamma(T)
is a relation from
H
to
H^{\prime}
.

Definition 7.3.17
.

Let
V
be a relation from
H
to
H^{\prime}
and
W
be a relation from
H^{\prime}
to
H^{\prime\prime}
. We define the following notions:

•

the
diagonal
relation on
H
is the linear subspace
\Delta_{H}:=\{(x,x)\ |\ x\in H\}
,

•

the
reverse
7
7

7

In the literature, the reverse is also called the
inverse
. We do not use this terminology since it is not to
be understood as an inverse with respect to the composition of relations we describe, with unit given by the diagonal relation.
of

V
is the relation from
H^{\prime}
to
H
given by

V^{\operatorname{rev}}:=\{(y,x)\in H^{\prime}\oplus H\ |\ (x,y)\in V\subseteq H\oplus H^{\prime}\},

(7.9)

•

the
closure

\overline{V}
of
V
is the closure of the linear subspace
V\subseteq H\oplus H^{\prime}
.
We call a relation
closed
if
\overline{V}=V
,

•

the
adjoint
of
V
is the relation from
H^{\prime}
to
H
given by

V^{\dagger}:=\{(u,v)\in H^{\prime}\oplus H\ |\ \langle v,x\rangle_{H}=\langle u,y\rangle_{H^{\prime}}\ \text{for all}\ (x,y)\in V\},

(7.10)

to see that this is a linear subspace, let
(u,v),(s,t)\in V^{\dagger}
. Then

\langle v+t,x\rangle_{H}=\langle v,x\rangle_{H}+\langle t,x\rangle_{H}=\langle u,y\rangle_{H^{\prime}}+\langle s,y\rangle_{H^{\prime}}=\langle u+s,y\rangle

for all
(x,y)\in V
, hence
(u+s,v+t)\in V^{\dagger}
, and

•

the
composition
of
V
and
W
is the relation from
H
to
H^{\prime\prime}
given by

W\circ V:=\{(x,z)\in H\oplus H^{\prime\prime}\ |\ \exists y\in H^{\prime}:(x,y)\in V\land(y,z)\in W\},

(7.11)

to see that this is a linear subspace, let
(x,z),(x^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in W\circ V
. Then there are
y,y^{\prime}
such that
(x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in V
and
(y,z),(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in W
,
hence
(x+x^{\prime},y+y^{\prime})\in V
and
(y+y^{\prime},z+z^{\prime})\in W
, hence
(x+x^{\prime},z+z^{\prime})\in W\circ V
and similarly
(\lambda x,\lambda z)\in W\circ V
for any complex
scalar
\lambda
.

Lemma 7.3.18
.

Let
T\colon H\to H^{\prime}
and
S\colon H^{\prime}\to H^{\prime\prime}
be unbounded operators. Then
\Gamma(ST)=\Gamma(S)\circ\Gamma(T)
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Gamma(ST)

\displaystyle=\{(x,STx)\ |\ x\in D(ST)\}

\displaystyle=\{(x,STx)\ |\ x\in D(T)\land T(x)\in D(S)\}

\displaystyle=\{(x,STx)\ |\ (x,Tx)\in\Gamma(T)\land(Tx,STx)\in\Gamma(S)\}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(S)\circ\Gamma(T)

where in the last equation we have used the evident inclusion together with the reverse inclusion which is obtain by applying twice the observation
that if
(x,y)\in\Gamma(T)
for fixed
x
, then
y
is unique.
∎

Notation 7.3.19
.

We denote by
\operatorname{Hilb}^{\operatorname{unb}}
the category of Hilbert spaces and unbounded operators, whose composition is described in

Remark

7.3.4
. We denote by
\operatorname{HilbRel}
the category of Hilbert spaces and relations, whose composition is
described in
Definition

7.3.17
.

Proposition 7.3.20
.

The assignment
T\mapsto\Gamma(T)
extends to an identity-on-objects functor
\operatorname{Hilb}^{\operatorname{unb}}\to\operatorname{HilbRel}
.

Proof.

Compatibility with composition is the subject of
Lemma

7.3.18
. The identity morphism on
H

is assigned to the diagonal relation on
H
, which is the identity in
\operatorname{HilbRel}
.
∎

Lemma 7.3.21
.

Let
T\colon H\to H^{\prime}
be a densely defined unbounded operator. Then
\Gamma(T^{\dagger})=\Gamma(T)^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Gamma(T^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\{(z,T^{\dagger}(z))|z\in\mathcal{D}(T^{\dagger})\}

\displaystyle=\{(u,v)\in D(T^{\dagger})\oplus H\ |\ \langle v,x\rangle_{H}=\langle T^{\dagger}u,x\rangle_{H}\ \text{for all}\ x\in D(T)\}

\displaystyle=\{(u,v)\in D(T^{\dagger})\oplus H\ |\ \langle v,x\rangle_{H}=\langle u,Tx\rangle_{H^{\prime}}\ \text{for all}\ x\in D(T)\}

\displaystyle=\{(u,v)\in H^{\prime}\oplus H\ |\ \langle v,x\rangle_{H}=\langle u,Tx\rangle_{H^{\prime}}\ \text{for all}\ x\in D(T)\}

\displaystyle=\{(u,v)\in H^{\prime}\oplus H\ |\ \langle v,x\rangle_{H}=\langle u,y\rangle_{H^{\prime}}\ \text{for all}\ (x,y)\in\Gamma(T)\}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(T)^{\dagger}

where in the second equation we have used that
T
is densely defined, in the third equation we have used

Equation

7.7
, in the fourth equation we have used the evident inclusion together with the reverse inclusion
guaranteed by the definition of
D(T^{\dagger})
, and in the remaining equations we only used definitions.
∎

Summarizing, we can define the adjoint of any relation which recovers the adjoint of an unbounded densely defined operator. In the following we investigate
functorial properties of the adjoint relation operation.

Lemma 7.3.22
.

Let
V
be a relation from
H
to
H^{\prime}
and
W
be a relation from
H^{\prime}
to
H^{\prime\prime}
. Then
V^{\dagger}\circ W^{\dagger}\subseteq(W\circ V)^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

We claim that the following equations and inclusions hold.

\displaystyle V^{\dagger}\circ W^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\{(u,v)\in H^{\prime\prime}\oplus H\ |\ \exists y\in H^{\prime}:(u,y)\in W^{\dagger}\land(y,v)\in V^{\dagger}\}

\displaystyle=\{(u,v)\in H^{\prime\prime}\oplus H\ |\ \exists y\in H^{\prime}:\langle y,x\rangle_{H^{\prime}}=\langle u,z\rangle_{H^{\prime\prime}}\ \text{for all}\ (x,z)\in W

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\land\langle v,a\rangle_{H}=\langle y,b\rangle_{H^{\prime}}\ \text{for all}\ (a,b)\in V\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{(u,v)\in H^{\prime\prime}\oplus H\ |\ \langle v,s\rangle_{H}=\langle u,t\rangle_{H^{\prime\prime}}\ \text{for all}\ (s,t)\in H\oplus H^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{such that}\ \exists o\in H^{\prime}:(s,o)\in V\land(o,t)\in W\}

\displaystyle=\{(u,v)\in H^{\prime\prime}\oplus H\ |\ \langle v,s\rangle_{H}=\langle u,t\rangle_{H^{\prime\prime}}\ \text{for all}\ (s,t)\in W\circ V\}

\displaystyle=(W\circ V)^{\dagger}

The only non-trivial statement here is the inclusion, which we will prove in the following. Let
(s,t)\in H\oplus H^{\prime\prime}
such that there exists an
o\in H^{\prime}

with
(s,o)\in V
and
(o,t)\in W
. By assumption, there then exists a
y\in H^{\prime}
such that

\langle v,s\rangle_{H}=\langle y,o\rangle_{H^{\prime}}=\langle u,t\rangle_{H^{\prime\prime}},

(7.12)

proving the claim.
∎

Corollary 7.3.23
.

Let
T\colon H\to H^{\prime}
and
S\colon H^{\prime}\to H^{\prime\prime}
be densely defined unbounded operators so that
S\circ T\colon H\to H^{\prime\prime}
is densely defined. Then

\Gamma(T^{\dagger}S^{\dagger})\subseteq\Gamma((ST)^{\dagger})

(7.13)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Gamma(T^{\dagger}S^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\Gamma(T^{\dagger})\circ\Gamma(S^{\dagger})

by
Lemma

7.3.18

\displaystyle=\Gamma(T)^{\dagger}\circ\Gamma(S)^{\dagger}

by
Lemma

7.3.21

\displaystyle\subseteq(\Gamma(S)\circ\Gamma(T))^{\dagger}

by
Lemma

7.3.22

\displaystyle=\Gamma(ST)^{\dagger}

by
Lemma

7.3.18

∎

Lemma 7.3.24
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space. Then
\Delta_{H}^{\dagger}=\Delta_{H}

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Delta_{H}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\{(u,v)\in H\oplus H\ |\ \langle v,x\rangle_{H}=\langle u,y\rangle_{H}\ \text{for all}\ (x,y)\in\Delta_{H}\}

\displaystyle=\{(u,v)\in H\oplus H\ |\ \langle v,x\rangle_{H}=\langle u,x\rangle_{H}\ \text{for all}\ x\in H\}=\Delta_{H}

where in the last line we have used the non-degeneracy of the inner product.
∎

Definition 7.3.25
.

Let
H
and
H^{\prime}
be Hilbert spaces and let
V
and
W
be relations from
H
to
H^{\prime}
. The
mapping set

\hom(V,W)
is defined as follows:

\hom(V,W):=\begin{cases}\{\star\}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{if}\hskip 28.45274ptV\subseteq W\\
\emptyset\hskip 35.56593pt\text{else}\\
\end{cases}

(7.14)

Lemma 7.3.26
.

Let
H
and
H^{\prime}
be Hilbert spaces, and let
V
and
W
be relations from
H
to
H^{\prime}
. Then
V\subseteq W\Rightarrow W^{\dagger}\subseteq V^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle W^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\{(u,v)\in H^{\prime}\oplus H\ |\ \langle v,x\rangle_{H}=\langle u,y\rangle_{H^{\prime}}\ \text{for all}\ (x,y)\in W\},

\displaystyle\subseteq\{(u,v)\in H^{\prime}\oplus H\ |\ \langle v,x\rangle_{H}=\langle u,y\rangle_{H^{\prime}}\ \text{for all}\ (x,y)\in V\}=V^{\dagger},

proving the claim.
∎

Lemma 7.3.27
.

Let
H,H^{\prime}
, and
H^{\prime\prime}
be Hilbert spaces, let
V\subseteq W
be relations from
H
to
H^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}\subseteq W^{\prime}
relations from
H^{\prime}
to
H^{\prime\prime}
. Then

V^{\prime}\circ V\subseteq W^{\prime}\circ W
.

Proof.

Let
(x,z)\in V^{\prime}\circ V
. Then there exists a
y\in H^{\prime}
such that
(x,y)\in V
and
(y,z)\in V^{\prime}
. Hence
(x,y)\in W
and
(y,z)\in W^{\prime}
and
in consequence
(x,z)\in W^{\prime}\circ W
.
∎

Notation 7.3.28
.

We denote by
\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{bi}}
the 2-category of Hilbert spaces and relations, with the respective mapping sets of

Definition

7.3.25
as the sets of 2-morphisms.

Proposition 7.3.29
.

The construction
V\to V^{\dagger}
of
Definition

7.3.17
extends to an identity on objects
lax 2-functor
\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{bi}}\to(\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{bi}})^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}
.

Proof.

The assignment is well-defined by construction and
Lemma

7.3.26
. It respects composition
of 2-morphisms, since
V\subseteq W\subseteq X
implies
X^{\dagger}\subseteq W^{\dagger}\subseteq V^{\dagger}
. We have
constructed the lax 2-cell implementing comaptibility with composition of 1-morphisms in
Lemma

7.3.22
and
we have constructed the 2-cell implementing compatibility with identity 1-morphisms in
Lemma

7.3.24
.
∎

Remark 7.3.30
.

There are more intricate versions of mapping spaces between relations; we have chosen the one in
Definition

7.3.25

as a minimalistic approach to interpret
Lemma

7.3.22
as a lax compatibility of the adjoint operation with composition of relations.

Having established functorial properties of the adjoint operation, we now turn our attention to the closure of relations.

Lemma 7.3.31
.

Let
V
be a relation from
H
to
H^{\prime}
and let
W
be a relation from
H^{\prime}
to
H^{\prime\prime}
. Then,
\overline{W\circ V}\subseteq\overline{W}\circ\overline{V}
.

Proof.

Let
(x,z)\in\overline{W\circ V}
. By definition of the closure, there exists a sequence
(x_{n},z_{n})_{n}\in W\circ V
with
\lim_{n}(x_{n},z_{n})_{n}=(x,z)
.
By definition of the composition, there exists a sequence
(y_{n})_{n}\in H^{\prime}
with
(x_{n},y_{n})\in V
and
(y_{n},z_{n})\in W
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence,
we have
(x,y)=\lim_{n}(x_{n},y_{n})\in\overline{V}
and
(y,z)=\lim_{n}(y_{n},z_{n})\in\overline{W}
, using the fact that limits are computed pointwise.
Hence,
(x,z)\in\overline{W}\circ\overline{V}
.
∎

Lemma 7.3.32
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space. Then
\overline{\Delta_{H}}=\Delta_{H}
.

Proof.

Recall that a topological space
X
is Hausdorff if and only if the diagonal is closed as a subset of the product space
X\times X
.
Recalling that every Hilbert space is a metric space and in particular Hausdorff, this implies the claim.
∎

Proposition 7.3.33
.

The operation
V\mapsto\overline{V}
extends to an identity-on-objects oplax 2-functor
\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{bi}}\to\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{bi}}
.

Proof.

To see that the assignment is well-defined, we note that an inclusion of relations
V\subseteq W
implies that
\overline{V}\subseteq\overline{W}
.
The oplax functoriality is subject of
Lemma

7.3.32
and
Lemma

7.3.31
.
Compatibility with composition of 2-morphisms follows from the observation that, for any two Hilbert spaces
H
and
H
’, an inclusion of relations

V\subseteq W\subseteq X
implies that
\overline{V}\subseteq\overline{W}\subseteq\overline{X}
for any three relations
V,W,X
from
H
to
H^{\prime}

∎

Remark 7.3.34
.

We emphasize that the adjoint is a lax 2-functor, whereas the closure is an oplax 2-functor on
\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{bi}}
.

Remark 7.3.35
.

The composition of two closed relations is closed again: indeed, let
V
be a closed relation from
H
to
H^{\prime}
and let
W
be a closed relation from

H^{\prime}
to
H^{\prime\prime}
. Combining the observation
W\circ V\subseteq\overline{W\circ V}
with the inclusion

\overline{W\circ V}\subseteq\overline{W}\circ\overline{V}=W\circ V,

(7.15)

where in the first step we have used
Lemma

7.3.31
and in the second step we have used our assumption
that
V
and
W
are closed, we find that
W\circ V=\overline{W\circ V}
. Together with
Lemma

7.3.32
,
this implies that Hilbert spaces, closed relations, and inclusions form a sub 2-category
\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{cl,bi}}\subseteq\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{bi}}
.

In the following we wish to study the involutiveness of the adjoint relation construction. Our discussion is a (slight) generalization of the discussion in

[
Rem21
]
to relations between different Hilbert spaces. We refer in particular to
[
Rem21
, Theorem 11.7]
for the following result.

Lemma 7.3.36
.

Let
H
and
H^{\prime}
be Hilbert spaces and let
V
be a relation from
H
to
H^{\prime}
. Then we have

(i)

\overline{V^{\dagger}}=V^{\dagger}
,

(ii)

V^{\dagger\dagger}=\overline{V}
, and

(iii)

\overline{V}^{\dagger}=V^{\dagger}
.

Note that this implies in particular that
V^{\dagger}=\overline{V^{\dagger}}=V^{\dagger\dagger\dagger}
.

Proof.

(i) First, define a bounded operator
J\colon H\oplus H^{\prime}\to H\oplus H^{\prime},(h^{\prime},h)\mapsto(h^{\prime},-h)
. We claim that
V^{\dagger}=(JV)^{\perp}
where

JV
denotes the image of
V
under
J
and
(-)^{\perp}
denotes the orthogonal complement with respect to the inner product on
H\oplus H^{\prime}
.
Indeed, for
(u,v)\in H^{\prime}\oplus H
we have

\displaystyle(u,v)\in V^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\langle v,x\rangle_{H}=\langle u,y\rangle_{H^{\prime}}\ \forall\ (x,y)\in V

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow 0=\langle v,x\rangle_{H}+\langle u,-y\rangle_{H^{\prime}}\ \forall\ (x,y)\in V

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow 0=\langle v,x\rangle_{H}+\langle u,z\rangle_{H^{\prime}}\ \forall\ (x,z)\in JV

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(u,v)\in(JV)^{\perp}.

However, the orthogonal complelement
(JV)^{\perp}
is closed so that
V^{\dagger}
is closed as well. (ii) We first note that
J(V^{\perp})=(JV)^{\perp}

by (sesqui)linearity of the inner products. We then compute

V^{\dagger\dagger}=(JV^{\dagger})^{\perp}=J((JV)^{\perp})^{\perp}=(J^{2}V)^{\perp\perp}=V^{\perp\perp}=\overline{V}

(7.16)

where the last equation is a standard fact. (iii) We compute

\overline{V}^{\dagger}\overset{(ii)}{=}(V^{\dagger\dagger})^{\dagger}=(V^{\dagger})^{\dagger\dagger}\overset{(ii)}{=}\overline{V^{\dagger}}\overset{(i)}{=}V^{\dagger}.

(7.17)

This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 7.3.37
.

The inclusions
V\subseteq\overline{V}=V^{\dagger\dagger}
induce a lax 2-transformation
(-)^{\dagger\dagger}\to\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\operatorname{bi}}}

with identity 1-morphism components. Its 2-morphism componets are trivial on the sub 2-category
\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{cl,bi}}\subseteq\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{bi}}

of Hilbert spaces, closed relations, and inclusions between them.

Remark 7.3.38
.

We summarize the results of this section in the following.

•

There is a 2-category
\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\operatorname{bi}}
of Hilbert spaces, relations (containing graphs of unbounded operators), and inclusions.

•

There is a lax identity-on-objects 2-functor
(-)^{\dagger}\colon\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\operatorname{bi}}\to(\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\operatorname{bi}})^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}
which
assigns each relation
V
to its adjoint
V^{\dagger}
(generalizing the adjoint operator construction for a densely defined unbounded operator), and each inclusion
to the induced inclusion of adjoint relations. The (normal) lax functoriality data is described in
Lemma

7.3.32

and
Lemma

7.3.22
.

•

There is a (normal) oplax identity-on-objects 2-functor
(-)^{\dagger\dagger}\colon\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\operatorname{bi}}\to\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\operatorname{bi}}
whose (normal)
oplax functoriality data for relations
V
from
H
to
H^{\prime}
and
W
from
H^{\prime}
to
H^{\prime\prime}
is given by the inclusions

(W\circ V)^{\dagger\dagger}=\overline{W\circ V}\subseteq\overline{W}\circ\overline{V}=W^{\dagger\dagger}\circ V^{\dagger\dagger}

(7.18)

where we have used
Lemma

7.3.31
and
Lemma

7.3.36
(ii).

•

There is an identity 1-morphism component lax 2-transformation
\chi\colon(-)^{\dagger\dagger}\to\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\operatorname{bi}}}
whose 2-morphism components are
given by the inclusions
V\subseteq\overline{V}=V^{\dagger\dagger}
. Moreover, we have
V^{\dagger}=V^{\dagger\dagger\dagger}
so that

\chi
satisfies a coherence property over the triple adjoint.

•

The operation
(-)^{\dagger}
restricts to a lax 2-functor
\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{cl,bi}}\to(\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{cl,bi}})^{(1,2)\operatorname{op}}
on the sub 2-category
of closed relations
\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{cl,bi}}\subseteq\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\text{bi}}
, which satisfies
(-)^{\dagger\dagger}=\operatorname{id}
.

Remark 7.3.39
.

A particularly interesting aspect of the theory we have developed here is that the lax 2-functor
(-)^{\dagger}
gives rise to an oplax 2-functor

(-)^{\dagger\dagger}
. A priori, the (1,2)-opposite of
(-)^{\dagger}
would be an oplax 2-functor, so that it is not clear whether the composition
of
(-)^{\dagger}
and its (1,2)-opposite would be any kind of 2-functor at all, whether pseudo, lax, or oplax. This is the primary reason why we
do not state our results as the existence of a kind of lax higher dagger structure on
\operatorname{HilbRel}^{\operatorname{bi}}
, despite the obvious resemblence.
The same objection still holds assuming closed relations only.

7.4
Bornological algebras

In this subsection, we focus on the closed symmetric monoidal category with (co)equalizers
c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
of complete (convex) bornological vector spaces.
According to
Theorem

6.6.4
,
\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
is a symmetric monoidal 2-category which is 1-closed and has duals
for objects. In the following, we wish to study the procedures discussed in
Section

6.6
in the present context.

Let
A
be an object in
\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
, that is, a complete bornological algebra. Recall that the identity 1-morphism
A\to A
in
\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
is given
by the Bornological
A
-
A
-bimodule
A
. We compute

A^{A}_{A}=\operatorname{eq}(A^{A}\rightrightarrows A^{A\otimes A})=\hom_{A}(A,A)\cong A

(7.19)

where in the last step we have used that an intertwiner of
A
-modules
A\to A
is completely determined by its value on
1\in A
. Next, let us consider
three objects
A,B,C
and two 1-morphisms
X\colon A\to B
and
Y\colon B\to C
in
\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
. We compute
B^{X}_{B}=\hom_{B}(X,B)
and
C^{Y}_{C}=\hom_{C}(Y,C)
.
On the other hand, we have
C^{Y\otimes_{B}X}_{C}=\hom_{C}(Y\otimes_{B}X,C)
. By closedness of
\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
we have an intertwiner of
A
-
C
-bimodules

\displaystyle\hom_{B}(X,B)\otimes_{B}\hom_{C}(Y,C)

\displaystyle\to\hom_{C}(Y\otimes_{B}X,C)

\displaystyle\phi\otimes\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 19.99008pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-19.99008pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y\otimes_{B}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.16139pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\otimes_{B}\phi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.99008pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 43.99008pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y\otimes_{B}B\cong Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 110.42499pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 128.07083pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 128.07083pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right)

which however does not need to be an isomorphism in general. This finishes our discussion of the structure 2-morphisms of our lax
2-functor
\mathcal{J}
on
\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
: the ones implementing unitality are isomorphisms while the ones expressing compatibility with composition are not (unless
one imposes strong finiteness conditions on the modules at hand).

Recalling that
\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
is symmetric monoidal and has duals for objects as well as a symmetric monoidal involution given by complex conjugation, it carries
in particular an
\operatorname{O(1)}
-volutive structure, whose underlying functor assigns each bornological algebra
A
to its complex conjugate opposite
\overline{A}^{\operatorname{op}}
, each
bornological
A
-
B
-bimodule to its associated bornological
\overline{B}^{\operatorname{op}}
-
\overline{A}^{\operatorname{op}}
-bimodule,
and similar for 2-morphisms. Passing to the
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category
S_{\operatorname{O(1)}}\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
amounts to considering bornological
*
-algebras
8
8

8

Strictly
speaking, the
\star
-structures are implemented via invertible bimodules rather than algebra isomorphisms. The latter are however a special case of the former.
,
as well as the same 1- and 2-morphisms as before. In the following we will denote the dagger structure by
(d,\eta)
. Let
M\colon A\to B
be a 1-morphism in

S_{\operatorname{O(1)}}\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
.
Then, we have the
A
-
B
-bimodule
J(M)=B^{M}_{B}
and moreover the
B
-
A
-bimodule
dJ(M)=\overline{B^{M}_{B}}=\overline{\hom_{B}(M,B)}
where the bimodule actions of the latter are
defined using the
*
-structures of
A
and
B
.

We are now in position to discuss (lax) hermitian fixed points in this context. In order to make the discussion more digestible, one may employ the
formulation of (lax) hermitian fixed points in terms of pairings. Let
M\colon A\to B
be a 1-morphism together with a lax hermitian fixed point structure

\theta_{M}\colon M\to\overline{\hom_{B}(M,B)}
. We may interpret this data as a potentially degenerate
B
-valued hermitian pairing on
M
. Assume now we are given another
1-morphism
N\colon B\to C
together with a lax hermitian fixed point structure. Then their composition as defined in

6.5.9
is
N\otimes_{B}M
equipped with the pairing given by

\langle m\otimes n,m^{\prime}\otimes n^{\prime}\rangle=\langle n,n^{\prime}\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle\rangle

(7.20)

where we have used the pairings corresponding to
\theta_{M}
and
\theta_{N}
on the right hand side, as well
as the right
B
-action on
N
.

We have emphasized in our general discussion that the pairing of
Equation

7.20
does not need to be non-degenerate
at this point, that is, the corresponding lax hermitian fixed point structure
\theta_{N\otimes_{B}M}
does not need
to be an isomorphism, even if
\theta_{M}
and
\theta_{N}
are. In the present case, however, there is a procedure one may perform to dispose of potential
degeneracies. Namely, one may quotient out the isotropic vectors, that is, those satisfying
\langle v,v\rangle=0
. After suitable completion,
one obtains an honest hermitian fixed point. With this new composition, we obtain a sub 2-category
whose 1-morphisms are honest hermitian fixed points, whose Hom-categories carry dagger structures in consequence, and which
in total carries a fully dagger structure. We stress again that these are facts specific to the present context, and do not generalize well
to an arbitrary fully closed symmetric monoidal 2-category.

In the remainder of this section, we wish to draw comparisons to von Neumann algebras and Hilbert/hermitian bimodules. Recall first that any von Neumann
algebra is in particular a Banach
\star
-algebra, hence also a complete bornological
\star
-algebra, that is, a special object in the
\operatorname{O(1)}
-dagger 2-category

S_{\operatorname{O(1)}}\operatorname{Mor}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
. Here we recall that the functor
\operatorname{Ban}\to c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
is symmetric monoidal, hence induces a functor
\operatorname{Alg}(\operatorname{Ban})\to\operatorname{Alg}(c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn})
where

\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
refers to the category of algebras and algebra homomorphisms in the monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
.

Recall further the classical result
[
Pas73
, Theorem 3.2]
that for any Hilbert module over a von Neumann algebra, the pairing induces an isomorphism
rendering it an (honest) hermitian fixed point in our sense; this is a generalization of the classical Riesz representation theorem, to which it reduces upon
considering the trivial algebra
\mathbb{C}
. Any Hilbert (bi)module over a von Neumann algebra in the sense of
[
Pas73
,
Pas74
]
therefore defines
an (honest) hermitian fixed point in our sense, where we again used the functor
\operatorname{Ban}\to c\mathfrak{B}\hskip-1.42271pt\operatorname{orn}
. The modified composition law we have explained above is precisely
the composition for such Hilbert bimodules (or rather their bornological analogues).
